Self-study course · English · 32 hours

From zero to a confident technical interview in four days

Built for the Design Engineer for Safety I&C in Nuclear role at Framatome Controls in Bratislava: the people who specify reactor protection and safety automation systems on the TELEPERM XS platform. Four days, eight focused hours each, everything in the English you will use at the interview.

What the job is

A Design Engineer for safety I&C turns plant safety requirements into a specified, sized and documented control system. In Framatome Controls' own words: elaboration of basic design documentation, collecting functional and non-functional needs, and specifying design solutions covering system architecture, sizing, implementation of safety functions, handling of interfaces, monitoring and periodic testing.

  • Degree in automation, electrical engineering or physics.
  • Understanding of DCS/PLC-based I&C systems (you will learn the concepts here).
  • Technical rigour and attention to detail: safety systems are documented, reviewed and tested line by line.
  • Technical English for documents and for an international team.
  • Willingness to work on long projects, often several years per plant.

What interviewers look for in a junior

  • Fundamentals: can you explain a 4–20 mA loop, a 2-out-of-4 vote, a PID controller, decay heat?
  • Structured thinking: do you reason aloud, from requirement to solution, naming assumptions?
  • Safety mindset: redundancy, independence, fail-safe, conservative decisions.
  • Honesty: saying "I have not used that, but here is how I understand it" beats bluffing.
  • English: clear, short sentences and the right technical words. Fluency matters less than precision.

The four-day plan

Each day is eight one-hour blocks: about 45 minutes of reading and examples, then the quiz, flashcards and interview questions. Keep the 50/10 rhythm (use the timer in the top bar) and take a real lunch break. Suggested clock times are shown; shift them to your day.

How to study so it sticks

  1. Read actively. Before each hour, read the objectives and guess the answers. After, re-read them and check.
  2. Answer out loud, in English. Every flashcard and interview question is a chance to rehearse speaking. Record yourself once a day.
  3. Draw. Sensor → channel → voting → actuator. Redraw the key diagrams from memory at the end of each day.
  4. Use the question bank on Day 4 (and at the end of Days 2 and 3) for random drills across everything you have covered.
  5. Mark hours complete. Progress is saved in this browser so you can close the page and return.

Where you are

You have not started yet. Begin with Day 1, Hour 1.

Day 1 · Hour 1

How a nuclear power plant makes electricity

This hour explains the physics inside the reactor and the path of energy from the fuel to the electrical grid. Interviewers expect even a junior I&C engineer to describe, in plain words, the plant that their systems will protect.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Explain fission, the chain reaction and why ordinary water is used as a moderator.
  • Define criticality and reactivity, and describe how control rods and boric acid change them.
  • Trace the energy path of a pressurized water reactor through its primary, secondary and tertiary circuits.
  • State typical pressures and temperatures for a western PWR, a VVER-440 and an EPR.
  • Explain decay heat and why the core must be cooled long after shutdown.

Fission: energy from splitting atoms

Nuclear fuel is uranium. Natural uranium contains only about 0.7% of the isotope uranium-235 (U-235); the rest is U-238. Fuel for a pressurized water reactor is enriched to about 3 to 5% U-235. The fuel is ceramic uranium dioxide (UO2) pellets stacked inside zirconium alloy tubes called fuel rods, which are grouped into the fuel assemblies that form the core.

When a slow neutron is absorbed by a U-235 nucleus, the nucleus splits into two smaller nuclei called fission products. One fission releases about 200 MeV (mega-electron-volts) of energy, mostly as kinetic energy of the two fragments, which stop inside the fuel and turn their energy into heat. The fission also releases two or three new neutrons (on average about 2.4) and some gamma radiation.

The chain reaction and the moderator

The new neutrons can cause further fissions. If each fission causes, on average, exactly one more fission, the number of fissions per second stays constant: this is a steady chain reaction. Neutrons are born fast (about 2 MeV) and fast neutrons rarely cause fission in U-235; slow (thermal) neutrons do so several hundred times more easily.

A material that slows neutrons down is a moderator. Ordinary water is an excellent moderator because a hydrogen nucleus has almost the same mass as a neutron, so each collision removes a large part of the neutron's energy. In a PWR the same water is both moderator and coolant. This is why a PWR cannot run without water in the core: no water means no moderation, and the chain reaction stops.

Note

About 0.7% of fission neutrons are delayed: fission products emit them seconds after the fission. They make the reactor respond in seconds rather than milliseconds, which is what allows rods, control systems and operators to keep it under control.

Controlling the reaction: criticality and reactivity

The state of the chain reaction is described by the multiplication factor k: the number of fissions in one generation divided by the number in the previous one. When k = 1 the reactor is critical and power is constant. When k < 1 it is subcritical and power falls; when k > 1 it is supercritical and power rises. To an engineer, "critical" simply means "running at steady power".

Reactivity is the distance from criticality, (k - 1) / k, often expressed in pcm (per cent mille, 1 pcm = 0.00001). Three things change it in a PWR:

  • Control rods contain strong neutron absorbers such as boron carbide or a silver-indium-cadmium alloy. On a reactor trip the drive mechanisms release them and they fall into the core by gravity within a few seconds.
  • Boric acid dissolved in the primary coolant absorbs neutrons through its boron-10. Operators adjust the concentration slowly to compensate for fuel burn-up over a fuel cycle of 12 to 18 months (chemical shim).
  • Temperature feedback: hotter water is less dense and moderates less, so reactivity falls. This negative moderator temperature coefficient makes a PWR self-stabilizing.

Thermal power is proportional to the number of fissions per second and therefore to the neutron flux. Neutron flux is the fastest indicator of reactor power and one of the most important inputs to the protection system.

From heat to electricity: the three circuits of a PWR

In a pressurized water reactor (PWR) the water in the core is kept under high pressure so that it does not boil, even at about 325 °C. The plant has three separate water circuits.

The primary circuit is a closed loop: reactor pressure vessel, hot-leg pipe, steam generator, main coolant pump and cold-leg pipe back to the vessel. The pressurizer, a vessel partly filled with water and partly with steam, holds the pressure at about 15.5 MPa using electric heaters and a water spray. Coolant enters the core at about 290 °C and leaves at about 325 °C. This water is radioactive, so the whole circuit sits inside the containment building.

The secondary circuit starts in the steam generator, a large heat exchanger. Primary water flows inside thousands of thin tubes; secondary water outside the tubes boils at about 6 to 7 MPa and 280 °C. The steam drives a turbine coupled to a generator synchronized with the 50 Hz grid. The steam is then condensed in the condenser and feedwater pumps return the water to the steam generator.

The tertiary circuit is the cooling water for the condenser: river water, sea water or water from cooling towers. It never mixes with the other circuits. Overall efficiency is about 33 to 36%, so a 3000 MW thermal reactor gives about 1000 MW of electricity and rejects about 2000 MW of heat.

Tip

Primary carries heat out of the core, secondary carries steam to the turbine, tertiary carries waste heat to the environment. Only the primary is radioactive, so a PWR turbine hall is a normal industrial area.

VVER and EPR: two PWR families you should know

The VVER (a Russian abbreviation for water-water energetic reactor) is the Russian-design PWR. Slovakia operates the VVER-440 model V-213 at Bohunice (units 3 and 4, called V2) and at Mochovce. Compared with a western PWR, a VVER-440 has six smaller loops instead of four large ones, horizontal steam generators instead of vertical ones, hexagonal fuel assemblies, and a lower primary pressure of about 12.3 MPa with coolant at about 270 to 300 °C. Each unit gives roughly 440 to 500 MW of electricity depending on upgrades. The V-213 confinement uses a pressure-suppression system called a bubbler condenser.

The EPR is the large Generation III+ PWR designed by Framatome: about 1600 MW of electricity, four loops, a double-wall containment, a core catcher for severe accidents and four independent safety trains (four separate sets of safety equipment, each able to do the whole job alone). Its safety I&C is based on the TELEPERM XS platform. Units operate in Finland, China and France, and are under construction in the United Kingdom.

ParameterWestern 4-loop PWRVVER-440 / V-213EPR
Electrical output≈900 to 1300 MWe≈440 to 500 MWe≈1600 MWe
Primary loops464
Steam generatorsVertical, U-tubeHorizontalVertical, U-tube
Primary pressure≈15.5 MPa≈12.3 MPa≈15.5 MPa
Coolant temperature≈290 to 325 °C≈270 to 300 °C≈295 to 330 °C
Safety trainsTypically 2 to 33 (×100%)4 (×100%)

Decay heat: why the cooling never stops

When the rods drop, fission stops within seconds. But the fission products are radioactive and keep decaying, and every decay releases heat. This decay heat is about 6 to 7% of full power immediately after shutdown, about 1.5% after one hour, about 1% within a few hours and about 0.5% after a day; it then decreases slowly for years. For a 3000 MW reactor that is about 200 MW at first, and it must be removed by pumps and heat exchangers.

This single fact shapes the whole safety design. Cooling systems, their power supplies and their I&C must keep working after shutdown, including during a loss of off-site power. The Fukushima Daiichi reactors in 2011 were already shut down; the cores were damaged because decay heat could not be removed.

Common mistake

Believing that "the reactor is shut down" means "the problem is over". Shutdown stops the chain reaction, not the heat. Nuclear safety has three fundamental functions: control reactivity, remove heat from the fuel, and confine radioactive material. Shutdown covers only the first.

Interview angle

A frequent opening question is "Explain to me how a nuclear power plant works." Practise a 60-second answer: fission heats the fuel, pressurized water carries the heat to steam generators, steam drives a turbine and generator, and decay heat must be removed even after shutdown. Mention that Slovakia runs VVER-440 units.

Key terms

Fission
The splitting of a heavy nucleus such as U-235 into two lighter nuclei, releasing about 200 MeV of energy and two or three neutrons.
Chain reaction
A process in which neutrons from one fission cause further fissions, so the reaction sustains itself.
Moderator
A material that slows down fast neutrons so they can cause fission more easily. In a PWR the moderator is ordinary water.
Criticality
The state in which each fission causes exactly one further fission (k = 1), so power is constant.
Reactivity
A measure of how far the reactor is from criticality, defined as (k - 1)/k. Positive means power rising, negative means power falling.
Control rod
A rod of neutron-absorbing material moved into or out of the core to change reactivity; it drops by gravity on a reactor trip.
Boric acid
A neutron absorber dissolved in the primary coolant and adjusted slowly to compensate for fuel burn-up (chemical shim).
Pressurizer
A vessel with water and steam, heaters and spray that keeps the primary circuit at its design pressure so the coolant does not boil.
Steam generator
A heat exchanger in which hot primary water inside tubes boils secondary water outside the tubes, producing steam for the turbine.
Decay heat
Heat released by radioactive fission products after the chain reaction has stopped; about 6 to 7% of full power right after shutdown.

Say it in English

  • "In a pressurized water reactor the primary water is kept at about 15.5 MPa so it stays liquid at about 325 °C."
  • "The steam generator is the boundary between the radioactive primary circuit and the clean secondary circuit."
  • "Criticality is not an emergency; it simply means the reactor is producing steady power."
  • "After a trip the chain reaction stops, but decay heat is still about 6 to 7% of full power, so we must keep cooling the core."
  • "Slovakia operates VVER-440 units with six loops and horizontal steam generators at a primary pressure of about 12.3 MPa."
  • "Neutron flux is the fastest indicator of reactor power, which is why it is a key input to the protection system."

Check yourself

Why is water used as the moderator in a PWR?

Collisions with hydrogen nuclei of similar mass remove a large share of the neutron's energy, so neutrons become thermal quickly and cause fission more easily.

What does it mean when a reactor is "critical"?

Critical means the multiplication factor k equals 1. It is the normal state of a reactor operating at constant power.

What is the approximate primary pressure of a VVER-440?

The VVER-440 runs at about 12.3 MPa, lower than the roughly 15.5 MPa of a western PWR; about 7 MPa is a typical secondary (steam) pressure.

Roughly how much heat does the core produce immediately after a reactor trip?

Decay heat is about 6 to 7% of full power right after shutdown and falls to about 1% within hours, which is still megawatts that must be removed.

Which statement about the three circuits of a PWR is correct?

In a PWR the primary water never leaves the containment; it transfers heat through the steam generator tubes to the clean secondary water that drives the turbine.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

Can you explain in simple terms how a nuclear power plant produces electricity?

Yes. In the reactor core, uranium-235 nuclei split when they absorb neutrons. Each fission releases heat and new neutrons, so the reaction sustains itself as a chain reaction. Water flowing through the core carries the heat away. In a pressurized water reactor this water is kept at about 15.5 MPa so it does not boil. It goes to steam generators, where it boils a separate secondary circuit. That steam drives a turbine coupled to a generator, which feeds the grid. The steam is condensed with cooling water and pumped back. About a third of the thermal power becomes electricity.

What is the difference between criticality and reactivity?

Criticality is a state. The reactor is critical when the multiplication factor k is exactly one, so each fission produces one further fission and the power stays constant. Reactivity is a measure of the distance from that state, defined as (k minus 1) divided by k. Zero reactivity means critical, positive means power is rising, negative means it is falling. In practice we change reactivity with control rods for fast changes and with boric acid concentration for slow changes over the fuel cycle. The negative temperature coefficient of the water also helps to stabilize the reactor.

Why do we still need to cool the reactor after it has been shut down?

Because of decay heat. When the control rods drop, the chain reaction stops within seconds, but the fission products in the fuel are radioactive and continue to decay, releasing heat. Right after shutdown this is about 6 to 7% of full power, which for a large reactor is around 200 MW. It falls to about 1% within a few hours and keeps decreasing slowly. If this heat is not removed, the fuel overheats and the cladding can fail. That is why cooling systems and their power supplies and I&C must stay available after shutdown.

What do you know about the reactors operated in Slovakia?

Slovakia operates VVER-440 reactors of the V-213 type at two sites, Bohunice and Mochovce. VVER is the Russian-design pressurized water reactor. Compared with a western PWR it has six smaller loops with horizontal steam generators, hexagonal fuel assemblies and a lower primary pressure of about 12.3 MPa, with coolant temperatures of about 270 to 300 °C. Each unit produces roughly 440 to 500 MW of electricity after upgrades. The older V1 units at Bohunice were shut down in 2006 and 2008 and are being decommissioned. The regulator is the Nuclear Regulatory Authority, ÚJD SR.

What is the role of the steam generator and why is it important for safety?

The steam generator is a large heat exchanger. Hot primary water flows through thousands of thin tubes and boils the secondary water on the outside, producing steam for the turbine. It is important for safety for two reasons. First, it is part of the barrier between the radioactive primary circuit and the clean secondary side, so a tube rupture is an accident we design for. Second, it is the main path for removing heat, including decay heat after shutdown, which is why steam generator level and the feedwater supply are monitored so carefully by the I&C.

Day 1 · Hour 2

Plant systems, the three barriers and plant states

This hour gives you the safety vocabulary of a nuclear plant: barriers, the parameters that matter, plant states, reactor trip and the engineered safety features. Interviewers use these words constantly and expect you to use them correctly.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Name the three barriers against the release of radioactivity and explain the defence-in-depth idea behind them.
  • List the main process parameters an operator and the protection system watch, with typical values.
  • Distinguish normal operation, anticipated operational occurrences, design basis accidents and design extension conditions.
  • Describe what a reactor trip does and what the engineered safety features do after it.
  • Explain a loss-of-coolant accident and name the Slovak nuclear fleet and its regulator.

Three barriers and defence in depth

The purpose of nuclear safety is to keep radioactive material away from people and the environment. The plant does this with three physical barriers, one inside the other.

  • Fuel cladding: the zirconium alloy tube around the fuel pellets, with a wall less than 1 mm thick. It keeps the fission products inside the rod. Some documents also count the ceramic pellet itself as a barrier.
  • Primary circuit boundary: the reactor pressure vessel (steel about 20 cm thick), the main pipes, the pressurizer and the steam generator tubes.
  • Containment: the building around the primary circuit, concrete with a steel liner, designed for the pressure and steam of a pipe break. The VVER-440 V-213 has a confinement whose bubbler condenser condenses the steam and limits the pressure.

The barriers are protected by defence in depth: several independent levels of provisions, so that if one fails the next still works. The International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA) describes five levels: (1) prevention by conservative design and quality, (2) control of abnormal operation by control and limitation systems, (3) control of accidents by the protection system and engineered safety features, (4) management of severe conditions, and (5) emergency planning to limit consequences. Safety I&C works mainly at level 3.

The parameters an operator watches

Everything the operator and the protection system know about the reactor comes from measured process parameters. The most important ones for a PWR are listed below, with typical values for a VVER-440 (western PWR values in brackets).

ParameterTypical value at full powerWhy it matters
Neutron flux (reactor power)100% nominal, scale 0 to 120%Fastest indicator of power
Primary pressure≈12.3 MPa (≈15.5 MPa)Too low: coolant boils; too high: boundary overstressed
Pressurizer level≈50 to 60% of rangeShows the coolant inventory; a falling level suggests a leak
Coolant temperatures≈270 °C cold leg, ≈300 °C hot leg (≈290 / 325 °C)With pressure, gives the margin to boiling
Steam generator levelControlled near a setpointLow level means the heat sink is being lost
Steam generator pressure≈4.6 MPa (≈6 to 7 MPa)Balance between reactor power and turbine load
Primary coolant flowNominal, all pumps runningLoss of flow reduces heat removal from the fuel
Containment pressureNear atmosphericA rise is a sign of a steam or coolant leak

The subcooling margin, the saturation temperature at the current pressure minus the hot-leg temperature, tells the operator how far the coolant is from boiling.

Plant states: from normal to design extension

Safety analysis sorts every situation into plant states, from frequent and harmless to very rare and severe. The rarer the event, the more damage is tolerated, but never a large release.

  • Normal operation: startup, power operation, shutdown, refuelling and testing within the operational limits.
  • Anticipated operational occurrences (AOO): disturbances expected once or several times in the life of the plant, such as a turbine trip, the loss of a feedwater pump or the loss of off-site power. The plant must return to normal without fuel damage.
  • Design basis accidents (DBA): rare events the plant is explicitly designed to cope with, such as a loss-of-coolant accident or a main steam line break. Analysis assumes conservative conditions plus one additional single failure. Fuel damage must stay within limits and the containment must hold.
  • Design extension conditions (DEC): events beyond the design basis, such as a station blackout (loss of all alternating current power) or a severe accident with core melt. The goal is to prevent or limit core damage and to avoid early or large releases. Older documents call these beyond design basis accidents (BDBA).

Reactor trip: the fast answer

A reactor trip (American English: scram) is the rapid automatic shutdown of the chain reaction. When a measured parameter crosses a trip setpoint, the reactor protection system opens the trip breakers that feed the control rod drive mechanisms. The electromagnets release the rods, which fall into the core by gravity within a few seconds. The design is fail-safe: a loss of power also drops the rods.

Typical trip signals are high neutron flux, low or high primary pressure, high coolant temperature, low primary flow, low steam generator level, high containment pressure and the manual trip button in the control room. A trip is itself an anticipated operational occurrence and causes no damage.

Engineered safety features: the long answer

Stopping the chain reaction is not enough; decay heat must be removed and the barriers protected. The engineered safety features (ESF) do this, and the part of the safety I&C that starts them is the ESF actuation system.

  • Emergency core cooling system (ECCS): injects borated water into the primary circuit when coolant is lost. It has high-pressure pumps for small leaks, passive accumulators (nitrogen-pressurized tanks that discharge by themselves at low pressure) and low-pressure pumps for large breaks and long-term cooling.
  • Auxiliary or emergency feedwater: pumps that feed the steam generators when normal feedwater is lost, so decay heat can still be removed through the secondary side.
  • Containment isolation: closing of all pipes and ducts through the containment wall that are not needed for safety, triggered by high containment pressure or high radiation.
  • Containment spray: condenses steam, lowers pressure and washes out radioactive iodine.
  • Emergency power: diesel generators and batteries that feed all of the above when the grid is lost.

Safety features are built in redundant trains (also called divisions): identical, physically separated sets of equipment, each able to perform the function alone. A VVER-440 V-213 has three trains; an EPR has four.

The loss-of-coolant accident

A loss-of-coolant accident (LOCA) is a break or leak in the primary circuit boundary. Pressure falls, the water starts to boil and the pressurizer level drops. If the core is uncovered, the cladding overheats and may fail. A large-break LOCA, the complete rupture of a main coolant pipe, is the classic sizing case for the ECCS and the containment. A small-break LOCA (a few centimetres) is much more likely and develops over minutes to hours. A steam generator tube rupture is a special LOCA because primary water escapes to the secondary side and can bypass the containment.

For the I&C engineer, a LOCA appears as a pattern of signals: falling primary pressure, falling pressurizer level, and rising containment pressure, temperature, humidity and sump level. The protection system uses several of these, in redundant channels, to trip the reactor and start the ECCS.

Common mistake

Mixing up the reactor trip with the safety injection. The trip stops the chain reaction and has many triggers. Safety injection is a separate engineered safety feature, started only by signals that indicate a loss of coolant, because an unnecessary injection of cold borated water is itself a disturbance.

The Slovak fleet and its regulator

Slovakia has two nuclear sites, both operated by Slovenské elektrárne. Bohunice has the two V2 units (3 and 4, VVER-440 V-213) in operation; the older V1 units (V-230 type) were shut down in 2006 and 2008 and are being decommissioned. Mochovce has units 1 and 2 (in operation since the late 1990s) and the newer units 3 and 4; unit 3 started up in 2023. The regulator is ÚJD SR, the Nuclear Regulatory Authority of the Slovak Republic, an independent state authority. It issues licences, reviews safety documentation, inspects the plants and applies the Slovak Atomic Act, in line with IAEA safety standards and the reference levels of the Western European Nuclear Regulators Association (WENRA).

Interview angle

Interviewers often ask "What are the three barriers?" or "What happens after a reactor trip?" Answer with structure: name the barrier or system, say what it protects against, and give one typical signal that triggers it. Using AOO, DBA and DEC correctly shows that you can read safety documentation.

Tip

Learn the three fundamental safety functions as a rhythm: control reactivity, cool the fuel, confine the radioactivity. Every safety I&C function you will ever design serves one of these three.

Key terms

Barrier
A physical boundary that keeps radioactive material in place: fuel cladding, primary circuit boundary and containment.
Defence in depth
Several independent levels of protection arranged so that the failure of one level is compensated by the next.
Anticipated operational occurrence (AOO)
A disturbance expected during the life of the plant, such as a turbine trip, that must cause no fuel damage.
Design basis accident (DBA)
A rare accident the plant is designed to withstand with limited fuel damage and an intact containment, for example a LOCA.
Design extension condition (DEC)
A condition beyond the design basis, including multiple failures and severe accidents, for which the plant must still avoid large releases.
Reactor trip (scram)
The rapid automatic insertion of all control rods to stop the chain reaction, triggered by the protection system.
Engineered safety features (ESF)
Systems such as emergency core cooling, auxiliary feedwater and containment isolation that protect the barriers after an accident starts.
Loss-of-coolant accident (LOCA)
A break in the primary circuit boundary through which coolant escapes, threatening the cooling of the fuel.
Train (division)
One of several redundant, physically separated sets of safety equipment, each able to perform the function alone.
ÚJD SR
The Nuclear Regulatory Authority of the Slovak Republic, the independent state body that licenses and inspects Slovak nuclear installations.

Say it in English

  • "The three barriers are the fuel cladding, the primary circuit boundary and the containment."
  • "A reactor trip is fail-safe: if the power to the rod drive mechanisms is lost, the rods drop by gravity."
  • "We classify events as anticipated operational occurrences, design basis accidents or design extension conditions."
  • "In a loss-of-coolant accident the protection system sees falling primary pressure and rising containment pressure, and it starts the safety injection."
  • "Slovakia operates four VVER-440 units at Bohunice and Mochovce under the supervision of ÚJD SR."

Check yourself

Which of the following is the second barrier against the release of radioactivity?

Counting from the inside, the cladding is first, the primary circuit boundary (vessel, pipes, steam generator tubes) second and the containment third.

A turbine trip that happens a few times during the plant's life is classified as what?

Anticipated operational occurrences are disturbances expected once or several times in the plant's life and must not cause fuel damage.

How does a reactor trip insert the control rods in a PWR?

Opening the trip breakers de-energizes the electromagnets that hold the rods, so they drop within a few seconds. Loss of power therefore also trips the reactor, which is fail-safe.

Which signal pattern is typical of a loss-of-coolant accident?

Coolant escaping from the primary circuit lowers its pressure and the pressurizer level, while the released steam raises the containment pressure, temperature and humidity.

What is ÚJD SR?

ÚJD SR is the independent state authority that licenses, reviews and inspects nuclear installations in Slovakia; the plants are operated by Slovenské elektrárne.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

What are the three barriers and what is defence in depth?

The three barriers are physical boundaries between the fission products and the environment: the fuel cladding, the primary circuit pressure boundary, and the containment building. Defence in depth is the principle that we never rely on a single provision. There are several independent levels: good design and quality to prevent problems, control systems to handle disturbances, the protection system and engineered safety features to handle accidents, severe accident management, and finally emergency planning. As an I&C engineer I work mostly on the third level, the protection system that defends the barriers when an accident starts.

What happens in the plant after a reactor trip?

First the protection system opens the trip breakers, the rod drive mechanisms lose power and the rods fall into the core, so the chain reaction stops within a few seconds. The turbine trips as well. Then the plant has to remove decay heat, which is still several percent of full power. In the normal case the steam generators do this with auxiliary feedwater and the steam dump. If the event is an accident, for example a loss of coolant, the engineered safety features start automatically: safety injection, containment isolation and, if needed, containment spray, all powered by the emergency diesels.

Can you explain the difference between a design basis accident and a design extension condition?

A design basis accident is a rare but postulated event that the plant is explicitly designed to withstand with conservative assumptions, including one additional single failure. Examples are a loss-of-coolant accident or a main steam line break. The acceptance criteria are limited fuel damage and an intact containment. A design extension condition goes beyond that: multiple failures such as a station blackout, or severe accidents with core melt. Here the aim is to prevent or limit core damage and above all to avoid early or large releases. This category was reinforced after Fukushima in 2011.

What is a loss-of-coolant accident and how does the I&C recognize it?

A loss-of-coolant accident is a break in the primary circuit boundary, from a small leak to the rupture of a main pipe. Coolant escapes, the pressure drops and the water starts to boil, so the fuel can be uncovered if nothing is done. The protection system recognizes it through a pattern of signals measured in redundant channels: falling primary pressure, falling pressurizer level and rising containment pressure, temperature and humidity. It trips the reactor and starts the emergency core cooling, which injects borated water, and it isolates the containment.

What do you know about the Slovak nuclear fleet and its regulator?

Slovakia has two sites operated by Slovenské elektrárne. Bohunice has units 3 and 4, and Mochovce has units 1 and 2 plus the newer units 3 and 4; all are VVER-440 V-213 pressurized water reactors. The older Bohunice V1 units were shut down in 2006 and 2008. Nuclear power covers more than half of the country's electricity. The regulator is ÚJD SR, the Nuclear Regulatory Authority of the Slovak Republic. It licenses the plants, reviews their safety documentation and inspects them, following the Atomic Act and international standards from the IAEA and WENRA.

Day 1 · Hour 3

What instrumentation and control is, and where a Design Engineer sits

This hour defines I&C from first principles, separates operational from safety I&C, compares the technologies you will hear about (DCS, PLC, safety platform) and explains the difference between a function and a system. These are the concepts the job description is built on.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Describe I&C as a sense, decide, act loop and name its five layers.
  • Explain the difference between operational I&C and safety I&C, and why they are kept independent.
  • Compare a DCS, a PLC and a safety-qualified platform in plain English.
  • Distinguish an I&C function from the system that implements it, with an example.
  • Describe the tasks of a Design Engineer for safety I&C in the project life cycle.

Sense, decide, act

Instrumentation and control (I&C) is the nervous system of the plant. Instrumentation means measuring: sensors that turn a physical quantity such as pressure into an electrical signal, and indicators that show it to people. Control means acting on the process: logic and controllers that decide, and actuators such as valves, pumps and breakers that do. Every I&C function, from a simple level indication to the reactor protection system, follows the same loop:

  1. Sense: a sensor measures the process (primary pressure is 12.1 MPa).
  2. Decide: logic compares the value with a setpoint and applies rules (pressure is below 11.0 MPa in two out of three channels, therefore trip).
  3. Act: a command goes to an actuator (open the trip breakers, start the injection pump).

A fourth element is always present: inform. The results are shown to the operator as indications and alarms, and recorded for later analysis.

The five layers of an I&C system

  • Field devices live in the plant: temperature sensors, pressure transmitters, limit switches, valve actuators, motor starters.
  • Signal conditioning and input/output (I/O) modules receive the electrical signals (for example 4 to 20 mA), protect the electronics with galvanic isolation, filter noise and convert analog signals to digital numbers. Output modules do the reverse for commands.
  • Processing units (controllers, processors) execute the application logic, usually in a fixed cycle of some tens of milliseconds.
  • Communication links the processors to each other and to the control room, through point-to-point cables or networks.
  • Human-machine interface (HMI) is everything the operator sees and touches: screens, conventional panels with indicators and push-buttons, and the alarm system.

Operational I&C versus safety I&C

Not all I&C has the same importance. Operational I&C runs the plant: it regulates power, pressure, levels and temperatures, sequences the start of pumps, and gives the operator the picture of the plant. If it fails, the plant may trip and lose money, but the barriers are not threatened. Safety I&C protects the barriers: it detects accident conditions and automatically trips the reactor and starts the engineered safety features. If it fails when needed, people and the environment can be harmed.

Standards therefore classify I&C functions by their importance to safety and assign the systems that implement them to safety classes. In the IEC vocabulary used by Framatome (IEC 61226 and IEC 61513), functions are category A, B or C and systems are class 1, 2 or 3. Category A, class 1 covers the reactor protection system and the actuation of safety features; category C covers functions with only an indirect safety role. The higher the class, the stricter the rules: qualified hardware, formally developed and verified software, redundancy, independence, periodic testing and much more documentation.

AspectOperational I&CSafety I&C
PurposeRun the plant efficientlyProtect the barriers in accidents
ExamplesPressurizer pressure control, feedwater control, turbine controlReactor trip, safety injection start, containment isolation
Consequence of failureLost production, plant tripPossible fuel damage or release
Typical classClass 3 or non-classifiedClass 1 (category A functions)
TechnologyDCS, PLCSafety-qualified platform or hard-wired logic
Design focusFlexibility, performance, costReliability, simplicity, independence, testability

Two rules follow. First, safety I&C must be independent of operational I&C, so that a fault in the control system cannot disable the protection. Second, when both send a command to the same valve or pump, the safety command has priority. Some plants, especially of German design, add a middle layer called a limitation system: it acts before the protection system, for example by reducing power, to avoid unnecessary trips.

DCS, PLC and safety-qualified platform

You will hear three technology names. A programmable logic controller (PLC) is an industrial computer that reads inputs, runs a logic program in a repeating cycle and writes outputs. PLCs came from factory automation, are cheap and robust, and are programmed in standard languages such as function block diagrams or ladder logic. A distributed control system (DCS) is a family of many controllers connected by a redundant network to common engineering tools and large operator screens. It is built for continuous processes with thousands of analog loops, so it is the natural choice for the operational I&C of a power plant. A safety-qualified platform is a set of hardware modules and software that was developed and tested under nuclear safety standards (for example IEC 60880 for software), with a simple, deterministic design: strictly cyclic processing, no interrupts, no dynamic memory, fixed communication, and proven qualification against temperature, vibration, seismic loads and electromagnetic disturbance. TELEPERM XS, the Framatome platform, is such a product; other vendors have their own.

Note

Technically a safety platform is also "a PLC with a very strict pedigree". The difference is not the basic idea but the evidence: every component, every line of software and every tool is documented, verified and qualified so that a regulator can accept it for class 1 functions.

Function versus system

Engineers separate what must be done from how it is done. An I&C function is a statement of what: "Trip the reactor if the primary pressure falls below 11.0 MPa." A good function definition also gives the setpoint, the permitted delay, the accuracy, the voting (two out of three channels) and when the function may be bypassed. An I&C system is the equipment that implements one or many functions: the sensors, cabinets, processors, cables and software, organised in trains. One system such as the reactor protection system implements dozens of functions, and one function may need several systems (sensors from one, logic in another, actuation through switchgear).

This separation drives the documentation. Requirements documents describe functions: functional needs (what, when, how fast, how accurate) and non-functional needs (reliability, independence, environmental conditions, testability, maintainability). Design documents describe systems: architecture, sizing, interfaces, monitoring and testing. A Design Engineer must be able to move between these two views without mixing them up.

Common mistake

Describing a system when asked about a function, or the reverse. If the interviewer asks "What is the function?", answer with a sentence that starts with a verb and names a condition: "Start safety injection when primary pressure is below the setpoint in two of four channels." Do not start with cabinets and processors.

Where a Design Engineer sits

A nuclear I&C project passes through basic design, detailed design, implementation (hardware manufacture and application software), factory testing, installation and commissioning, and finally operation and maintenance. A Design Engineer for safety I&C works mainly in basic design and the early part of detailed design. Typical tasks are: collecting the functional and non-functional needs from the customer, the safety analysis and the process engineers; writing the system specification and the design basis; defining the architecture (how many trains, which functions in which cabinet, how signals are shared between trains); sizing the system (number of inputs and outputs, processor load, cabinet space, power consumption and heat); defining interfaces to the operational I&C, the HMI and the electrical systems; specifying self-monitoring and alarms; and defining how each function will be periodically tested. The outputs are documents, reviewed by colleagues, the customer and the regulator, so clear written English and attention to detail are part of the job itself.

Interview angle

Expect "What do you think a Design Engineer does here?" A strong answer connects the job posting to the life cycle: you turn safety requirements into a specified, sized and documented system architecture, you define interfaces and testing, and you work in an international team with the customer and the regulator in English.

Tip

When you explain any I&C topic, walk through sense, decide, act, inform. It keeps your answers structured and shows that you think in functions, not in boxes.

Key terms

Instrumentation and control (I&C)
All equipment that measures the process, decides what to do and acts on it, plus the interface to the operators.
Field device
A sensor, transmitter or actuator installed in the plant and connected to the I&C by cables.
Input/output (I/O) module
An electronic card that converts field signals into digital values for the processor, or processor commands into output signals.
Human-machine interface (HMI)
The screens, panels, indicators and alarms through which operators monitor and control the plant.
Safety I&C
The I&C that detects accident conditions and automatically trips the reactor and actuates safety features; class 1 in the IEC classification.
Programmable logic controller (PLC)
An industrial computer that cyclically reads inputs, runs a logic program and writes outputs.
Distributed control system (DCS)
A networked family of controllers with common engineering and operator tools, used for plant-wide operational control.
Safety-qualified platform
Hardware and software developed and qualified under nuclear standards for safety-class functions, for example TELEPERM XS.
I&C function
A statement of what must be done and under which conditions, independent of the equipment that does it.

Say it in English

  • "I&C is a sense, decide, act loop: sensors measure the process, logic decides and actuators carry out the command."
  • "Safety I&C must be independent of operational I&C, and its commands have priority over operational commands."
  • "A function says what must be done; a system is the equipment that implements it."
  • "A safety-qualified platform is deterministic and simple by design, and every component is qualified and documented."
  • "As a Design Engineer I would turn the functional and non-functional requirements into a specified and sized system architecture."

Check yourself

Which layer converts a 4 to 20 mA signal into a digital number for the processor?

Input modules isolate, filter and digitize field signals; the processor then works with numbers.

Why must safety I&C be independent of operational I&C?

Independence ensures that the system that caused a disturbance cannot also prevent the protective response; safety commands also have priority.

Which statement correctly describes an I&C function?

A function states what must be done under which condition; the other options describe equipment, which belongs to a system.

What is the main difference between a safety-qualified platform and an ordinary PLC?

The basic principle is similar, but the safety platform has a deterministic cyclic design and a complete body of qualification and verification evidence acceptable to regulators.

In which project phase does a Design Engineer for safety I&C mainly work?

The Design Engineer elaborates basic design documentation: requirements collection, specifications, architecture, sizing, interfaces, monitoring and periodic testing.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

How would you explain what I&C is to someone outside the industry?

I would say that I&C is the nervous system of the plant. Instrumentation is the senses: sensors measure pressure, temperature, level, flow and neutron flux and turn them into electrical signals. Control is the brain and the muscles: logic compares the measurements with limits and decides, and actuators such as valves, pumps and breakers carry out the decision. Everything is also shown to the operators as indications and alarms. In a nuclear plant part of this system has a special job, the safety I&C, which automatically shuts the reactor down and starts the safety systems if something goes wrong.

What is the difference between operational I&C and safety I&C?

Operational I&C runs the plant day to day: it regulates pressure, levels and power, sequences equipment and gives the operators their picture. If it fails, we lose production. Safety I&C protects the barriers: it detects accident conditions and automatically trips the reactor and actuates engineered safety features. If it fails when needed, the consequences can be radiological. So safety I&C is class 1, built on a qualified platform with redundancy, independence from the operational systems, priority of its commands and periodic testing, and it is documented in much more detail for the regulator.

What is the difference between a DCS, a PLC and a safety platform like TELEPERM XS?

A PLC is a single industrial controller that runs logic in a cycle; it is robust and cheap and comes from factory automation. A DCS is a plant-wide system of many controllers on a redundant network with shared engineering and operator tools, built for continuous processes with thousands of loops, so it is the typical choice for operational I&C. A safety-qualified platform uses a similar basic idea but is designed to be simple and deterministic, with strictly cyclic processing and no interrupts, and every hardware module, software element and tool is qualified and verified under nuclear standards so it can be licensed for class 1 functions.

What do we mean by an I&C function, as opposed to a system?

A function says what must be done and under which condition, independently of equipment, for example "start safety injection when primary pressure is below the setpoint in two of four channels within one second". It comes with a setpoint, a response time, an accuracy and a voting rule. A system is the set of equipment that implements functions: sensors, I/O, processors, cabinets, cables and software organised in redundant trains. One system implements many functions, and a function can span several systems. In design we first specify the functions and then allocate them to systems and size the systems accordingly.

What do you think a Design Engineer for safety I&C actually does?

From the job description and what I have learned, the Design Engineer works mostly in basic design. I would collect the functional and non-functional needs from the customer and the safety analysis, then write the specification: the system architecture with its redundant trains, how functions are allocated to cabinets and processors, the sizing of inputs, outputs, processing load and power, the interfaces to the operational I&C, the HMI and the electrical systems, and the self-monitoring and periodic testing concept. The result is a set of documents that colleagues, the customer and the regulator review, so writing clearly in English is a core part of the work.

Day 1 · Hour 4

Sensors and signals: how the plant is measured

Every safety function begins with a measurement. This hour explains the sensors you will meet in a nuclear plant, the standard electrical signals that carry their values, and the vocabulary used in specifications. The question "why 4 to 20 mA?" is a favourite of interviewers.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Explain how resistance thermometers, thermocouples, pressure and differential-pressure transmitters and neutron detectors measure the process.
  • Explain the 4 to 20 mA standard, why the live zero matters, and when 0 to 10 V or binary 24 V and 48 V DC signals are used.
  • Define range, span, accuracy, resolution, response time, calibration and drift.
  • Describe validity and plausibility checks on a measured signal.
  • Convert raw counts to engineering units with a worked example.

Temperature: resistance thermometers and thermocouples

A resistance temperature detector (RTD) uses the fact that the electrical resistance of a metal rises with temperature. The most common type is the Pt100: a platinum element with 100 Ω at 0 °C that gains about 0.385 Ω per °C, so it reads about 138.5 Ω at 100 °C and about 212 Ω at 300 °C. The input module sends a small measuring current through the element and measures the voltage; because the cable also has resistance, three-wire or four-wire connections cancel it out. RTDs are accurate (about ±0.75 °C at 300 °C for a class A element) but slow, several seconds inside a protective thermowell. They measure the hot-leg and cold-leg temperatures.

A thermocouple is a junction of two different metals that produces a small voltage depending on the temperature difference between the measuring junction and the reference junction at the cable end. Type K (nickel-chromium against nickel-aluminium) gives about 41 µV per °C. The input module measures the temperature of its own terminals (cold-junction compensation) to compute the absolute temperature. Thermocouples are fast, small and work above 1000 °C, but are less accurate (about ±1 to 2 °C). They are typical for core-exit temperatures.

Pressure, level and flow: the differential-pressure family

In a pressure transmitter, the process pressure deflects a thin metal diaphragm; a piezoresistive or capacitive element converts the deflection into an electrical quantity, and the electronics correct it for temperature and produce a standard output signal. A differential-pressure (dp) transmitter has a high-side and a low-side port and measures the difference between them. This one device type measures three quantities:

  • Pressure: the low side is open to the atmosphere (gauge pressure) or sealed under vacuum (absolute pressure).
  • Level: a water column exerts Δp = ρ · g · h (density times gravity times height), about 9.8 kPa per metre of cold water. In a closed, pressurized vessel such as a steam generator, the low side is connected to the steam space through a reference leg kept full of water, and the I&C corrects the result for the density of hot water and steam.
  • Flow: an orifice plate (a plate with a calibrated hole) in the pipe creates a pressure drop. Flow is proportional to the square root of Δp, so the square root is extracted in the transmitter or in the I&C. Small flows are measured poorly: at 10% of the Δp span the flow is already 32%.

Neutron flux and binary sensors

Neutron flux is measured by ex-core detectors around the reactor vessel. An ionization chamber is a gas-filled tube coated with boron-10. Neutrons react with the boron and produce charged particles that ionize the gas; the resulting current is proportional to the neutron flux and therefore to reactor power. Because power spans about ten decades from shutdown to full power, three overlapping ranges are used: the source range (pulse-counting proportional counters, for start-up), the intermediate range (compensated ionization chambers with a logarithmic output) and the power range (uncompensated ionization chambers with a linear output, 0 to 120% of power).

Binary sensors give a yes or no. Limit switches are mechanical or inductive contacts operated by a valve stem or a damper blade and report "fully open" or "fully closed". Modern designs prefer an analog transmitter plus a software limit, because one transmitter gives the value, the trend, any number of limits and a way to monitor its own health.

Standard signals: 4 to 20 mA, 0 to 10 V and binary inputs

The 4 to 20 mA current loop is the most common analog signal. The transmitter regulates the current in a two-wire loop powered from 24 V DC in the cabinet. The current is the same at every point of the loop regardless of cable length and resistance, so the signal survives hundreds of metres of cable and resists noise.

The live zero is the key feature: 0% of the range is 4 mA, not 0 mA. A current of 0 mA cannot be a valid measurement, so a broken wire, a short circuit or a dead transmitter is recognized immediately. The recommendation NAMUR NE43 goes further: below 3.6 mA or above 21 mA means "fault", and a smart transmitter drives its output to about 3.5 or 22 mA when its self-test detects an internal failure.

0 to 10 V signals are simple but suffer from voltage drop and noise, so they are used only inside cabinets or over short distances. Binary inputs use 24 V DC inside the I&C (24 V means 1, 0 V means 0). For field contacts over long cables, 48 V DC is used, because the higher voltage keeps the contacts clean and the signal more immune to disturbance. Safety designs often add line monitoring: resistors at the contact let the input module distinguish open, closed, broken wire and short circuit.

SignalTypical useStrengthFault detection
4 to 20 mATransmitters in the fieldImmune to cable length, two wires carry power and signalLive zero; below 3.6 mA or above 21 mA is a fault
0 to 10 VInside cabinets, short distancesSimpleNone by itself (0 V is a valid value)
24 V DC binarySignals within I&C cabinetsStandard, cheapOnly with line monitoring resistors
48 V DC binaryField contacts, long cablesRobust against contact resistanceOnly with line monitoring resistors
Pulses (counts)Source-range neutron detectorsWorks at very low fluxLoss of pulses can be monitored

Specification vocabulary: range, span, accuracy, drift

  • Range: the lower and upper values the instrument is set to measure, for example 10 to 18 MPa.
  • Span: upper minus lower range value, here 8 MPa. Error figures are usually a percentage of span.
  • Accuracy: the maximum error under reference conditions, for example ±0.1% of span = ±8 kPa.
  • Resolution: the smallest change the chain can represent; a 12-bit converter has 4096 steps (0.024% of span).
  • Response time: how fast the output follows a step, given as a time constant (63% of the step) or T90 (90%). A pressure transmitter responds in well under a second; an RTD in a thermowell needs several seconds.
  • Calibration: comparing the instrument with a reference standard and adjusting it, typically at each refuelling outage.
  • Drift: the slow change of the output with time, temperature and radiation, for example ±0.2% of span per year. Setpoint calculations must allow for the drift that can accumulate between calibrations.

Note

"±0.5% accuracy" means nothing until you know "of what". For a 10 to 18 MPa transmitter, 0.5% of span is 40 kPa everywhere; 0.5% of reading would be 50 kPa at 10 MPa and 90 kPa at 18 MPa. A careful specification always says which.

Validity and plausibility checks

A validity check asks: is this signal technically usable? The input module and processor check that the current is inside the 3.6 to 21 mA window, that no wire break or short circuit is detected, and that the module and its communication link are healthy. The result is a validity flag that travels with the value through the logic. The design must define what happens to an invalid channel: treat it conservatively as tripped, or exclude it from the voting and adapt the voting (two out of three becomes one out of two), always with an alarm.

A plausibility check asks: is this value physically believable? Typical checks compare redundant channels (deviation alarm above a threshold), compare related quantities (measured temperature against the saturation temperature at the measured pressure), limit the rate of change (primary pressure cannot fall 5 MPa in 10 ms) and detect values that never change.

Worked example: from milliamps to megapascals

A primary pressure transmitter has the range 10 to 18 MPa and a 4 to 20 mA output. The input module maps 4 mA to 0 counts and 20 mA to 20000 counts (1250 counts per mA). What does the processor see at 12.3 MPa, and what pressure corresponds to 14250 counts?

Range 10 to 18 MPa, span = 18 - 10 = 8 MPa
Current  I = 4 mA + 16 mA × (P - 10) / 8

Forward (process to counts):
  P = 12.3 MPa  →  fraction = (12.3 - 10) / 8 = 0.2875
  I = 4 + 16 × 0.2875 = 8.6 mA
  counts = (8.6 - 4) × 1250 = 5750

Reverse (counts to engineering units):
  counts = 14250  →  fraction = 14250 / 20000 = 0.7125
  P = 10 + 8 × 0.7125 = 15.7 MPa

Resolution: 8 MPa / 20000 counts = 0.4 kPa per count
Validity: counts below (3.6 - 4) × 1250 = -500 or above
          (21 - 4) × 1250 = 21250  →  mark the channel INVALID

Common mistake

Forgetting the live zero when scaling: 8.6 mA is not 8.6/20 = 43% of span but (8.6 - 4)/16 = 28.75%. The same trap exists for flow: a dp transmitter at 50% of span means about 71% flow, because of the square root.

Tip

For any signal, ask three questions: what physical quantity, what electrical form (mA, V, contact, pulses), and how would I know if it were broken? The third question is what makes a safety engineer.

Interview angle

"Why do we use 4 to 20 mA instead of 0 to 20 mA?" is a classic. Give both reasons: the current signal is independent of cable length and resistance, and the live zero makes a broken wire or dead transmitter detectable. Add NAMUR NE43 for extra credit.

Key terms

Resistance temperature detector (RTD, Pt100)
A temperature sensor whose platinum element has 100 Ω at 0 °C and about 0.385 Ω more per °C; accurate and stable but slow.
Thermocouple
A junction of two metals that produces a small voltage depending on the temperature difference to the reference junction; fast and robust, less accurate.
Transmitter
A field device that converts a measured quantity into a standard electrical signal such as 4 to 20 mA.
Differential pressure (dp)
The difference between two pressures; used to measure level (hydrostatic column) and flow (orifice plate) as well as pressure itself.
Ionization chamber
A gas-filled neutron detector with a boron coating whose output current is proportional to neutron flux and so to reactor power.
Live zero
A signal scale whose 0% is not zero current (4 mA), so that a broken wire (0 mA) can be distinguished from a real zero.
Span
The difference between the upper and lower range values of an instrument; error figures are usually a percentage of span.
Drift
The slow change of an instrument's output over time, caused by ageing, temperature and radiation, corrected by periodic calibration.
Validity flag
A status that travels with a measured value and says whether the signal is technically usable (in range, no wire break, module healthy).

Say it in English

  • "We use 4 to 20 mA because the current does not depend on cable length, and the live zero lets us detect a broken wire."
  • "A Pt100 has 100 ohms at zero degrees and increases by about 0.385 ohms per degree, so we use a three- or four-wire connection to cancel the cable resistance."
  • "Level and flow are both measured with differential-pressure transmitters; for flow we need the square root of the pressure drop."
  • "The specification must say whether the accuracy is a percentage of span or of reading."
  • "An out-of-range signal is flagged invalid, and the design defines how the voting logic treats an invalid channel."

Check yourself

A 4 to 20 mA pressure transmitter has the range 0 to 16 MPa. What pressure does 12 mA represent?

12 mA is (12 - 4)/16 = 50% of the span, and 50% of 16 MPa is 8 MPa. Dividing 12 by 20 ignores the live zero and gives the wrong 9.6 MPa.

Why is the live zero of the 4 to 20 mA standard useful?

Because 0% is 4 mA, any current near 0 mA means a fault in the loop rather than a low process value.

A Pt100 reads 138.5 Ω. What is its approximate temperature?

The Pt100 has 100 Ω at 0 °C and gains about 0.385 Ω per °C; 38.5 Ω above 100 Ω corresponds to about 100 °C.

Why is flow measured with an orifice plate not simply proportional to the differential pressure?

The pressure drop across an orifice grows with the square of the flow, so the I&C or the transmitter must take the square root; small flows are therefore measured poorly.

What is a plausibility check?

Validity checks look at the technical health of the signal; plausibility checks compare the value with physics and with other measurements.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

Why is 4 to 20 mA the standard analog signal, and not 0 to 20 mA or 0 to 10 V?

There are two reasons. First, a current loop is robust: the current is the same everywhere in the loop, so the signal does not depend on cable length or resistance, and two wires carry both the power and the signal over hundreds of metres. A voltage signal would lose part of its value along the cable and pick up noise. Second, the live zero: 0% is 4 mA, so a reading near 0 mA can only mean a broken wire, a short circuit or a dead transmitter. With 0 to 20 mA we could not tell a real zero from a failure. NAMUR NE43 adds defined fault bands below 3.6 mA and above 21 mA.

How would you measure the level in a steam generator?

With a differential-pressure transmitter. The high side is connected near the bottom of the steam generator and the low side to the steam space through a reference leg that is kept full of water. The difference between the two columns is proportional to the water level. Because the steam generator operates at high temperature and pressure, the density of the water and steam changes, so the I&C applies a density correction based on pressure. For safety we use several redundant transmitters, and we distinguish narrow-range measurements for control from wide-range measurements for accident conditions.

What is the difference between an RTD and a thermocouple, and where would you use each?

An RTD, typically a Pt100, measures the change of resistance of a platinum element, about 0.385 ohms per degree from 100 ohms at zero degrees. It is accurate and stable but slow, and it needs a measuring current and a three- or four-wire connection to compensate the cable. A thermocouple produces a small voltage from the junction of two metals, about 41 microvolts per degree for type K, and needs cold-junction compensation. It is fast, small and works at very high temperatures but is less accurate. I would use RTDs for hot-leg and cold-leg temperatures, and thermocouples for core-exit temperatures.

What do range, span, accuracy and drift mean in an instrument specification?

The range is the lower and upper value the instrument is set to measure, for example 10 to 18 MPa. The span is the difference, 8 MPa. Accuracy is the maximum error under reference conditions, usually given as a percentage of span, so ±0.1% here means ±8 kPa; I always check whether it is of span or of reading. Drift is the slow change of the output over time due to ageing, temperature and radiation. It matters because it accumulates between calibrations, so when we calculate a protection setpoint we have to include a drift allowance together with accuracy and other uncertainties.

How does a safety system deal with a failed sensor signal?

First it has to detect the failure. The input module checks that the current is within the valid window, roughly 3.6 to 21 mA, and detects wire breaks and module faults, and the processor can add plausibility checks such as comparing redundant channels or limiting the rate of change. The result is a validity flag attached to the value. Then the design defines the reaction: in many protection systems an invalid channel is treated conservatively, as if it had reached its trip condition, or it is removed from the vote and the voting is adapted, for example from two out of three to one out of two, and an alarm informs the operator.

Day 1 · Hour 5

Actuators and final elements: how a command moves the plant

After sensing and deciding comes acting. This hour explains the devices that move the plant (valves, pumps, breakers, dampers), the commands and feedback around them, and the priority logic that decides whose command wins. Interviewers like to ask how a signal becomes a valve movement and why a safety command must have priority.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Describe how motor-operated, solenoid and air-operated valves, pumps, circuit breakers and dampers are driven and what feedback they return.
  • Explain fail-open and fail-closed behaviour and how the fail-safe position is chosen.
  • Explain binary commands, limit and torque switches and command/feedback monitoring.
  • Describe drive priority logic: manual, automatic and protection commands, interlocks and permissives.
  • Trace the complete path from a sensor to a breaker opening, with a rough time budget.

What a final element is

An actuator converts an electrical command into mechanical action: torque, force or the switching of a circuit. The final element is the device that directly changes the process: the disc of a valve, the impeller of a pump, the contacts of a breaker. Every actuator needs a source of power (electric, pneumatic or hydraulic), a command, and a way to report back what it did. The first question a safety engineer asks about any actuator is: what does it do when its power or its command is lost?

Valves: motor-operated, solenoid and air-operated

A motor-operated valve (MOV) has an electric motor, usually three-phase 400 V AC, and a gearbox that turns the valve stem. Large valves travel from open to closed in about 10 to 60 seconds. Two limit switches report the end positions, and a torque switch stops the motor when the valve is firmly seated or blocked, protecting motor and valve. On loss of power an MOV stays where it is ("fail as is"). The motor is switched by a reversing starter (two contactors), so the I&C sends "open" and "close" commands to the starter rather than to the valve.

A solenoid valve uses an electric coil that pulls a plunger directly. It is fast (milliseconds), small and simple, and a spring returns it when the coil is de-energized. Solenoid valves handle small lines and very often serve as pilot valves that admit or vent the air of a larger pneumatic actuator.

An air-operated valve (AOV) uses instrument air at about 0.5 to 0.7 MPa on a diaphragm or piston against a spring. When the air is vented or lost, the spring drives the valve to its fail position. A valve that needs air to open is fail-closed; one that needs air to close is fail-open. The choice comes from the safety analysis: a containment isolation valve or a main steam isolation valve fails closed, while a valve supplying cooling water to an essential consumer may fail open. Pneumatic valves close in a few seconds, which is why they are used where speed matters.

Valve typePowerSpeedOn loss of power or airTypical use
Motor-operated400 V AC motor10 to 60 sStays in positionIsolation, large valves, both directions
SolenoidDC coilMillisecondsSpring positionSmall lines, pilot for air valves
Air-operatedInstrument air plus pilotSecondsSpring drives to fail-open or fail-closedFast isolation, control valves

Common mistake

Thinking "fail-safe" always means "closed". Fail-safe means the position that is safe for the plant in that specific line. For a steam line it is closed; for an emergency cooling line it may be open. Always ask which position the safety analysis requires.

Pumps, circuit breakers and dampers

Pumps are driven by electric motors. Large motors, such as main coolant pumps (several megawatts) or safety injection pumps, are fed at medium voltage (6 kV in a VVER plant) through circuit breakers; smaller motors are fed at 400 V through contactors. "Start" means "close the breaker" and "stop" means "open the breaker". Feedback comes from the breaker position (closed means running), the motor current and the discharge pressure or flow.

A circuit breaker is a switching device that can also interrupt a fault current. It has a closing coil, an opening (trip) coil, a charged spring and auxiliary contacts that report its position. Its control circuit runs on 220 V DC from the battery-backed supply, so it works during a blackout. The reactor trip breakers use an undervoltage coil: the breaker opens when the coil loses voltage, so a loss of the protection system's output trips the reactor. Dampers are valves for air in ventilation ducts, driven electrically or pneumatically; they typically fail closed to isolate the containment ventilation.

Commands and feedback

Commands to drives are binary: open or close, start or stop, usually as a pulse of about one second or as a maintained signal. Each drive has a drive control function in the I&C that receives commands from several sources, checks them, outputs them to the starter or breaker and monitors the result. Position feedback uses two limit switches, "open" and "closed": while the valve travels both are false, and if both are true a switch is faulty. The drive control also performs command/feedback monitoring: after an "open" command, the "open" feedback must arrive within the travel time plus a margin, for example 30 s plus 5 s; otherwise a "drive fault" alarm is raised.

Note

The I&C never knows directly whether water flows. It knows that a breaker closed, that a limit switch changed and that a discharge pressure rose. Good design uses several independent feedbacks so that a stuck limit switch does not hide a stuck valve.

Drive priority logic: whose command wins

A single valve can receive commands from four sources: a manual command from the operator, an automatic command from the operational control system, a protection command from the safety I&C, and a local command from a maintenance panel near the equipment. The priority logic decides between them:

  1. The protection command has the highest priority. The operator and the control system cannot cancel it, and it stays active as long as the protection condition exists (or until an authorized reset, depending on the design).
  2. Between manual and automatic, the operator selects a mode and commands from the other mode are ignored. After a protection action, manual control returns only when the protection signal is gone and the operator resets.
  3. Equipment-protection interlocks may or may not override a protection command. Some stay active (never close a breaker onto a short circuit); others are deliberately bypassed in an accident, because a damaged pump is better than a damaged core. The requirements must say which, drive by drive.

In many safety designs the priority logic sits in a dedicated priority module close to the switchgear, built with simple qualified hardware, so that the path of the safety command is short, independent of the operational system and easy to test.

Interlocks and permissives

An interlock forbids an action while a condition exists: do not start the pump while its suction valve is closed; do not open the valve while the pressure difference across it is too high. A permissive is a condition that must be true before an action is allowed: a breaker may be closed only when the bus is live; a test mode is permitted only when the reactor is shut down. Protection functions also have operational permissives: a low-pressure trip is blocked at low power during start-up and automatically unblocked when power rises. All of these appear in the function diagrams, and a Design Engineer must specify every one of them and how it is tested.

The complete path from sensor to breaker

Here is the whole chain for a reactor trip on low primary pressure, with rough times:

  1. Pressure falls; the transmitter (range 10 to 18 MPa) outputs 6.0 mA for 11.0 MPa, within about 0.1 s.
  2. The input module in each of three redundant divisions digitizes and validates the signal.
  3. Each processor compares the value with the setpoint and sets its channel trip; the divisions exchange results over isolated links and each votes two out of three (about 0.1 to 0.2 s in total).
  4. The output module de-energizes its trip output and a relay in the trip path opens.
  5. The undervoltage coil of the trip breaker loses power and the breaker opens within about 0.05 s.
  6. The rod drive mechanisms lose power and the rods fall; insertion takes about 2 to 4 s.
  7. Breaker position, rod-bottom indications and falling neutron flux return as feedback to the control room.

From the pressure crossing the setpoint to the breaker opening takes roughly half a second; the rods need a few seconds more. The safety analysis assumes a total delay, and the I&C must prove by test that it stays below it.

Tip

For every actuator, write down four items: power source, command sources, feedback signals and fail position. If you cannot fill in all four, the design is not finished.

Interview angle

A typical question is "A safety system and the operator both want to control the same valve. Who wins and why?" Answer: the protection command has priority, the operator cannot override it, and the priority logic sits close to the switchgear in simple qualified hardware so the safety path stays independent and testable. Then mention which interlocks may be bypassed in an accident.

Key terms

Actuator / final element
The device that converts a command into mechanical action and directly changes the process: a valve drive, a pump motor, a breaker.
Motor-operated valve (MOV)
A valve driven by an electric motor through a gearbox; slow, powerful, stays in position on loss of power.
Air-operated valve (AOV)
A valve driven by compressed air against a spring; on loss of air the spring moves it to its fail-open or fail-closed position.
Fail-safe position
The position an actuator takes on loss of power or signal, chosen by the safety analysis as the safe one for that line.
Limit switch
A contact operated at the end of travel that reports "fully open" or "fully closed".
Torque switch
A switch in a motor actuator that stops the motor when the required torque is reached, indicating seating or a blockage.
Command/feedback monitoring
A check that the expected feedback arrives within the travel time after a command; otherwise a drive fault alarm is raised.
Priority logic
The logic that selects between manual, automatic, protection and local commands, giving the protection command the highest priority.
Interlock
A condition that forbids an action while it exists, to protect equipment or the plant.
Permissive
A condition that must be true before an action or a protection function is allowed.

Say it in English

  • "The protection command has priority over operational and manual commands, and the operator cannot override it."
  • "This valve is fail-closed: it needs air to open, so on loss of air the spring closes it."
  • "A motor-operated valve stays in its last position on loss of power, so we must consider that in the safety analysis."
  • "If the open feedback does not arrive within the travel time plus a margin, the drive control raises a discrepancy alarm."
  • "From the setpoint crossing to the trip breaker opening takes roughly half a second; the rods need a few seconds more."

Check yourself

An air-operated valve needs air to open. What happens on loss of instrument air?

Air-to-open means the spring acts in the closing direction, so when the air is lost the valve closes. This is the typical arrangement for isolation valves.

What does a torque switch in a motor-operated valve do?

Limit switches report end positions; the torque switch protects the motor and valve by stopping the drive when the torque limit is reached.

The operator sends a "close" command to a valve that the protection system is holding open. What should happen?

Protection commands have the highest priority and cannot be cancelled by manual or automatic operational commands while the protection condition exists.

Which statement describes a permissive?

A permissive enables an action (for example closing a breaker only when the bus is live), while an interlock forbids an action while a condition exists.

Why do reactor trip breakers use an undervoltage coil?

With an undervoltage coil the breaker opens when the coil is de-energized, so a failure of the protection system's output or power supply trips the reactor rather than blocking the trip.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

Can you walk me through what happens between a sensor detecting low pressure and the reactor tripping?

The pressure transmitter converts the pressure into a 4 to 20 mA signal. In each redundant division an input module digitizes and validates it, and the processor compares it with the trip setpoint. The divisions exchange their channel results over isolated links and each one votes, for example two out of three. When the vote is positive, the output module de-energizes the trip path, the undervoltage coil of the trip breaker loses power and the breaker opens in a few tens of milliseconds. The rod drive mechanisms lose power and the rods fall into the core within a few seconds. The whole chain to the breaker takes roughly half a second.

What is the difference between a motor-operated valve and an air-operated valve from an I&C point of view?

A motor-operated valve is driven by an electric motor through a gearbox. It is slow, tens of seconds, it stays in its last position on loss of power, and the I&C sends open and close commands to a reversing starter and reads back two limit switches and a torque switch. An air-operated valve uses compressed air against a spring. The I&C usually controls only a small solenoid pilot valve, the valve moves in a few seconds, and on loss of air or signal the spring drives it to a defined fail position. So for an MOV I must consider "fail as is" in the analysis, and for an AOV I must specify fail-open or fail-closed.

How do you decide whether a valve should fail open or fail closed?

It is not an I&C decision alone; it comes from the safety analysis of that specific line. I ask what the safe state is when everything is lost: air, electrical power and the I&C. A containment isolation valve or a main steam isolation valve should fail closed, because the safe state is a closed barrier. A valve that supplies cooling water to a safety-related consumer may need to fail open, because losing cooling is the danger.
