I would first check the plant safety analysis, because classification comes from there, not from my opinion. Typically a limitation function reduces power before a protection setpoint is reached, so it reduces how often the category A reactor trip is demanded. That is a complementary role, so it is usually category B. If the analysis credited it to prevent unacceptable consequences in a design basis event, it would have to be category A, and then it would need full redundancy and class 1 software. In many plants the limitation system is kept in class 2, separate from the protection system, so that the two layers of defence remain independent.

What does the class of a system mean for its interfaces with other systems?

The main rule is that a higher-class system must never depend on a lower-class system and must not be disturbed by its failure. In practice, a class 1 protection system can send data to the operational I&C and the plant computer, but only through a one-way, qualified interface, so that nothing can come back and influence a safety function. Commands from a lower class, such as an operator request from the operational system to a safety actuator, go through a priority module where the safety command always wins. Power supply and cooling for class 1 equipment also have to be class 1 support systems.

Have you noticed any differences between IEC and US terminology?

Yes, the biggest one is the phrase "safety-related". In IAEA and IEC language a safety system is the highest class, and "safety-related" means an item important to safety that is not a safety system, so it is lower. In the US rules and IEEE standards, "safety-related" is the highest class, and the electrical equipment in it is Class 1E. So the same words can mean the top or a lower class depending on who is speaking. In an international project I would always say which standard I mean, for example "class 1 according to IEC 61513" or "Class 1E according to IEEE 603".

Day 2 · Hour 3

Design principles for safety I&C: redundancy, independence, diversity and friends

These twelve principles are the checklist a reviewer uses on every safety I&C design, and the words an interviewer listens for. This hour defines each one and ties it to a concrete feature of a reactor protection system, so that you can explain the principle and immediately show how it looks in hardware.

By the end of this hour you can

  • State the single failure criterion and explain why four channels (N+2) are used rather than three.
  • Describe the three faces of independence: physical separation, electrical isolation and communication independence.
  • Explain fail-safe (de-energise-to-trip) design and its trade-off with spurious actuation.
  • Define determinism, simplicity, testability and qualification for a digital protection system.
  • Give one protection system example for each principle.

How to read this hour

Each principle is explained the same way: what it means, why it exists, and how it appears in a reactor protection system (RPS) with four redundant divisions. The principles come from IAEA SSR-2/1 and SSG-39, IEC 61513 and IEEE 603 (Hour 6); you need the ideas and examples, not clause numbers.

Redundancy and the single failure criterion

Redundancy means providing more channels than the minimum needed, so that the function survives the failure of one. The single failure criterion (SFC) is the formal rule: a safety group must perform its function even if any single component fails, together with all consequential failures, and even if one other item is out for maintenance. The failure is assumed in addition to the initiating event. The SFC is mandatory for category A functions.

Why four channels and not three? Three channels with 2-out-of-3 (2oo3) voting tolerate one failure. But when one channel is in test or repair, only two remain, and a further failure either blocks the trip or causes a spurious one. With four channels and 2-out-of-4 (2oo4) voting you can bypass one channel (the logic becomes 2oo3) and still tolerate a single failure. This is the N+2 idea: two channels more than the minimum, one for the single failure and one for maintenance. It is why most modern protection systems have four divisions.

RPS example: primary pressure is measured by four transmitters, one per division; a trip needs at least two channels below the setpoint. One transmitter can fail and one channel can be in test, and the trip still works.

Independence: separation, isolation, communication

Redundancy is worthless if one event can destroy all channels together. Independence protects the redundancy. It has three faces.

  • Physical separation. The divisions sit in different rooms or fire compartments with separate cable routes, so a fire or flood takes out at most one division.
  • Electrical isolation. Each division has its own battery-backed supply, and signals crossing a division boundary pass through isolation devices (optical couplers, isolation amplifiers, fibre optics), so a short circuit or over-voltage cannot propagate. RPS example: partial trip signals exchanged for voting travel on fibre-optic links.
  • Communication independence. Data exchange must not become a path for failures, including software failures or message floods. Safety systems use point-to-point links with fixed cyclic data; the receiving processor never waits for a message and, if data are missing, uses a defined substitute with a validity flag. Data to the operational I&C leave through a one-way gateway.

Independence also applies between defence in depth levels: the protection system (level 3) must be independent from the control systems (level 2) that may have caused the disturbance.

Diversity

Diversity means achieving the same goal with different means, so that one common cause cannot defeat all of them. It is the defence against common cause failure (CCF), which gets its own hour (Hour 5). RPS example: a loss of coolant accident is detected both by low primary pressure and by high containment pressure, two different parameters from different sensors (functional and signal diversity). A diverse actuation system on a different platform can trip the reactor if the main system is lost (platform diversity).

Fail-safe design and determinism

Fail-safe means that the most likely failures drive the system toward the safe state. The classic implementation is de-energise-to-trip: the trip breakers are held closed by energised coils and the control rods are held up by energised magnets. Loss of power, a broken wire or a dead processor removes the energy, the breakers open and the rods fall by gravity. Nothing has to work for the trip to happen. The price is a higher chance of a spurious trip, which is why voting and reliable power supplies are needed.

Determinism means that behaviour and timing are predictable. A deterministic processor runs the same sequence every cycle: read inputs, compute, write outputs, with a fixed cycle time (for example about 50 ms), no interrupts, no dynamic memory and no dependence on data content. The response time can be calculated, not only measured. RPS example: the time from the setpoint crossing to the breaker command is bounded, say under 200 ms, in every case, shown by analysis plus test.

Simplicity, testability, qualification

Simplicity: a safety system contains only what its safety functions need. Fewer functions mean fewer failure modes and easier verification; operational conveniences belong in the operational I&C. RPS example: the application consists of fixed function blocks (comparison, timer, voting) connected in diagrams, with no general-purpose operating system.

Testability: every part must be testable during operation without losing the function. This means signal injection points, bypass switches indicated to the operator, and overlapping tests covering the whole chain from sensor to actuator. Continuous self-monitoring in digital systems (memory checks, watchdogs, range checks) detects many failures within seconds and reduces manual testing. RPS example: one channel goes into test bypass, the logic degrades to 2oo3, a test signal is injected, the partial trip is observed, the channel is returned to service, and the reactor never trips.

Qualification: proof by type tests and analysis that the equipment works where it is needed. Three families: environmental (temperature, humidity, radiation, ageing; equipment inside containment must survive accident conditions), seismic (shaker table tests with the plant's design earthquake, cabinet functioning throughout) and electromagnetic compatibility (EMC: immunity to disturbances, limited emission). RPS example: a protection cabinet is shaken while running its application and the trip outputs are monitored.

Prevention of spurious actuation and defence against CCF

A protection system must act when needed and must not act otherwise. A spurious actuation (unwanted trip or injection) is a transient for the plant, a thermal shock for components and roughly a day of lost generation. Prevention: majority voting (2oo4 rather than 1oo4), signal validation (plausibility and rate-of-change checks), short delays where the safety analysis allows, reliable power supplies. Defence against common cause failure combines the principles: quality and simplicity to avoid faults, diversity and a diverse backup to tolerate them. The table summarises.

PrincipleIn one sentenceRPS example
RedundancyMore channels than the minimumFour pressure transmitters, 2oo4
Single failure criterionSurvives any single failure plus maintenanceN+2: four divisions
IndependenceOne event cannot hit several channelsSeparate rooms, batteries, fibre links
DiversityDifferent means for the same goalLow pressure and high containment pressure
Fail-safeLikely failures lead to the safe stateDe-energise-to-trip breakers
DeterminismPredictable behaviour and timingFixed cycle, no interrupts
SimplicityOnly what safety needsFunction blocks, no general OS
TestabilityTestable at powerChannel test bypass, 2oo3 during test
QualificationProven for environment, earthquake, EMCShaker table test of the cabinet
Prevention of spurious actuationAct only when neededMajority voting, signal validation
Defence against CCFAvoid and tolerate common faultsDiverse actuation system

Common mistake

Saying "redundancy protects against common cause failure". It does not. Four identical channels with the same software fault fail together. Redundancy covers random, independent failures; only diversity and quality cover common cause failure.

Tip

Learn the principles in pairs that pull in opposite directions: 1oo4 voting maximises safety but increases spurious trips; 2oo4 balances them. Fail-safe design increases spurious trips; voting reduces them. Naming a trade-off shows engineering judgement.

Interview angle

"Why do protection systems have four channels?" is a favourite. Answer with the single failure criterion plus maintenance: one channel in test, one failed, two left, trip still possible without a spurious trip. Then add that four identical channels still share a common cause failure risk, handled by diversity. That second sentence separates a good candidate from an average one.

Key terms

Redundancy
Providing more channels or components than the minimum needed, so that a single failure does not prevent the function.
Single failure criterion (SFC)
The requirement that a safety group performs its function despite any single failure and its consequences, with one item possibly in maintenance.
N+2
Design with two channels more than the minimum: one spare for a failure, one for maintenance; the reason for four divisions with 2oo4 voting.
Independence
Physical separation, electrical isolation and communication independence, so that one event or failure cannot affect several redundant channels.
Diversity
Using different means (parameters, equipment, design, people) to achieve the same safety goal, as a defence against common cause failure.
Fail-safe (de-energise-to-trip)
A design in which loss of power or signal drives the system to the safe state, for example trip breakers that open when their coils lose power.
Determinism
Predictable behaviour and bounded response time, achieved by strictly cyclic processing without interrupts or dynamic memory.
Qualification
Demonstration by type tests and analysis that equipment performs in its service conditions: environmental, seismic and EMC.
Spurious actuation
An unwanted protective action, such as a trip without a real demand; a safety and economic concern.

Say it in English

  • "We use four divisions with two-out-of-four voting so that one channel can be in maintenance and one can fail, and the system still meets the single failure criterion."
  • "Independence has three faces for me: physical separation, electrical isolation and communication independence."
  • "The trip breakers are de-energise-to-trip, so a loss of power opens them; nothing has to work for the reactor to trip."
  • "Determinism means I can calculate the worst-case response time, because the processor runs a fixed cycle with no interrupts."
  • "Redundancy protects against random failures, but only diversity protects against a common cause failure."
  • "Every channel must be testable at power, so we provide a test bypass that degrades the voting to two-out-of-three."

Check yourself

Why do modern protection systems typically use four redundant divisions instead of three?

This is the N+2 idea: the single failure criterion must be met even with one channel out for maintenance, which 2oo4 (degrading to 2oo3) provides and 2oo3 does not.

What is meant by de-energise-to-trip?

In a fail-safe design, breakers and rod holding magnets are energised in normal operation; removing the energy opens the breakers and lets the rods fall by gravity.

Which of the following is an example of communication independence?

Communication independence prevents a data path from becoming a failure path. Separate rooms are physical separation, own batteries are electrical isolation, and two sensor technologies are diversity.

Against which type of failure does redundancy alone NOT protect?

Identical redundant channels share any common design or software fault, so redundancy does not help; diversity and quality are the defences against common cause failure.

What does determinism mean for a digital protection system?

Deterministic processing means the worst-case response time can be calculated and demonstrated, which is essential for a system whose timing is part of the safety analysis.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

What is the single failure criterion and how do you meet it?

The single failure criterion says that a safety group must still perform its function if any single component fails, including the failures that follow from it, and even if another item is out of service for maintenance. The failure is assumed on top of the initiating event. We meet it with redundancy and independence. For a protection system that typically means four independent divisions with two-out-of-four voting. One channel can be in test, so the logic becomes two-out-of-three, and one more channel can fail, and we can still trip without a spurious actuation. Independence between the divisions makes sure one event does not count as several failures.

What does independence mean in a safety I&C design?

Independence means that one event or one failure cannot affect more than one redundant channel, and that a lower-class system cannot affect a higher-class one. I think of three aspects. Physical separation: divisions in separate rooms or fire compartments with separate cable routes. Electrical isolation: each division has its own battery-backed supply, and signals between divisions pass through optical links or isolation devices. Communication independence: processors exchange fixed cyclic data on point-to-point links, never wait for a message, and send data to the operational systems only through a one-way gateway. Independence is what makes redundancy meaningful.

Why is a protection system designed fail-safe, and what is the drawback?

Fail-safe design means that the most probable failures drive the plant toward the safe state. The classic example is de-energise-to-trip: the trip breakers are held closed by energised coils and the rods are held by energised magnets, so a loss of power, a broken wire or a dead processor opens the breakers and the rods fall by gravity. Nothing has to work for the trip to happen. The drawback is that any loss of a channel looks like a partial trip, so the chance of a spurious trip increases. That is why we combine fail-safe design with majority voting and very reliable power supplies.

What does determinism mean and why is it important for safety software?

Determinism means that the system behaves the same way every time and that its response time is bounded and known. In a deterministic platform the processor runs a fixed cycle: read all inputs, compute the function diagrams, write all outputs, and repeat, for example every fifty milliseconds. There are no interrupts, no dynamic memory allocation and no behaviour that depends on data content or network traffic. This is important because the response time of the protection system is an input to the safety analysis. With a deterministic design I can calculate the worst case and then confirm it by test, instead of hoping that a measured average is representative.

How do you make a protection system testable without tripping the reactor?

Each channel has a test bypass that is indicated in the control room. When one channel is bypassed, the voting degrades from two-out-of-four to two-out-of-three, so the function is still available and a single failure is still tolerated. Then I can inject a test signal at the sensor input or at the processing stage and observe that the partial trip appears, without a real trip because the other channels are healthy. Tests are overlapping, so that the whole chain from sensor to breaker is covered when all test segments are combined. Digital platforms add continuous self-monitoring, which detects many failures within seconds and allows longer intervals between manual tests.

Day 2 · Hour 4

Reactor protection system and engineered safety features actuation: the complete chain

The reactor protection system is the single most important I&C system in the plant and the reference example in every interview. This hour walks the whole chain from the sensor to the trip breaker, including voting, bypasses, manual actions and testing, so that you can draw it on a whiteboard and defend every box.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Explain the difference between the reactor protection system (RPS) and the engineered safety features actuation system (ESFAS).
  • List eight typical trip parameters and say which event and which barrier each one addresses.
  • Describe 2-out-of-4 voting, its degradation to 2-out-of-3 during a test, and the role of priority modules.
  • Explain operational bypasses, permissives, maintenance bypasses and manual trip.
  • Walk through the chain sensor, acquisition, trip evaluation, voting, breaker, feedback, with typical response times.

Two systems, one purpose

The reactor protection system (RPS) monitors plant parameters and trips the reactor (drops the control rods) when a parameter leaves its safe range. The engineered safety features actuation system (ESFAS) uses the same kind of measurements to start the systems that mitigate an accident: safety injection pumps, emergency feedwater, containment isolation valves, emergency diesel generators. In many plants both run in the same four-division digital system and "protection system" covers both. The reactor trip is a single action. ESF actuation is many actions, each with its own logic and actuators, often in a timed sequence (start the diesel, then load the pumps in steps).

Trip parameters and what they protect

The safety analysis chooses the parameters so that at least one, usually two, detect every design basis event early enough. The table shows typical PWR parameters; lists and setpoints are plant-specific.

ParameterTrip onTypical event detectedProtects
Neutron flux (power range)HighUncontrolled reactivity increase, rod withdrawalFuel and cladding
Neutron flux rate (reactor period), start-up rangesShort periodReactivity accident at low powerFuel
Primary pressureLowLoss of coolant accident, steam line breakCladding (avoids boiling crisis)
Primary pressureHighLoss of heat removal, overpressureCoolant pressure boundary
Pressuriser levelHigh or lowOverfilling, or a leakPressure boundary, cladding
Steam generator levelLowLoss of feedwaterHeat removal from the core
Primary coolant flow, main coolant pump statusLow flow, pumps trippedLoss of forced circulationCladding
Hot leg temperature or subcooling marginHigh temperature, low marginApproach to boiling in the coreCladding
Containment pressureHighPipe break inside containmentContainment (also starts ESF)

Typical ESF actuations: safety injection on low primary pressure or high containment pressure; emergency feedwater on low steam generator level; containment isolation on high containment pressure or high radiation; steam line isolation on low steam line pressure; emergency diesel start on loss of voltage on the safety buses.

Four channels and two-out-of-four voting

Each parameter is measured by four sensors, one per division. Each division acquires its own sensor, checks validity (range, rate of change, sensor fault) and compares with the setpoint. The result is a partial trip: a binary signal "my channel says trip". The partial trips are exchanged between divisions over isolated links and every division votes itself. A trip is generated when at least two of four channels agree.

Channel i (i = 1..4):
  P_i      = measured primary pressure in division i
  valid_i  = range OK AND sensor OK AND communication OK
  PT_i     = (P_i < P_setpoint) AND valid_i      -- partial trip

Voting in each division (normal state):
  TRIP = at least 2 of {PT_1, PT_2, PT_3, PT_4}  -- 2oo4

Channel 3 in test or maintenance bypass:
  TRIP = at least 2 of {PT_1, PT_2, PT_4}        -- 2oo3

Channel 3 with a detected fault, project choice:
  (a) treat PT_3 as TRUE   -- fail-safe, becomes 1oo3 for the rest
  (b) exclude channel 3    -- becomes 2oo3

Two-out-of-four is the compromise between safety and availability: a single failed channel can neither block the trip nor cause a spurious one. How a detected channel fault is treated (option a or b) is a project decision reviewed by the regulator; it affects both the probability of failure on demand and the spurious trip rate.

From the vote to the actuator

For the reactor trip, each division's vote drives the reactor trip breakers (or the power supply of the rod drives). They are de-energise-to-trip: the signal removes power from the breaker coil, the breaker opens, the rod drives lose power and the rods fall by gravity. The breaker arrangement is redundant, so one breaker failing to open does not prevent the trip.

For ESF actuation, the voted signal goes to an actuation logic that translates "safety injection required" into commands for each actuator: start pump A, open valve B, close valve C, with time steps where needed. Each actuator has a priority and actuator control module, a simple, usually hardwired device near the switchgear. It receives commands from the safety automation (highest priority), manual safety commands, and the operational I&C (lowest priority). It makes sure the protection command always wins, and it handles actuator protection (motor overload, torque limit) and feedback. Position or running signals are returned and displayed, so the operator can confirm the demanded state.

Note

The priority module is where safety I&C and operational I&C legitimately meet. Because it is simple and hardwired, it can be qualified as class 1 and still accept commands from a lower-class system without violating independence: the lower-class command can never override a safety command.

Manual trip, bypasses and permissives

A manual trip is always provided: control room push-buttons acting directly on the trip breakers, independent of the digital processing, so the operator can trip even if the automatic system has failed completely. Manual system-level actuation ("start safety injection") is also provided.

A maintenance bypass takes one channel out of the vote for testing or repair; it is limited to one channel at a time per parameter, it is indicated in the control room, and the voting automatically becomes 2oo3.

An operational bypass blocks a trip in a plant state in which the trip is not needed and would make normal operation impossible. Example: the low primary pressure trip must be blocked during start-up and cooldown, when pressure is legitimately below the setpoint. The block is allowed only when a permissive condition is true (for example "reactor power below about 10 %"), and the trip is automatically reinstated when the permissive disappears. Another example is the low-power bypass of pump-related trips: at very low power natural circulation cools the core, so a pump trip needs no reactor trip. Permissives are safety functions themselves and use the same redundancy.

Common mistake

Confusing the two kinds of bypass. A maintenance bypass removes one channel from the vote for a test. An operational bypass removes a whole trip function in a defined plant state, under a permissive, and must come back automatically. Both must be indicated to the operator.

Periodic testing

Because a protection system normally does nothing, failures could stay hidden. Periodic tests find them: a channel check (comparing the four indications, daily), a channel functional test (injecting a signal, checking the partial trip, monthly to quarterly), a calibration (refuelling outage), a logic test of voting and actuation, an actuator test (stroking valves, starting pumps) and a response time test. The tests overlap to cover the complete chain. Continuous self-monitoring in digital platforms detects many hardware failures within seconds, which justifies longer manual test intervals.

The complete chain and its timing

Typical numbers: a pressure transmitter responds within about 0.1 to 0.5 s; a digital system with a cycle time of about 50 ms needs two or three cycles (acquisition, evaluation, voting, output), so about 100 to 200 ms, to produce the breaker command; the breaker opens in a few tens of milliseconds; the rods then need seconds to insert (longer in the VVER-440, whose control assemblies are long and heavy). The safety analysis assumes a total delay from setpoint crossing to rod motion, often of the order of one second, and the design must show the real chain is faster. Only the I&C part, tens to hundreds of milliseconds, is under the I&C engineer's control.

Interview angle

"Describe how a reactor trip works" is best answered as a walk along the chain: four sensors, acquisition and validity check per channel, comparison with the setpoint, exchange of partial trips, 2oo4 vote in every division, de-energised breakers, rods fall by gravity, feedback to the operator. Add one sentence on timing and one on testing, and you have covered what a design engineer is expected to know.

Key terms

Reactor protection system (RPS)
The safety system that detects unsafe plant conditions and automatically trips the reactor by dropping the control rods.
ESFAS
Engineered safety features actuation system: the logic that starts mitigating systems such as safety injection, emergency feedwater and containment isolation.
Trip parameter and setpoint
A measured variable monitored by the RPS and the value at which a partial trip is generated, chosen with margin by the safety analysis.
Partial trip
The trip decision of one channel, before voting with the other channels.
2-out-of-4 (2oo4) voting
A trip is initiated when at least two of four redundant channels demand it; it degrades to 2oo3 when one channel is bypassed.
Reactor trip breaker
A de-energise-to-trip breaker that removes power from the control rod drives so the rods fall by gravity.
Priority and actuator control module
A simple device at each actuator that arbitrates commands from safety, manual and operational sources, with safety commands always winning.
Permissive
A plant condition that allows a trip to be bypassed in a defined state (for example low power) and reinstates it automatically when the state ends.
Maintenance bypass
Removal of one channel from the vote for testing or repair, indicated in the control room.
Response time
The time from the parameter crossing the setpoint to the protective action; the I&C part is typically tens to hundreds of milliseconds.

Say it in English

  • "Each division compares its own measurement with the setpoint and produces a partial trip; the four partial trips are then voted two-out-of-four in every division."
  • "When one channel is in maintenance bypass, the voting degrades to two-out-of-three, so the single failure criterion is still met."
  • "The trip breakers are de-energised by the vote, so the rod drives lose power and the rods fall by gravity."
  • "The priority module guarantees that a protection command always wins over an operational command at the actuator."
  • "The low pressure trip is bypassed during start-up under a low-power permissive and is automatically reinstated when power rises above the permissive setpoint."
  • "The I&C part of the response time is typically a few processing cycles, roughly one to two hundred milliseconds."

Check yourself

What happens to the voting logic of a four-channel protection parameter when one channel is placed in maintenance bypass?

With one channel removed, the remaining three channels vote 2oo3, which still tolerates one failure and still avoids a spurious trip from a single channel.

Which trip parameter is primarily intended to detect a loss of coolant accident?

A leak from the primary circuit lowers primary pressure quickly; high containment pressure is a second, diverse indication of the same event.

What is the main purpose of a priority and actuator control module?

The priority module sits at the actuator, receives commands from several sources and gives the safety automation the highest priority, while also handling actuator protection and feedback.

Why is the low primary pressure trip bypassed during plant start-up?

An operational bypass removes a trip in a plant state where it is not needed; the permissive condition (for example low power) must be true, and the trip returns automatically when the condition ends.

What is a typical order of magnitude for the I&C part of the reactor trip response time in a digital protection system?

With a cycle time of about 50 ms, acquisition, evaluation, voting and output take two or three cycles. The breaker and rod drop add more time, but those are outside the I&C.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

Can you walk me through how a reactor trip is generated?

Each parameter, say primary pressure, is measured by four transmitters, one per division. Each division acquires its own signal, checks validity and compares it with the setpoint, which gives a partial trip. The four partial trips are exchanged over isolated links and every division votes two-out-of-four. When the vote is true, the division de-energises its trip breaker. The breakers remove power from the rod drives and the rods fall by gravity. Breaker position and rod bottom signals are fed back to the operator. The I&C part takes a few processing cycles, so roughly one to two hundred milliseconds, and the whole chain is verified by periodic tests.

What is the difference between the reactor protection system and ESFAS?

The reactor protection system has one output, the reactor trip, which stops the chain reaction. ESFAS, the engineered safety features actuation system, starts the systems that mitigate an accident: safety injection, emergency feedwater, containment isolation, steam line isolation, the emergency diesels. Both use the same measurements and the same four-division, two-out-of-four architecture, and in modern plants they often run on the same platform. The difference is in the output side: ESFAS has many actuators, each with a priority module, and often timed sequences, for example starting a diesel and then loading pumps step by step.

Why do we use two-out-of-four voting rather than one-out-of-four?

One-out-of-four would be the safest in terms of never missing a trip, but any single failed channel would trip the reactor, so the spurious trip rate would be unacceptable. Two-out-of-four needs two independent channels to agree, so a single sensor fault or a single processor failure cannot cause a spurious trip, and at the same time a single failed channel cannot prevent the trip, because three healthy channels remain and two of them are enough. It also allows one channel to be in test while the system still tolerates one more failure. It is the standard compromise between safety and availability.

What is a permissive and can you give an example?

A permissive is a plant condition that allows a trip to be bypassed in a state where the trip is not needed and would prevent normal operation. A classic example is the low primary pressure trip: during start-up and cooldown the pressure is legitimately below the setpoint, so the trip is blocked while reactor power is below a low-power permissive, for example about ten percent. As soon as power rises above that value, the trip is reinstated automatically; the operator cannot forget to re-enable it. Permissives are safety functions in their own right, so they use the same redundant measurements and voting as the trips they control.

How is the protection system tested during operation?

Testing is done channel by channel so that the function is never lost. A daily channel check compares the four redundant indications. A functional test, typically monthly or quarterly, puts one channel in maintenance bypass, which degrades the voting to two-out-of-three, injects a test signal and verifies the partial trip. Calibration is done in the outage. Logic and actuator tests exercise the voting and the breakers or valves. The tests overlap so that the whole chain from sensor to actuator is covered. In a digital platform, continuous self-monitoring finds most hardware faults within seconds, which allows longer intervals for the manual tests.

Day 2 · Hour 5

Common cause failure and diversity: the weak point of redundancy and how we defend it

Common cause failure is the one failure type that four identical channels cannot stop, and it is the reason digital safety systems are licensed with so much care. This hour explains what it is, why software makes it harder, how diversity and a diverse actuation system defend against it, and why unwanted actuations matter too.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Define common cause failure (CCF) and distinguish hardware CCF from software CCF.
  • Explain why digital protection systems raise the software CCF question and how it is addressed.
  • Name five kinds of diversity and give an example of each.
  • Describe the purpose and typical scope of a diverse actuation system or hardwired backup, and what IEC 62340 asks for.
  • Explain why spurious actuation is both a safety and an economic concern.

What a common cause failure is

A common cause failure (CCF) is the failure of two or more components, channels or systems as the result of one shared cause, so that the failures occur together or within a short time. Redundancy assumes that failures are independent: the probability that two channels fail at the same time is then the product of two small numbers, which is very small. A common cause breaks this assumption. If all four pressure transmitters come from one faulty production batch, or all four processors run the same software with the same latent fault, the four channels are not really four; for that cause they behave like one. In reliability studies CCF often dominates the unavailability of highly redundant systems, because random failures have been designed out.

Hardware CCF, software CCF and why digital sharpens the question

Hardware causes are familiar from analogue systems: a design error in a module used in all channels, a bad component batch, a shared environment (temperature, flooding, fire, earthquake, electromagnetic interference), a shared power supply, and human causes such as calibrating all channels with the same wrong reference. They are handled by qualification, separation, independent supplies, staggered maintenance and component quality control.

Software CCF is different in two ways. First, software does not wear out and does not fail randomly; it fails systematically, when a latent fault meets the specific input condition that triggers it. Second, the four divisions of a digital protection system run identical application software and identical system software. The redundant divisions see nearly the same plant inputs at the same time. If the triggering condition arrives, all four fail together, in the same way, at the same moment. Analogue systems also had systematic faults (a wrong setpoint, a badly designed comparator), but software is more complex, so testing can never be exhaustive. This is why regulators ask for a demonstrated defence against software CCF for every digital category A system, even one with an excellent record. The answer has two parts: avoid the faults, and tolerate the ones that may remain.

Note

Avoidance is the first line: simple deterministic platform, small function block library, automatic code generation from reviewed diagrams, independent verification and validation, and millions of hours of operating experience with the same platform software. Tolerance (diversity) is the second line, because no one can prove that the software has zero faults.

Defence: diversity in five flavours

Diversity means doing the same job in different ways, so that a single cause does not defeat all of them. The usual classification distinguishes five kinds.

Kind of diversityMeaningExampleDefends against
FunctionalDifferent physical effects of the same event are usedLOCA detected by low primary pressure and by high containment pressureFailure of one function or one measurement principle
SignalDifferent sensors and parameters feed the logicPressure transmitter versus level transmitter versus temperature elementSensor type CCF, wrong calibration of one type
Equipment (platform)Different hardware and system softwareMain protection on a digital platform, backup on relays, FPGA or a different platformPlatform hardware and system software CCF
Design (software)Different algorithms, tools, teamsBackup logic specified and implemented by a separate team with other toolsApplication software CCF, specification errors
HumanAutomatic and manual means, different peopleOperator manual trip from the control room; independent checker in designAutomation CCF, individual mistakes

Functional and signal diversity are built into a well-designed protection system, since every design basis event must be detected by at least two parameters. Platform and design diversity usually need a separate system.

Diverse actuation system, hardwired backup and IEC 62340

A diverse actuation system (DAS) is a second, independent system that performs a reduced set of the most important protection functions on a different technology: for example reactor trip on high neutron flux and on low primary pressure, turbine trip, and start of emergency feedwater. It may be built from relays and hardwired logic, from field programmable gate arrays (FPGA), or from a different digital platform whose software has nothing in common with the main system. It is typically a lower class than the main protection system, because its design basis is a design extension condition: a postulated CCF of the main system combined with a transient. This is exactly the anticipated transient without scram (ATWS) case from Hour 1. The DAS must share nothing with the main system (processors, power, communication) and must not add many spurious actuations, which is why its scope is small. In some new-build projects the regulator asked for a hardwired backup system next to the digital protection system for the same reason.

The standard dedicated to this topic is IEC 62340, "Requirements for coping with common cause failure of computer-based systems". Its logic in three sentences: the designer must postulate a CCF of the computer-based system performing category A functions, in combination with each postulated initiating event; show by analysis that the plant still stays within acceptable limits, crediting only means that are independent and diverse from the failed system (other I&C systems at other defence in depth levels, operator actions, inherent plant behaviour); and where this cannot be shown, add diverse means. The standard also lists measures to reduce the probability of CCF in the first place (independence between divisions, deterministic design, protection against environmental and electromagnetic influences).

Tip

When you describe a defence against CCF, always name the postulated failure and the diverse means in the same sentence: "If the digital protection system fails completely on a loss of feedwater, the diverse actuation system trips the reactor on low steam generator level using hardwired logic." Cause, event, backup.

Spurious actuation: the other side of the coin

Most of this hour is about failing to act. The opposite failure, spurious actuation, is acting without a real demand: an unwanted trip, injection or containment isolation. It is a safety concern because every actuation is a transient: thermal cycling of vessel and pipes, pressure peaks, pressurised thermal shock if cold water enters a hot primary circuit, and demands on other safety systems. It is an economic concern because a trip costs roughly a day of lost generation plus restart work, of the order of a million euro for a large unit, and repeated trips damage the plant's standing with the regulator. A CCF can cause spurious actuation too: a software fault or an electromagnetic disturbance that makes all four channels trip at once. IEC 61513 therefore requires a spurious actuation analysis for the I&C architecture. The defences are majority voting, signal validation, EMC qualification, reliable power, and careful change control.

Three short cases

  1. Identical software, identical failure (Ariane 5, 1996). The first Ariane 5 had two inertial reference units, active and hot backup, running the same software. A numeric overflow in a conversion routine, triggered by the new launcher's higher horizontal velocity, stopped both within a fraction of a second. Lesson: identical redundant software is one channel for software faults.
  2. Network traffic as a common cause (Browns Ferry 3, 2006). Excessive traffic on a non-safety plant control network made the controllers of both recirculation pump drives stop responding, and the operators had to shut the unit down manually. Lesson: a shared communication network is a shared cause; safety systems need deterministic, point-to-point links and one-way gateways, and non-safety networks must be isolated from them.
  3. Shared location (Fukushima Daiichi, 2011). The redundant emergency diesel generators and switchgear of several units were in low-lying buildings and were flooded by the same tsunami. Lesson: redundancy without diversity of location and protection against the same external hazard is one failure waiting to happen; design extension conditions must include such hazards.

Common mistake

Believing that a long record of operation proves the absence of software faults. It proves that the input conditions seen so far did not trigger a fault. Operating experience lowers the estimated probability, and it is a strong argument, but regulators still require a diverse means for the CCF case.

Interview angle

Expect "How do you deal with common cause failure in a digital system?". Structure the answer in two parts: first avoidance (deterministic platform, simplicity, V&V, operating experience), then tolerance (functional and signal diversity in the protection system itself, plus a diverse actuation system or hardwired backup for the postulated full CCF). Mention IEC 62340 by name and purpose, not by clause.

Key terms

Common cause failure (CCF)
Failure of two or more redundant items from one shared cause, so that they fail together rather than independently.
Systematic failure
A failure that always occurs when a given condition is met, such as a software fault meeting its triggering input; it is not random and does not wear out.
Software CCF
Simultaneous failure of all redundant channels because they run identical software with the same latent fault and receive the same triggering inputs.
Diversity
Achieving the same goal by different means (functional, signal, equipment, design, human) so that one cause cannot defeat all of them.
Functional diversity
Detecting the same event through different physical effects, such as low primary pressure and high containment pressure for a LOCA.
Diverse actuation system (DAS)
An independent system on a different technology that performs a reduced set of protection functions if the main digital system suffers a CCF.
Hardwired backup
Relay or discrete-logic implementation of a few essential trips and actuations, with no software in common with the digital system.
IEC 62340
The IEC standard on coping with common cause failure of computer-based I&C systems: postulate the CCF, analyse the consequences, credit only diverse means.
Spurious actuation
A protective action without a real demand; a transient for the plant and a loss of generation.

Say it in English

  • "Redundancy assumes independent failures; a common cause failure breaks that assumption, so four identical channels behave like one channel for that cause."
  • "Software fails systematically, not randomly, so identical software in all four divisions is a common cause by definition."
  • "We defend in two steps: first we avoid faults with a deterministic platform and independent V&V, then we tolerate a postulated CCF with diverse means."
  • "A loss of coolant accident is detected both by low primary pressure and by high containment pressure; that is functional diversity inside the protection system itself."
  • "The diverse actuation system covers the anticipated transient without scram case; it is small on purpose so that it does not add spurious trips."

Check yourself

Which statement about common cause failure is correct?

CCF defeats redundancy because the redundant items share the cause; the defence is diversity and fault avoidance, not more identical channels.

Why does software raise a special CCF concern in digital protection systems?

Software failures are systematic: the same fault with the same triggering condition produces the same failure in every identical copy at the same moment.

Detecting a loss of coolant accident by low primary pressure and also by high containment pressure is an example of which kind of diversity?

Functional diversity uses different physical effects of the same event. Equipment diversity would be a different platform; human diversity would be manual action.

What is the typical design basis of a diverse actuation system?

The DAS exists for the design extension condition in which the main system is assumed lost; it performs a small set of essential trips and actuations on a different technology.

Why is spurious actuation considered a safety concern and not only an economic one?

Unwanted trips or injections cause thermal and pressure transients, possibly pressurised thermal shock, and create demands on systems that would not otherwise have been challenged.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

What is a common cause failure and why is it important for digital safety systems?

A common cause failure is when several redundant channels fail from the same cause at the same time, for example a shared design fault, a flood in a shared room or the same calibration error. Redundancy assumes the failures are independent, so a common cause defeats it. For digital systems the concern is software: all four divisions run identical software, and software fails systematically when a latent fault meets its triggering input. If that happens, all divisions fail at the same moment. That is why a digital protection system needs both fault avoidance, through a deterministic platform and independent verification, and a diverse means that can still protect the plant if the whole system is lost.

What kinds of diversity do you know?

I usually name five. Functional diversity uses different physical effects of the same event, for example detecting a loss of coolant by low primary pressure and by high containment pressure. Signal diversity uses different sensors and parameters. Equipment or platform diversity uses different hardware and system software, for example a hardwired or FPGA backup next to a digital system. Design diversity uses different algorithms, tools and teams. Human diversity combines automatic action with manual action by the operator and independent checking in design. Functional and signal diversity are inside the protection system itself; platform diversity usually needs a separate diverse actuation system.

What is a diverse actuation system and what should it contain?

A diverse actuation system is an independent system on a different technology that takes over a few essential protection functions if the main digital protection system suffers a common cause failure. Its design basis is a design extension condition, typically a transient combined with a complete failure of the trip, the ATWS case. It usually contains a reactor trip on one or two parameters such as high flux and low pressure, a turbine trip and the start of emergency feedwater. It must share nothing with the main system, no processors, power or communication. And it should stay small, because every added function is another source of spurious actuation.

Can you explain what IEC 62340 requires?

IEC 62340 is the standard on coping with common cause failure of computer-based I&C systems. In simple terms, it asks the designer to postulate a complete common cause failure of the computer-based system that performs category A functions, together with each postulated initiating event, and then to show by analysis that the plant still stays within acceptable limits. In that analysis you may only credit means that are independent and diverse from the failed system, for example other I&C systems at other defence in depth levels, operator actions or the inherent behaviour of the plant. Where the analysis does not pass, you add diverse means. It also lists measures to make CCF less likely in the first place.

Why do engineers worry about spurious actuation if it is on the safe side?

Because a spurious actuation is not free of risk. An unwanted trip or injection is a real transient: it cycles the vessel and piping thermally, it can create pressure peaks, and injecting cold water into a hot pressurised primary circuit is a pressurised thermal shock concern. It also places demands on other systems that would not otherwise have been challenged. Economically, a trip costs about a day of generation and a restart, which for a large unit is of the order of a million euro, and repeated trips harm the plant's standing with the regulator. So in the architecture we analyse spurious actuation explicitly and balance it against the failure-on-demand probability, for example with two-out-of-four voting.

Day 2 · Hour 6

Standards and regulations an I&C engineer must know, and how to talk about them

Nobody expects a junior engineer to recite clauses, but everybody expects you to know which standard is for what, who issues it, and how it reaches your desk. This hour gives you the map: IAEA, IEC, IEEE, WENRA and the Slovak regulator, plus a way of speaking about standards that sounds competent rather than memorised.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Describe the hierarchy from national law to company procedures and where IAEA and IEC documents fit.
  • State the purpose of IAEA SSR-2/1, SSG-39 and SSG-30 in one sentence each.
  • Name the main IEC nuclear I&C standards (61513, 61226, 60880, 62138, 60987, 62340, 60780) and what each is for.
  • Explain how IEC 61508 and its SIL levels relate to nuclear standards without confusing them.
  • Talk about IEEE 603, IEEE 7-4.3.2, WENRA and ÚJD SR in an interview with sensible, non-memorised sentences.

How standards reach your desk

Standards do not apply by themselves. They form a hierarchy, and knowing it helps you understand why a project document quotes a particular one.

  1. National law and the regulator. In Slovakia the Atomic Act (Act No. 541/2004 Coll.) and the decrees of the regulator ÚJD SR are legally binding. The regulator issues the licence and defines what evidence it wants.
  2. IAEA safety standards. Requirements (SSR series) and Safety Guides (SSG series) represent international consensus. They are not law, but most regulators, including ÚJD SR, align their rules with them, and the licensee usually commits to them in the licensing basis.
  3. Industry standards: IEC and IEEE. These say how to meet the requirements in engineering terms. They become binding when a contract, a licence condition or a regulator's guide refers to them. In Europe and in Framatome's projects the IEC family dominates; in the United States the IEEE family does.
  4. Company and project procedures. Framatome's internal processes translate the standards into templates, checklists and review steps that you actually use day to day.

So when a specification says "the system shall comply with IEC 61513", it is because the licensing basis or the contract made it applicable. Your job is to know what that implies for your design.

IAEA: the requirements and the I&C guide

SSR-2/1 (Rev. 1), "Safety of Nuclear Power Plants: Design", is the IAEA requirements document for plant design. It contains the general principles from Hour 1 (fundamental safety functions, defence in depth, design basis, single failure criterion) and a group of requirements specifically on I&C: provision of I&C systems, a protection system, reliability and testability, use of computer-based equipment, separation of protection and control, control rooms, emergency response facilities. SSG-39, "Design of Instrumentation and Control Systems for Nuclear Power Plants", is the Safety Guide that explains how to meet those requirements, including software, human-machine interface, and the overall I&C architecture. SSG-30 covers safety classification (Hour 2). If you remember one sentence: SSR-2/1 says what must be achieved, SSG-39 says how I&C typically achieves it.

The IEC nuclear I&C family

IEC Subcommittee 45A writes the nuclear I&C standards: one umbrella document and several specialised ones.

StandardTitle in plain wordsWhat it is forWhen you use it
IEC 61513I&C important to safety, general requirements for systemsUmbrella standard: overall I&C architecture, system lifecycle, requirements for class 1, 2, 3 systems, defence in depth, independence, spurious actuation analysisWriting a system requirements or architecture document
IEC 61226Classification of I&C functionsCategories A, B, C and the criteria to assign themAgreeing the category of a function with the plant designer
IEC 60880Software for category A functionsSoftware lifecycle, requirements, design, coding rules, V&V, tools, modification, for class 1 systemsSpecifying and verifying protection software
IEC 62138Software for category B and C functionsLighter software requirements graded for class 2 and 3Operational and limitation systems
IEC 60987Hardware design requirements for computer-based systemsHardware lifecycle, design, manufacturing, qualification of modulesPlatform and cabinet hardware design
IEC 62340Coping with common cause failurePostulating CCF of computer-based systems and proving diverse defenceDiversity and defence in depth analysis
IEC/IEEE 60780-323Qualification of electrical equipment important to safetyEnvironmental and accident qualification methods; seismic and EMC handled with related standardsQualification plans and test specifications
IEC 61508Functional safety of electrical and programmable systems (generic)Industrial framework with SIL 1 to 4; parent concept for sector standardsUnderstanding where the nuclear standards come from; not applied directly
IEEE 603Criteria for safety systems (US)Single failure, independence, qualification, testability, bypasses for safety systemsProjects for US-licensed plants
IEEE 7-4.3.2Digital computers in safety systems (US)Software quality, V&V, CCF, cyber security for digital safety systemsDigital upgrades under US rules

The relationship between them is simple to remember: 61513 is the umbrella and defines the lifecycle at architecture and system level; 61226 tells you the category; the category selects 60880 or 62138 for the software; 60987 covers the hardware; 62340 covers CCF; 60780 covers qualification. In a project you rarely open all of them, but you must know which one answers which question.

IEC 61508 and SIL: related, but not the nuclear standard

IEC 61508 is the generic functional safety standard for electrical, electronic and programmable electronic systems in all industries. It defines four safety integrity levels (SIL 1 to 4), each with a target range of probability of failure on demand: for low-demand systems roughly 10⁻² to 10⁻¹ for SIL 1, 10⁻³ to 10⁻² for SIL 2, 10⁻⁴ to 10⁻³ for SIL 3 and 10⁻⁵ to 10⁻⁴ for SIL 4, plus process requirements that increase with the level. Process industry and machinery use it directly or through daughter standards such as IEC 61511. The nuclear sector does not use SIL. IEC 61513 was written to be consistent with the framework of IEC 61508 (lifecycle, structured requirements), but nuclear classification is deterministic: the category comes from the role of the function in the safety analysis, not from a probability target, and class 1 requirements (single failure criterion, qualification, CCF defence) go beyond what a SIL number expresses. If someone asks "what SIL is the reactor protection system?", the right answer is that nuclear uses categories and classes; a class 1 system is comparable in rigour to the highest SIL levels, but the two schemes are not formally mapped.

Common mistake

Describing a nuclear protection system as "SIL 4" or calling IEC 61508 the nuclear safety standard. Interviewers hear this often from candidates with a process-industry background. Show that you know the difference and why: deterministic classification plus specific nuclear requirements.

US standards, WENRA and the Slovak regulator

IEEE 603 ("Standard Criteria for Safety Systems for Nuclear Power Generating Stations") is the US counterpart of the system-level requirements in IEC 61513 and SSR-2/1: single failure criterion, independence, qualification, bypasses, testability, information display. IEEE 7-4.3.2 adds the criteria for digital computers in those safety systems: software quality, verification and validation, common cause failure and security. The US Nuclear Regulatory Commission endorses specific editions, so in a US project the edition matters. Related IEEE standards cover independence (384), periodic testing (338), the single failure criterion (379) and qualification (323).

WENRA, the Western European Nuclear Regulators Association, is a club of European regulators that publishes harmonised Safety Reference Levels for existing reactors (updated after Fukushima) and safety objectives for new reactors. Members, including ÚJD SR, commit to transposing the reference levels into national rules. For I&C they matter mostly through defence in depth and design extension requirements that flow into national regulation.

ÚJD SR (Úrad jadrového dozoru Slovenskej republiky, the Nuclear Regulatory Authority of the Slovak Republic) is the national regulator. It licenses the Bohunice and Mochovce units, approves safety documentation and modifications, inspects, and issues decrees under the Atomic Act that set nuclear safety and quality management requirements. In a Slovak project, Framatome's design documentation ends up as part of the licensee's submission to ÚJD SR, so it must be traceable to the regulator's requirements and to the standards named in the licensing basis.

Talking about standards in an interview

Use a three-part sentence for any standard: who issues it, what it covers, what it means for my work. For example: "IEC 60880 is the IEC standard for software of category A functions; it defines the software lifecycle and verification requirements; for me it means that every function diagram I design must be traceable to a requirement and independently verified." Never claim clause knowledge you do not have. "I know the purpose and structure of the standard, and I would consult it for the detailed requirements" is an honest and respected answer. Finally, show that you understand the hierarchy: standards apply because the licensing basis or the contract makes them applicable, and the regulator's requirements come first.

Tip

Build a one-page map of the standards with the three-part sentence for each and reread it before the interview. Ten sentences are enough; nobody asks more than three or four.

Interview angle

Likely questions: "Which standards do you know for safety I&C?", "What is the difference between IEC 60880 and IEC 62138?", "What is the relation between IEC 61508 and IEC 61513?". For the last one: 61513 follows the structure of 61508 but uses deterministic categories instead of SIL and adds nuclear-specific requirements.

Key terms

Licensing basis
The set of laws, regulatory requirements, standards and commitments against which a plant is licensed; it decides which standards apply to a design.
IAEA SSR-2/1
The IAEA requirements document for nuclear power plant design, including requirements on protection systems and computer-based I&C.
IAEA SSG-39
The IAEA Safety Guide on the design of I&C systems, explaining how to meet the SSR-2/1 requirements.
IEC 61513
The umbrella IEC standard for nuclear I&C important to safety: architecture, lifecycle and system requirements for classes 1, 2, 3.
IEC 60880 / IEC 62138
Software standards for category A functions (60880) and for categories B and C (62138).
Safety integrity level (SIL)
The four-level scheme of IEC 61508 for generic functional safety; not used for nuclear classification.
IEEE 603 and IEEE 7-4.3.2
US standards for safety system criteria and for digital computers in safety systems.
WENRA
Western European Nuclear Regulators Association; publishes harmonised safety reference levels that members transpose into national rules.
ÚJD SR
Nuclear Regulatory Authority of the Slovak Republic; licenses and inspects Slovak plants under the Atomic Act and its decrees.

Say it in English

  • "IEC 61513 is the umbrella standard for nuclear I&C important to safety; it defines the architecture and the system lifecycle, and it points to IEC 60880 or IEC 62138 depending on the category of the function."
  • "IEC 61508 is the generic functional safety standard with SIL levels; the nuclear sector uses deterministic categories instead, so I would not describe a protection system by a SIL."
  • "IEEE 603 is the US counterpart of the system-level requirements; it covers the single failure criterion, independence, qualification and bypasses."
  • "Standards apply because the licensing basis or the contract makes them applicable; the regulator's requirements always come first."
  • "I know the purpose and structure of these standards, and I would consult the text for the detailed requirements of a specific design task."

Check yourself

Which standard is the umbrella document for nuclear I&C systems important to safety, covering architecture and lifecycle?

IEC 61513 is the general system standard; 60880 is software for category A, 62340 is common cause failure, and 61508 is the generic industrial standard.

A software requirement for a category B function would normally be taken from which standard?

IEC 62138 covers software for categories B and C. IEC 60880 is for category A, IEC 60987 is hardware, and IEEE 384 is a US independence standard.

How does IEC 61508 relate to nuclear safety I&C?

Nuclear classification is deterministic (categories A, B, C), and the nuclear standards add specific requirements that a SIL target does not express.

What does WENRA publish?

WENRA is the association of Western European regulators; it harmonises safety requirements. Licences in Slovakia are issued by ÚJD SR.

Which IAEA document is the Safety Guide dedicated to the design of I&C systems?

SSG-39 is the I&C design guide. SSR-2/1 is the plant design requirements document, SSG-30 covers classification and INSAG-4 defined safety culture.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

Which standards are relevant for safety I&C design, in your understanding?

At the top are the IAEA documents: SSR-2/1 gives the design requirements for the plant, including the protection system, and SSG-39 is the guide for I&C design. In engineering practice the IEC family is used: IEC 61513 is the umbrella standard for architecture and lifecycle, IEC 61226 classifies functions into categories A, B and C, IEC 60880 and 62138 cover software for category A and for B and C, IEC 60987 covers hardware, IEC 62340 covers common cause failure and IEC 60780 covers qualification. For US plants the IEEE 603 and 7-4.3.2 standards play the same role. I know their purpose and structure; for a specific task I would consult the text.

What is the difference between IEC 60880 and IEC 62138?

Both are software standards from the IEC nuclear committee, and the category of the function decides which one applies. IEC 60880 is for software performing category A functions, which means class 1 systems such as the reactor protection system. It is the strictest: a full software lifecycle, detailed requirements and design documentation, coding rules, independent verification and validation, and rules for tools and modifications. IEC 62138 is for category B and C functions, so class 2 and 3 systems such as limitation or monitoring systems. It follows the same logic but with graded, lighter requirements. In practice, if my function diagram belongs to a category A function, 60880 is my reference.

Is a reactor protection system SIL 4?

I would answer carefully, because the nuclear sector does not use SIL. IEC 61508 is the generic functional safety standard with four safety integrity levels defined by probability targets and process requirements. The nuclear standard IEC 61513 was written to be consistent with the structure of 61508, but nuclear classification is deterministic: the category of a function comes from its role in the safety analysis, not from a probability target. A class 1 protection system is comparable in rigour to the highest SIL levels, and it adds nuclear-specific requirements like the single failure criterion, qualification for accident conditions and defence against common cause failure. So the honest answer is that the two schemes are related but not formally mapped.

How do standards become applicable to a project?

Through the licensing basis and the contract. The national law and the regulator's requirements, in Slovakia the Atomic Act and the decrees of ÚJD SR, are binding. The licensee commits in its safety documentation to meet IAEA requirements and to apply specific IEC or IEEE standards, often with the edition stated. The contract between the licensee and the supplier then lists the applicable standards for the delivered systems. So when a system specification says "compliant with IEC 61513 and IEC 60880", it is because the licensing basis made them applicable. My task as a design engineer is to know what those standards imply for the architecture, the documentation and the verification, and to make compliance traceable.

What do you know about WENRA and the Slovak regulator?

WENRA is the Western European Nuclear Regulators Association. It publishes harmonised safety reference levels for existing reactors and safety objectives for new ones, and its members, including the Slovak regulator, commit to transposing them into national rules. ÚJD SR is the Nuclear Regulatory Authority of the Slovak Republic. It licenses and inspects the Bohunice and Mochovce units, approves safety documentation and modifications, and issues decrees under the Atomic Act on nuclear safety and quality management. For a supplier like Framatome this means that design documentation for a Slovak project becomes part of the licensee's submission, so it must be traceable to the regulator's requirements and to the standards in the licensing basis.

Day 2 · Hour 7

TELEPERM XS and the Framatome platform family: architecture, SPACE and a typical project

TELEPERM XS (TXS) is the platform you would work with every day at Framatome Controls. This hour explains what it is, how its architecture embodies the design principles from Hour 3, how an application is specified with the SPACE tool, and what a TXS project looks like from the point of view of a design engineer. Knowing this before the interview shows real interest in the job.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Give a short history of TELEPERM XS and name reference applications, including the Slovak VVER-440 units.
  • Describe the main architectural elements: processing, signal and communication modules, service unit, gateway, Qualified Display System.
  • Explain why strictly cyclic, interrupt-free processing with static memory is the heart of the platform's safety case.
  • Describe how an application is specified as function diagrams in SPACE and turned into code automatically.
  • Outline the steps of a TXS project from requirements to tests, and describe Framatome Controls in Bratislava in two sentences.

Where TELEPERM XS comes from

TELEPERM XS is a digital I&C platform designed specifically for safety functions of nuclear power plants. It was developed in the 1990s by Siemens KWU, the nuclear division of Siemens in Erlangen, Germany, as the safety-grade member of the TELEPERM family of automation systems. When Siemens and Framatome merged their nuclear businesses (Framatome ANP, later AREVA NP, and since 2018 Framatome again), the platform came with them, so today TXS is a Framatome product. It is qualified for category A functions (class 1 systems) and has been reviewed by regulators in several countries, including a generic review by the US regulator. It is installed in several dozen reactor units of different designs (German and French PWRs, VVERs, boiling water reactors, the EPR). Roughly twenty-five years of operation have produced a large body of operating experience, which is itself an argument in the safety case.

For Slovakia the connection is direct. The safety I&C of the Bohunice V2 units (3 and 4) was modernised with TXS in the early 2000s, Mochovce 1 and 2 received Siemens I&C with TXS for the safety systems at completion, and the new-build units Mochovce 3 and 4 use TXS for safety I&C; unit 3 started operation in 2023 and unit 4 is being completed. All are VVER-440 reactors, so TXS has a long VVER-440 history.

What makes it a safety platform

TXS is built around a few principles that make its behaviour predictable and its software verifiable.

  • Strictly cyclic processing. Each processing module runs the same loop with a fixed cycle time (typically about 50 ms, configurable): read all inputs, process the function diagrams in a fixed order, write all outputs, exchange data, run self-tests. The sequence never depends on the data.
  • No interrupts, no dynamic memory. Nothing can pre-empt the cycle, and all memory is allocated statically at generation time. Response time and memory use can therefore be calculated and demonstrated, which is what determinism (Hour 3) requires.
  • A dedicated runtime environment instead of a general-purpose operating system, with a small, qualified library of function blocks (comparators, timers, voting, arithmetic, limiters). The application only connects these blocks.
  • Self-monitoring. Hardware tests, watchdogs, integrity checks on memory and on every message exchanged between modules, and validity flags attached to signals. A detected fault marks the affected signal invalid and reports it, so the voting logic reacts as the project defined.
  • Communication designed for independence. Processors exchange data cyclically over point-to-point links, mostly on optical fibre between divisions; a receiver never waits for a sender, and missing data are flagged. Data leave the safety system through a gateway that allows information to flow only out, to the operational I&C and the plant computer.

The architecture in one picture

The signal modules acquire analogue inputs (for example 4 to 20 mA from transmitters), binary inputs (contacts), and drive binary and analogue outputs. The processing modules run the application. Communication modules link processors inside a division and between divisions. The service unit is an engineering computer used for diagnostics, for loading software and for authorised parameter changes; access is controlled, for example by a key switch, so that in normal operation it can only read. The Qualified Display System (QDS) provides safety-classified displays and manual commands in the control room on qualified hardware. The priority modules at the actuators are the hardwired devices described in Hour 4.

SPACE: specifying by drawing

The application software of a TXS system is not written by hand. It is specified graphically in the SPACE engineering tool (Specification And Coding Environment) as function diagrams: sheets on which function blocks from the qualified library are connected by signal lines, with parameters such as setpoints and time constants. Function diagrams are grouped per processing module. The same tool holds the hardware specification (cabinets, racks, module slots, terminal allocation) and the network specification. From the function diagrams the code generator produces the application code automatically, which is then compiled and loaded. Because the generator and the library are qualified, verification can concentrate on the diagrams, which are readable by process engineers, reviewers and the regulator. Supporting tools allow simulation of the diagrams against plant models and comparison of the generated code with the specification, which strengthens the verification argument required by IEC 60880. A design engineer therefore works mostly in function diagrams, not in a programming language.

Tip

If you have used any graphical automation language (PLC function block diagrams, Simulink, LabVIEW), say so. The way of thinking (signals, blocks, cycle time, parameters) transfers directly to function diagrams in SPACE.

The platform family: TXS Compact and SPINLINE

Framatome offers variants and sister platforms. TELEPERM XS Compact is a reduced form of the platform for smaller systems, with the same software principles in a more compact hardware arrangement; it suits applications with fewer signals or limited cabinet space. SPINLINE is a separate safety I&C platform of French origin that Framatome acquired with the civil nuclear I&C business of Rolls-Royce around 2020 to 2021; it is used in a number of plants, notably in France, and gives Framatome a second, diverse platform. For an interview it is enough to know they exist and that platform diversity (Hour 5) can be a reason to use two of them in one plant.

A TXS project as a design engineer sees it

  1. Requirements. The plant designer or the customer delivers functional requirements: which safety functions, their inputs, setpoints, voting, time constraints, interfaces to actuators and to the operational I&C, plus non-functional needs such as classification, response time, environmental conditions and test requirements.
  2. System design. Architecture (number of divisions, allocation of functions to processors), sizing (number of I/O, processor load, memory, cycle time), cabinet layout, power supply, communication topology, response time estimate, and the concept for monitoring and periodic testing. This is the basic design documentation the job advertisement mentions.
  3. Application specification. Function diagrams in SPACE, hardware and network specification, parameter lists. Every diagram is traceable to a requirement.
  4. Code generation and integration. Automatic generation, compilation, loading onto the target hardware in the test field.
  5. Verification and validation. Reviews of diagrams by an independent team, simulation, module and integration tests, a factory acceptance test (FAT) with simulated plant signals, then a site acceptance test (SAT) and commissioning tests with the real plant, and finally the periodic test procedures for the operator.
  6. Documentation and change control. Design documents, test specifications and reports, traceability matrices and a formal modification process throughout, because the documents become part of the licensing submission.

Note

Expect a large share of writing. Specifications, interface descriptions, test procedures and reports are the deliverables; the function diagrams are part of them. The advertisement's emphasis on documentation and English is not decoration.

Framatome Controls in Bratislava

Framatome Controls s.r.o., formerly AREVA NP Controls s.r.o., is Framatome's I&C engineering company in Bratislava. It employs roughly 80 to 90 engineers who perform software and hardware engineering of safety automation systems, mostly on TELEPERM XS, for Slovak and international projects, and who support commissioning on site. Typical work includes application engineering (function diagrams), hardware engineering (cabinets, signal allocation), test engineering (FAT, SAT, commissioning) and documentation for licensing, in an international team with the Framatome I&C centres in Germany and France and with the Slovak plants.

Interview angle

Be ready for "What do you know about TELEPERM XS?". A strong answer in four sentences: a Framatome digital safety platform from Siemens KWU, qualified for category A; strictly cyclic, deterministic processing with no interrupts and static memory; applications specified as function diagrams in SPACE with automatic code generation; references including the Slovak VVER-440 units at Bohunice and Mochovce. Then stop and let them ask more.

Key terms

TELEPERM XS (TXS)
Framatome's digital safety I&C platform, originally developed by Siemens KWU, qualified for category A functions and used in many reactor units worldwide.
Processing module
The computer board of TXS that runs the application in a strictly cyclic, interrupt-free way with statically allocated memory.
Signal module
Input and output boards that acquire analogue and binary signals from the plant and drive outputs to actuators and indications.
Service unit
The engineering computer for diagnostics, loading and authorised parameter changes, connected through a monitoring and service interface with controlled access.
Gateway
The interface that sends data one way from the safety system to the operational I&C and the plant computer, so nothing can flow back.
Qualified Display System (QDS)
Safety-classified displays and manual command facilities for the control room, built on qualified hardware.
SPACE
