The TXS engineering tool in which the application is specified as function diagrams and from which the code is generated automatically.
Function diagram
A graphical sheet connecting qualified function blocks (comparators, timers, voters) with signals and parameters; the specification of the application software.
FAT and SAT
Factory acceptance test with simulated signals in the test field, and site acceptance test with the real plant.

Say it in English

  • "TELEPERM XS is a digital safety I&C platform developed by Siemens KWU and now owned by Framatome; it is qualified for category A functions."
  • "The processors run a strictly cyclic program with no interrupts and static memory allocation, so the response time is deterministic and can be calculated."
  • "The application is specified as function diagrams in the SPACE tool and the code is generated automatically, which lets verification focus on the diagrams."
  • "Data leave the safety system through a one-way gateway, and the service unit has controlled access so that it can only read in normal operation."
  • "TXS has a long history with the Slovak VVER-440 units at Bohunice and Mochovce, which is one of the reasons I am interested in Framatome Controls."

Check yourself

Which statement correctly describes the processing of a TELEPERM XS processing module?

Deterministic, cyclic, interrupt-free processing with static memory is the basis of the platform's timing and verification arguments.

What is SPACE in the context of TELEPERM XS?

SPACE (Specification And Coding Environment) holds the function diagrams, the hardware and network specification, and drives the automatic code generation.

Why does TXS use a gateway between the safety system and the operational I&C?

The gateway implements communication independence between classes: information flows out, commands and disturbances cannot flow in.

Which Slovak reactor units use TELEPERM XS for their safety I&C?

Bohunice V2 was modernised with TXS, Mochovce 1 and 2 received it at completion, and Mochovce 3 and 4 use it in the new build.

In a TXS project, what normally comes right after the function diagrams are specified in SPACE?

The code is generated automatically from the diagrams; the engineering effort then goes into reviews, simulation, integration tests, FAT, SAT and commissioning.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

What do you know about TELEPERM XS?

TELEPERM XS is Framatome's digital safety I&C platform. It was developed in the 1990s by Siemens KWU and came to Framatome through the merger of the nuclear businesses. It is qualified for category A functions, so it is used for reactor protection and ESF actuation, and it is installed in several dozen reactor units, including the Slovak VVER-440 units at Bohunice V2 and Mochovce. Technically, its processors run a strictly cyclic, deterministic program with no interrupts and static memory, which makes the response time calculable. The application is specified as function diagrams in the SPACE tool and the code is generated automatically, which supports verification. It also has extensive self-monitoring.

Why is strictly cyclic processing important for a safety platform?

Because it makes the system deterministic. In every cycle the processor reads all inputs, processes the function diagrams in the same fixed order, writes all outputs and runs its self-tests, and the cycle time is fixed, typically tens of milliseconds. There are no interrupts that could change the order and no dynamic memory that could run out. As a result I can calculate the worst-case response time and the memory use at design time and then confirm them by test. For a protection system, whose response time is an assumption in the safety analysis, this is essential. It also makes the software easier to verify, because its behaviour does not depend on timing accidents.

How is the application software of a TXS system developed?

It is specified graphically, not programmed by hand. In the SPACE engineering tool the design engineer draws function diagrams: function blocks from a qualified library, such as comparators, timers and voters, connected by signals and parameterised with setpoints and time constants. The diagrams are grouped per processing module, and the same tool holds the hardware and network specification. A code generator then produces the application code automatically, which is compiled and loaded. Because the library and the generator are qualified, verification focuses on the diagrams, which can be reviewed by process engineers and the regulator, simulated against plant models and compared with the generated code. This matches the IEC 60880 requirements for category A software.

What would your work look like on a typical TXS project?

I would expect to start by collecting and clarifying the functional and non-functional requirements from the plant designer or the customer: which functions, inputs, setpoints, voting, response times, interfaces and classification. Then I would contribute to the system design: allocation of functions to divisions and processors, sizing of I/O and processor load, cabinet layout, communication, and the concept for monitoring and periodic testing. Next comes the application specification as function diagrams in SPACE, with traceability to the requirements, followed by automatic code generation and integration in the test field. Finally I would support verification: reviews, simulation, integration tests, the factory acceptance test and later the site acceptance test and commissioning, with documentation throughout.

What do you know about Framatome Controls in Bratislava?

Framatome Controls is Framatome's I&C engineering company in Bratislava, formerly AREVA NP Controls. As far as I know it has roughly eighty to ninety engineers who do software and hardware engineering of safety automation systems, mainly on TELEPERM XS, for Slovak and international projects, and who support commissioning on site. That means application engineering with function diagrams, hardware engineering of cabinets and signal allocation, test engineering with factory and site acceptance tests, and a lot of technical documentation in English. The connection to the Slovak VVER-440 units at Bohunice and Mochovce, and the international team with the Framatome centres in Germany and France, are what attract me to the position.

Day 2 · Hour 8

Reliability and failure analysis: failure modes, PFD, FMEA and fault trees

Design decisions about redundancy, self-diagnostics and test intervals are justified with a small set of reliability concepts. This hour gives you those concepts with numbers you can reproduce on paper, and it closes Day 2 with a review of the ten ideas you must be able to explain.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Classify failures as safe or dangerous and as detected or undetected, and explain why dangerous undetected failures matter most.
  • Use failure rate, MTBF and the approximation PFD ≈ λ·T/2, and show with numbers how the test interval changes the probability of failure on demand.
  • Build a small FMEA table for a pressure transmitter.
  • Read a simple fault tree with AND and OR gates.
  • Compare 1oo2, 2oo3 and 2oo4 voting in words, for both safety and spurious actuation.

Failure modes: safe or dangerous, detected or undetected

A protection system can fail in two directions. A safe failure pushes it toward the protective action: a channel that falsely signals "trip", a breaker that opens by itself. It costs availability, not safety. A dangerous failure prevents the action when needed: a transmitter frozen at its last value, a breaker that does not open. Independently, a failure is detected if the system reveals it quickly (self-diagnostics, range check, watchdog) or undetected if it stays hidden until a test or a real demand. The critical class is dangerous undetected (DU): it sits silently and appears only when the system is needed.

DetectedUndetected
SafeFault alarmed, channel treated as invalid or as tripHidden fault, spurious trip later
DangerousChannel cannot trip, but the fault is alarmed within secondsChannel cannot trip and nobody knows until a test or a real demand

Self-diagnostics move failures from the undetected to the detected column. Their diagnostic coverage is the fraction of dangerous failures detected; it is high for the electronics of digital platforms, lower for sensors and the process side (a blocked sensing line).

Failure rate, MTBF and the exponential model

For electronic equipment in its useful life, failures are assumed to occur at a constant failure rate λ in failures per hour. A quality electronic module has λ of the order of 10⁻⁶ per hour, one failure per million hours. The mean time between failures (MTBF) is 1/λ, so 10⁶ h ≈ 114 years. Careful: a module does not last 114 years; rather, in a population of 114 modules you expect about one failure per year. For small λt the probability of failure in time t is about λt: with λ = 10⁻⁶/h and one year (8760 h), about 0.9 %.

Probability of failure on demand and the test interval

The probability of failure on demand (PFD) is the probability that the function does not work when called. A component with dangerous undetected failure rate λ_DU, tested every T hours, may fail at any moment and stays failed until the test finds it. On average it is unavailable for half the interval, so

PFD_avg ≈ λ_DU · T / 2      (valid when λ_DU · T is much smaller than 1)

Example, one transmitter channel, λ_DU = 2 × 10⁻⁶ per hour:
  T = 3 months ≈ 2190 h  ->  PFD ≈ 2 × 10⁻⁶ × 2190 / 2 ≈ 2.2 × 10⁻³
  T = 12 months = 8760 h ->  PFD ≈ 8.8 × 10⁻³  (four times worse)
  90 % diagnostic coverage: λ_DU = 2 × 10⁻⁷ per hour, T = 2190 h
                         ->  PFD ≈ 2.2 × 10⁻⁴

Two lessons follow. The test interval is a design parameter with a direct effect on safety: halving T halves the undetected part of the PFD. And self-diagnostics are worth as much as more frequent testing: moving 90 % of failures into the detected class improved the PFD tenfold. The full unavailability also includes test bypass time and repair time after a detected failure (λ_DD × mean time to repair). For redundant architectures the PFD of the voted function is much smaller than for one channel, but common cause failure (Hour 5) sets a floor that redundancy cannot lower.

Common mistake

Quoting a PFD of 10⁻⁷ for a four-channel system by multiplying channel probabilities. A realistic analysis adds a common cause contribution (often a beta factor of a few per cent of the channel failure rate), which dominates the result.

FMEA: a small example for a pressure transmitter

A failure modes and effects analysis (FMEA) goes through every component, lists how it can fail, and records the effect on channel and system, the detection, and the measure needed. It is systematic and bottom-up. A shortened example for a 4 to 20 mA pressure transmitter in a 2oo4 low-pressure trip:

Failure modeTypical causeEffect on channelDetectionEffect on 2oo4 function
Output below 3.6 mA or above 21 mACable break, supply fault, electronicsOut of range, flagged invalidRange check, immediateChannel excluded or treated as trip per design; three channels remain
Output frozen at last valueElectronics faultChannel blind to pressure changes; looks healthyOnly by channel check against other channels or periodic testDangerous undetected for one channel; 2oo4 still trips with the other three
Slow driftAgeing, temperatureWrong value within range; setpoint shiftsDeviation alarm between channels, calibrationSmall change of trip margin
Blocked sensing lineProcess side, deposits, closed valveSlow or no responsePeriodic test, comparisonDangerous undetected; not visible to electronics

The FMEA shows where detection is weak (frozen output, blocked line) and therefore where channel checks, deviation alarms and periodic tests must be specified. It also feeds the fault tree and the spurious actuation analysis.

Fault trees: AND and OR gates

A fault tree is top-down: start from an undesired top event and ask what combinations of lower events cause it. An OR gate means any one input is enough (probabilities roughly add). An AND gate means all inputs must occur together (probabilities multiply if independent).

The tree shows what to work on: the AND branches are small because of redundancy, so the CCF branch and any single point of failure (a shared power supply, a single test switch) deserve the attention. Fault trees are also the tool of the plant's probabilistic safety assessment.

1oo2, 2oo3 and 2oo4 in words

  • 1oo2 (either channel trips): very low PFD, since both must fail dangerous (roughly (λT)² against λT/2 for one channel), but double the spurious rate. Maximum safety, poor availability.
  • 2oo2 (both must agree): very few spurious trips, but double the PFD, because either channel failing dangerous blocks the trip.
  • 2oo3: tolerates one dangerous and one safe failure; PFD roughly (λT)², and a spurious trip also needs two failures. A good balance, but testing one channel leaves only two.
  • 2oo4: the same balance as 2oo3, plus one channel can be bypassed for test with 2oo3 remaining. Three channels must fail dangerous to lose the function. This is the N+2 architecture of Hours 3 and 4.

Interview angle

A typical question is "How does the periodic test interval influence reliability?". Answer with PFD ≈ λ·T/2, one worked number, and the remark that self-diagnostics reduce λ_DU and allow longer intervals. Add that bypass time during the test is also unavailability, so there is an optimum.

Day 2 review: the ten ideas to carry into the interview

  1. Every safety function serves one of three fundamental functions: control reactivity, remove heat, confine radioactive material.
  2. Defence in depth has five independent levels; safety I&C acts mainly at level 3, with limitation at level 2 and diverse backup at level 4.
  3. Functions are classified (IEC 61226 categories A, B, C), systems take the class of their highest function (1, 2, 3), and the class drives redundancy, qualification, software and independence (graded approach).
  4. The single failure criterion plus maintenance gives the N+2 architecture: four divisions, 2oo4 voting, degrading to 2oo3 during a test.
  5. Independence has three faces (physical separation, electrical isolation, communication independence); data to lower classes flow one way.
  6. Fail-safe (de-energise-to-trip), determinism, simplicity, testability and qualification complete the design principles.
  7. The protection chain is sensor, acquisition, setpoint comparison, partial trips, vote, breaker, feedback; the I&C part takes about 100 to 200 ms.
  8. Common cause failure defeats redundancy; software CCF is handled by fault avoidance plus diversity and a diverse actuation system (IEC 62340).
  9. Standards map: IAEA SSR-2/1 and SSG-39; IEC 61513 as umbrella with 61226, 60880, 62138, 60987, 62340, 60780; IEC 61508 is related but not nuclear; IEEE 603 for US plants; ÚJD SR in Slovakia.
  10. TELEPERM XS is a deterministic cyclic platform specified by function diagrams in SPACE with automatic code generation; reliability is argued with PFD ≈ λT/2, FMEA and fault trees, where dangerous undetected failures and CCF are the real enemies.

Key terms

Dangerous undetected failure
A failure that prevents the protective action and is not revealed by self-diagnostics, so it stays hidden until a test or a real demand.
Diagnostic coverage
The fraction of dangerous failures that the system's self-monitoring detects quickly.
Failure rate λ
The expected number of failures per unit time, assumed constant during useful life; typically about 10⁻⁶ per hour for a quality electronic module.
MTBF
Mean time between failures, equal to 1/λ for a constant failure rate; a population average, not the life of one unit.
Probability of failure on demand (PFD)
The probability that a protection function does not work when demanded; for a tested component about λ_DU·T/2.
Test interval T
The time between periodic tests that reveal undetected failures; halving it halves the undetected contribution to PFD.
Unavailability
The fraction of time a function cannot perform, including undetected failures, repair time and test bypass time.
FMEA
Failure modes and effects analysis: a bottom-up table of how each component can fail, the effects, and how the failure is detected.
Fault tree
A top-down logic diagram from an undesired top event through OR and AND gates to basic failures.
Beta factor
A simple way to model common cause failure as a fraction of the channel failure rate that affects all channels together.

Say it in English

  • "The failures I worry about most are dangerous undetected ones, because they stay hidden until the system is really needed."
  • "For a tested component the average probability of failure on demand is about lambda times T over two, so the test interval is a real design parameter."
  • "An MTBF of a million hours does not mean the module lasts a hundred years; it means about one failure per year in a population of a hundred modules."
  • "Self-diagnostics move failures from undetected to detected, which lowers the PFD as much as testing more often would."
  • "In the fault tree the redundant branches are small; the common cause branch under the OR gate is what dominates."

Check yourself

Which failure class is the most critical for a protection system?

A dangerous undetected failure prevents the protective action and stays hidden until a test or a real demand, so it contributes directly to the probability of failure on demand.

A component has λ_DU = 2 × 10⁻⁶ per hour and is tested every 2190 hours. What is its approximate average PFD?

PFD ≈ λ_DU × T / 2 = 2 × 10⁻⁶ × 2190 / 2 ≈ 2.2 × 10⁻³. Doubling the interval would double it.

What does an MTBF of 10⁶ hours for a module mean?

MTBF = 1/λ is a population average. With λ = 10⁻⁶ per hour, the probability of failure in a year (8760 h) is about 0.9 %.

Which failure mode of a pressure transmitter is the hardest to detect by the electronics of the channel?

Out-of-range outputs are caught at once by range checks. A frozen output looks healthy and is only revealed by comparison with other channels or by a periodic test.

In a fault tree, what does an AND gate represent?

AND gates model redundancy: all redundant items must fail. OR gates model single points of failure, where any one input suffices.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

What kinds of failures do you distinguish in a safety system?

I distinguish two directions and two visibilities. A safe failure drives the system toward the protective action, for example a channel that falsely says trip; it costs availability but not safety. A dangerous failure prevents the action, for example a transmitter frozen at its last value. Then a failure is detected if self-diagnostics or an alarm reveal it quickly, and undetected if it stays hidden until a periodic test or a real demand. The combination that matters most is dangerous undetected, because it sits silently. Good design makes dangerous failures rare and detected, through qualified hardware, self-monitoring with high diagnostic coverage, and periodic tests for what the diagnostics cannot see.

How does the periodic test interval affect the reliability of a protection channel?

For a component with a dangerous undetected failure rate lambda, tested every T hours, the average probability of failure on demand is about lambda times T over two, because a failure can happen at any time in the interval and stays hidden until the test. With two times ten to the minus six per hour and a quarterly test of about 2190 hours, the PFD is about two times ten to the minus three; yearly testing makes it four times worse. Self-diagnostics reduce the undetected failure rate and therefore allow longer intervals. But the test itself puts a channel in bypass, which is also unavailability, so there is an optimum interval.

What is an FMEA and when would you use it?

A failure modes and effects analysis is a systematic, bottom-up study. For every component I list the ways it can fail, the likely cause, the effect on the channel and on the overall function, how the failure is detected and how fast, and what measure is needed. For a pressure transmitter, an open circuit is caught immediately by the range check, but a frozen output or a blocked sensing line looks healthy and is only found by channel comparison or a periodic test. So the FMEA tells me where to specify deviation alarms and tests. I would use it during system design to justify the monitoring and testing concept; its results feed the fault tree and the spurious actuation analysis.

Can you explain the difference between a fault tree and an FMEA?

They work in opposite directions. An FMEA starts from the components and goes up: how can this part fail, and what happens. A fault tree starts from an undesired top event, such as no reactor trip on demand, and goes down: what combinations of lower events cause it. OR gates mean any one input is enough, so probabilities roughly add; AND gates mean all inputs must occur, so probabilities multiply, which is how redundancy appears. The fault tree shows at a glance that the redundant branches are small and that common cause and single points of failure dominate. I use the FMEA to find failure modes and the fault tree to quantify and prioritise them.

Compare one-out-of-two, two-out-of-three and two-out-of-four voting.

One-out-of-two trips if either channel demands it, so it has a very low probability of failure on demand, because both channels would have to fail dangerous, but it doubles the spurious trip rate. Two-out-of-two is the mirror image: few spurious trips but a higher failure on demand. Two-out-of-three balances the two, tolerating one dangerous and one safe failure, but when one channel is in test only two remain. Two-out-of-four gives the same balance and additionally allows one channel to be bypassed for testing while two-out-of-three remains behind it, so the single failure criterion is still met during maintenance. That is why modern protection systems use four divisions with two-out-of-four voting.

Day 3 · Hour 1

The safety I&C lifecycle: from plant requirement to operating system

This hour shows the whole road from a plant-level safety requirement to a validated, licensed I&C system, and where a Design Engineer stands on that road. Interviewers ask about it because they want to know that you understand the process you will work inside, not only the technology.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Draw the V-model of the safety I&C lifecycle and name its phases in the right order
  • Name the main document produced in each phase and who checks it
  • Explain why verification and validation must be independent from the design team
  • Describe a realistic working day of a Design Engineer at an I&C supplier
  • Explain the sentence "the right leg of the V tests the left leg"

Why a lifecycle at all?

A safety I&C system is not simply "programmed". It is engineered through a controlled sequence of phases. Each phase takes documented inputs, produces documented outputs, and those outputs are checked before the next phase may start. The reason is practical: an error found in a specification costs an hour to fix; the same error found during site commissioning costs weeks and a formal change process. The lifecycle exists to find errors as early and as cheaply as possible.

The international standard IEC 61513 ("Nuclear power plants, Instrumentation and control important to safety, General requirements for systems") describes this lifecycle for the I&C system as a whole. Below it sit IEC 60880 (software for Category A functions), IEC 62138 (software for Category B and C functions) and IEC 60987 (hardware design). Today we use these standards as a map of the daily work.

The V-model

The lifecycle is usually drawn as a letter V. The left leg goes down from general to detailed: plant requirements, system requirements, system design, detailed design. The bottom is implementation: building the cabinets and generating the software. The right leg goes up again through integration, system validation and commissioning. The key idea is that every level on the right leg tests the corresponding level on the left leg. Integration tests check the detailed design, system validation checks the system requirements, and commissioning checks that the plant-level requirements are met in the real plant.

The phases and their documents

  1. Plant-level I&C requirements. The plant designer and the utility derive from the safety analysis which safety functions are needed, their safety category, setpoints and required response times. Output: functional requirement documents, logic diagrams, setpoint lists, the I/O list and the P&IDs (piping and instrumentation diagrams). For the supplier this is the customer input.
  2. System requirements specification. The supplier translates the customer input into one complete, consistent and verifiable list of requirements for one system, for example the reactor protection system. Output: the System Requirements Specification (SRS). Every test at the end will be traced back to it.
  3. System design (architecture). How many divisions, how many cabinets per division, which processing units, which networks, how I/O is allocated, which interfaces to other systems. Output: the System Design Description (SDD), interface specifications, preliminary sizing.
  4. Detailed design. Application software as function diagrams (FDs), hardware as cabinet layouts, wiring diagrams and terminal plans. Output: Software Design Description, Hardware Design Description, the complete set of FDs and drawings.
  5. Implementation. Code is generated from the function diagrams by a qualified tool; cabinets are manufactured and wired.
  6. Integration. Software is loaded into the real hardware and the units talk to each other for the first time.
  7. System validation. The complete system is tested against the SRS on a test bench, usually connected to a plant simulator that produces realistic process signals. The Factory Acceptance Test (FAT) with the customer belongs here. Output: validation test specification, validation report, FAT report.
  8. Installation and commissioning. Cabinets are installed in the plant, cables are connected, every loop is checked from sensor to actuator. The Site Acceptance Test (SAT) and integrated plant tests close this phase.
  9. Operation and maintenance. Periodic tests, surveillance and modifications. A modification re-enters the V at the level it touches and runs down and up again.
PhaseMain documentsTypical checker
System requirementsSystem Requirements Specification (SRS), traceability to customer inputSenior design engineer, independent V&V, customer
System designSystem Design Description (SDD), interface specifications, sizing calculationDesign review board, V&V
Detailed designFunction diagrams, hardware drawings, software and hardware design descriptionsSecond design engineer (four-eyes), V&V
Integration and validationTest specifications, test reports, anomaly reportsIndependent V&V team, customer witnesses FAT
CommissioningSAT procedures, loop check records, as-built documentsSite team, utility, regulator may witness

Reviews and the independence of V&V

No document in a safety project is used with only one signature. The usual chain is author, checker, approver. The checker is a colleague who did not write the document and reads it against its inputs (the "four-eyes principle"). Above this, a separate Verification and Validation (V&V) team exists. For Category A systems IEC 60880 requires that V&V is performed by people who are independent from the design team: a different group, ideally a different reporting line, writing their own test specifications directly from the requirements and not from the design. If the designer misunderstood a requirement, a tester who copies the designer's understanding will never find the error; an independent tester who reads only the requirement will.

Note

Verification asks "did we build it right?" at every step (does the SDD satisfy the SRS, does the function diagram satisfy the SDD). Validation asks "did we build the right thing?" once, at the end, against the original requirements. Hour 6 returns to this in detail.

Where the Design Engineer sits

The job title in the posting, "Design Engineer for Safety I&C", covers the left leg of the V. You collect customer requirements, write or update the SRS, propose the architecture and sizing in the SDD, write interface specifications, design function diagrams and sometimes hardware documents, and explain design intent to the test team. You do not write the V&V test specifications for your own work; that would break independence. You do answer V&V findings.

A realistic day

At 8:30 you open the customer's new input package: revision C of the logic diagrams and a revised I/O list. You run a comparison against revision B and find twelve changed signals and one new trip condition. You update the affected requirements in the SRS and mark each change with its reason. One new trip condition has no setpoint in the setpoint list. You do not guess. You write it into the Open Point List: "Setpoint for low condenser vacuum trip missing. Proposed interpretation: use the value from the plant safety analysis report, section 7.2, until confirmed. Owner: customer. Needed by: 15 November."

At 11:00 you review a colleague's function diagram for a containment isolation signal. You check it against the requirement text, the signal list and the design rules, and find a timer whose 500 ms delay has no documented justification. You write this in the review sheet as a comment; the author decides how to close it. At 14:00 you join a one-hour call with the customer to close three open points; you send the minutes the same day, because an oral agreement does not exist in a safety project. At 15:30 you answer a V&V finding: "SRS-0412 is not verifiable; 'fast response' is not measurable." You rewrite it: "The system shall generate the trip output within 300 ms after the input signal crosses the setpoint." At 16:30 you update the traceability matrix and check the documents into the configuration management system with a new revision index. That is the job.

Tip

Whenever you describe this process at the interview, use the names of documents, not generic words. "I would update the SRS and the traceability matrix" sounds like an engineer; "I would update the files" does not.

Common mistake

Candidates often say that testing happens "at the end". In the V-model, testing is planned at the beginning: the test specification for each level is written when the corresponding specification is written, long before any hardware exists.

Interview angle

A classic question is "Describe the lifecycle of a safety I&C system." Walk down the left leg naming the documents, across the bottom, and up the right leg naming the tests. Finish with one sentence on independent V&V. Two minutes, no more.

Key terms

Safety lifecycle
The ordered set of phases from requirements to decommissioning through which a safety system is specified, built, tested and maintained, with controlled documents between phases.
V-model
A picture of the lifecycle in which specification levels go down the left leg and the matching test levels go up the right leg.
IEC 61513
The top-level international standard for I&C systems important to safety in nuclear power plants; it defines the system lifecycle and refers to IEC 60880, IEC 62138 and IEC 60987.
System Requirements Specification (SRS)
The document that states, in verifiable sentences, everything the system must do and the conditions under which it must do it.
System Design Description (SDD)
The document that describes the architecture: divisions, cabinets, processing units, networks, I/O allocation and interfaces.
Function diagram (FD)
A graphical sheet of connected function blocks that specifies one piece of application software; in a safety platform it is also the source from which code is generated.
Four-eyes principle
Every document or design is checked by at least one qualified person who did not produce it.
Independent V&V
Verification and validation performed by a team that is organisationally separate from the designers and works from the requirements, not from the design.
Factory Acceptance Test (FAT)
The test of the complete system at the supplier's premises, witnessed by the customer, before shipment.
Open Point List
A controlled list of unresolved questions, each with a description, a proposed interpretation, an owner and a due date.

Say it in English

  • "The lifecycle follows IEC 61513: we go from plant requirements to the system requirements specification, then to the architecture and detailed design, and we test back up the right leg of the V."
  • "As a Design Engineer I work mainly on the left leg: requirements, system design, function diagrams and interface specifications."
  • "Every document goes through author, checker and approver, and independent V&V tests the system against the requirements, not against our design."
  • "When customer input is unclear, I record it in the open point list with a proposed interpretation and ask for written confirmation."
  • "A modification re-enters the lifecycle at the level it touches; it is never a quick edit."

Check yourself

In the V-model, which activity verifies the System Requirements Specification?

System validation is at the same height as the SRS on the opposite leg; it tests the complete system against the requirements.

Which standard describes the overall lifecycle of I&C systems important to safety in nuclear plants?

IEC 61513 is the system-level standard; IEC 60880 covers Category A software and IEC 60987 covers hardware.

Why must the V&V team be independent from the design team?

Independent testers derive test cases from the requirements, so they can catch an interpretation error that the designer would never notice.

A customer's setpoint list does not contain a value you need. What is the correct action?

Open points are tracked formally; a proposed interpretation lets work continue while the written answer is pending.

Which document is normally produced in the system design phase?

The SDD describes the architecture; function diagrams belong to detailed design and the SAT report to commissioning.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

Can you describe the lifecycle of a safety I&C system?

I would describe it as the V-model from IEC 61513. On the left leg we start with the plant-level requirements from the safety analysis, write the System Requirements Specification, then the System Design Description with the architecture, and then the detailed design: function diagrams for the software and drawings for the hardware. At the bottom we implement, meaning code generation and cabinet manufacturing. On the right leg we integrate and test: module tests, integration tests, system validation on a test bench with FAT, and finally installation, commissioning and SAT at the plant. Each test level verifies the specification level opposite to it, and verification and validation are done by an independent team.

What would a Design Engineer actually do in a typical week?

Most of my time would go into documents on the left leg of the V. I would read customer input such as logic diagrams, I/O lists and setpoint lists, and turn them into requirements in the SRS. I would work on the architecture and sizing in the System Design Description, design function diagrams and interface specifications, and keep the traceability matrix up to date. A part of every week is review work: checking a colleague's document against its inputs and writing comments. And I would answer findings from the V&V team and clarify open points with the customer, always in writing.

Why is independence of V&V so important in nuclear projects?

Because the biggest risk in a safety system is not a typing error but a misunderstanding of what the system should do. If the same people who designed the system also test it, they test their own interpretation and the misunderstanding survives. An independent V&V team derives its test cases from the requirements, not from our design, so a wrong interpretation shows up as a failed test. IEC 60880 requires this independence for Category A software, and in practice it also gives the regulator confidence, because the evidence comes from a team with no interest in saying that the design is fine.

How do you handle customer input that is incomplete or contradictory?

First I never guess silently. I write the issue into the open point list with a clear description, the documents and revisions involved, and a proposed interpretation so that work can continue. Then I raise it with the customer, usually in a technical query or a clarification meeting, and I ask for a written confirmation. When the answer arrives, I update the SRS, mark the change with its reason, and update the traceability so that the design and the tests follow. If the contradiction is between two customer documents, I quote both and let the customer decide which one is valid.

What happens when a requirement changes after the design is finished?

The change goes through the change management process, not through a quick edit. I would do an impact analysis: which requirements, function diagrams, hardware drawings, test cases and documents are affected, using the traceability matrix. After approval the change is implemented, and then every affected level of the V is re-verified: the modified function diagrams are reviewed and tested again, and the validation tests that trace to the changed requirement are repeated, plus a regression test to show that nothing else moved. Finally all documents get a new revision. In a safety system a small change is never small.

Day 3 · Hour 2

Requirements engineering: writing what the system shall do

Requirements are the raw material of the whole project: every design decision and every test traces back to one. This hour teaches you to read customer input, write good requirements in "shall" language and keep traceability. The job posting literally asks for "collecting functional and non-functional needs", so expect questions on it.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Distinguish functional from non-functional requirements and give three examples of each
  • Rewrite a vague requirement into an atomic, verifiable "shall" statement
  • Name the customer input documents and say what you take from each of them
  • Build a simple traceability matrix from requirement to design element to test case
  • Handle an unclear requirement with an open point and a proposed interpretation

What a requirement is, and what it is not

A requirement is a statement of something the system must do, or a property it must have, written so that a test or an analysis can show whether it is met. It is not a design. "The reactor protection system shall trip the reactor on low steam generator level" is a requirement. "The low-level trip shall be implemented with voting block type V2oo4 on processor board 3" is a design decision. Keeping the two apart matters: if you write design into the requirements, you can never change the design without re-opening the requirements with the customer. The System Requirements Specification (SRS) you met in Hour 1 should contain requirements only, each with a unique identifier such as SRS-RPS-0412.

Functional and non-functional requirements

Functional requirements say what the system does: which inputs, which logic, which outputs, under which conditions. Non-functional requirements say how well and under which circumstances it must do it. Beginners concentrate on the functional ones because they look like logic; experienced engineers know that most of the engineering effort, and most of the disputes with the customer, come from the non-functional ones.

TypeExample requirement (shortened)How it is verified
FunctionalGenerate the reactor trip when SG level is below 25 % of span in 2 of 4 channelsTest on test bench
PerformanceResponse time from input terminal to output terminal ≤ 300 msTest with timing measurement
AccuracyOverall I&C channel error ≤ ±1 % of span at reference conditionsAnalysis of module data plus calibration test
Availability / reliabilityProbability of failure on demand of the trip function below the value from the safety analysisReliability analysis (fault tree)
EnvironmentalOperate at 5 to 40 °C and up to 90 % relative humidity, non-condensingQualification test report, inspection
EMCWithstand the immunity test levels specified for the I&C roomsType test of cabinets
CybersecurityNo remote access path to any safety processing unitInspection of architecture and configuration
MaintainabilityReplace any I/O module while the other three divisions remain in operationDemonstration

Each requirement carries a verification method: Test, Analysis, Inspection or Demonstration. Deciding the method while writing the requirement is the fastest way to discover that it cannot be verified at all.

Qualities of a good requirement

  • Atomic. One statement, one thing to verify. The word "and" between two different demands is a warning sign.
  • Unambiguous. Only one reading is possible. Words such as "fast", "adequate", "appropriate", "user-friendly", "etc." and "as far as possible" are forbidden.
  • Verifiable. A number with a unit, a tolerance and a condition. "Within 300 ms" is verifiable; "quickly" is not.
  • Necessary. It traces to a customer input, a standard or a safety analysis. A requirement without a source is either missing a source or should be deleted.
  • Consistent. It does not contradict another requirement and uses the same terms and units as the rest of the document (not "SG level" in one place and "steam generator water level" in another).
  • Traceable. Unique ID, source reference, links to design and test.
  • Complete. It also says what happens in the unpleasant cases: invalid input, loss of power, a channel in test.

Use shall for a mandatory requirement, should for a recommendation and may for something permitted. Never mix "will", "must" and "shall" in one document, because reviewers will ask whether the difference means something.

Bad versus good: three rewrites

BAD   "The system should quickly trip the reactor if the SG level is too low."
GOOD  SRS-0412  The RPS shall generate the reactor trip signal when the
                narrow-range level of any steam generator is below 25 % of
                span in at least 2 of the 4 channels of that steam generator.
      SRS-0413  The response time from the level input crossing the setpoint
                to the trip output at the cabinet terminal shall be <= 300 ms.
      SRS-0414  The trip of SRS-0412 shall be automatically bypassed while
                reactor power is below 10 % of nominal power.

BAD   "All signals shall be processed with high accuracy and the HMI shall
       display them clearly."
GOOD  SRS-0520  The total error of each analog input channel from the cabinet
                terminal to the processed value shall be <= 0.2 % of span.
      SRS-0610  Each analog value shall be shown on the safety display with
                its KKS tag, engineering unit and validity status.

BAD   "The system shall be reliable."
GOOD  SRS-0701  The probability of failure on demand of the reactor trip
                function shall not exceed the value given in document
                [SAR, section 15.0.3]. Verification: reliability analysis.

Notice what happened each time: one sentence became two or three, every number got a unit and a reference point, and every "nice" word disappeared.

Common mistake

Writing the requirement in the passive voice without naming the subject: "The trip shall be generated." Generated by what? Always name the system or the function: "The RPS shall generate..." It is the only way to know who is responsible when the requirement is split over several systems.

Where requirements come from: the input documents

At an I&C supplier the customer input arrives as a package of documents. You need to know what each one is and what you extract from it.

  • P&ID (piping and instrumentation diagram). The process drawing: vessels, pipes, pumps, valves and every instrument with its tag number. From it you learn which measurements exist, where the sensors are and which valves and pumps you will actuate.
  • I/O list. One line per signal: tag, description, signal type (4 to 20 mA, RTD, 24 V DC binary, relay output), range and unit, source, destination, division, safety class. It drives sizing and I/O allocation.
  • Functional requirement documents. The safety functions in words, their category, the postulated events they protect against.
  • Logic diagrams. The plant designer's drawing of the logic: AND, OR, voting, timers, setpoints, interlocks. You translate them into function diagrams.
  • Setpoint lists. The numerical values: setpoint, hysteresis, time delay, tolerance, and the reference to the safety analysis that justifies them.
  • Interface specifications. What crosses the boundary to other systems: signal lists, protocols, isolation requirements, who owns the cable.
  • Site and environmental documents. Room layouts, ambient conditions, seismic level, EMC environment, power supply data.

Tip

The first thing to do with a new input package is a consistency check between documents: does every instrument on the P&ID appear in the I/O list, with the same tag and the same range as in the setpoint list? Most early open points are found this way.

Traceability

The traceability matrix links each requirement backward to its source and forward to the design elements that implement it and the test cases that verify it. It is a living table, kept in a requirements tool or a controlled spreadsheet, and it is checked at every review.

SourceRequirementDesign elementTest caseStatus
Logic diagram LD-RPS-017 rev CSRS-0412FD-RPS-SG-LVL-01, SDD section 5.3TC-VAL-0412-1, TC-VAL-0412-2Verified
Setpoint list SPL-003 rev BSRS-0413SDD section 4.2 (cycle time budget)TC-VAL-0413-1Open
Open point OP-031 (assumed)SRS-0414FD-RPS-SG-LVL-01TC-VAL-0414-1Awaiting confirmation

Two checks come out of the matrix. Forward coverage: every requirement has at least one design element and one test case. Backward coverage: every design element traces to at least one requirement, so nobody built something that the customer did not ask for.

Open points and unclear requirements

Unclear input is normal. The professional reaction has a fixed shape. First, write the issue down in the open point list with an ID and quote the source document and revision. Second, propose an interpretation and say why, choosing the conservative (safer) reading when in doubt. Third, state the impact if the interpretation turns out to be wrong. Fourth, send it to the customer through the formal channel, usually a technical query. Fifth, mark the affected requirement in the SRS as "assumption, OP-031". Sixth, when the written answer arrives, update the SRS and the traceability and close the point. An answer given by phone is a reminder to ask for the e-mail, nothing more.

Interview angle

Expect "What makes a good requirement?" and "Give me an example of a bad requirement and fix it." Have one rewrite ready, with a number and a unit in the good version. Then mention traceability without being asked; it shows you think about the whole V.

Key terms

Functional requirement
A statement of what the system must do: inputs, logic, outputs and the conditions under which it acts.
Non-functional requirement
A statement of how well or under which conditions the system must perform: response time, accuracy, availability, environment, EMC, cybersecurity, maintainability.
Shall statement
The standard form of a mandatory requirement: subject, "shall", one verifiable demand with a number, a unit and a condition.
Atomic requirement
A requirement that contains exactly one demand, so that one test can pass or fail it.
Verification method
The way a requirement is shown to be met: Test, Analysis, Inspection or Demonstration.
Traceability matrix
A table linking each requirement to its source, its design elements and its test cases, used to prove complete coverage in both directions.
P&ID
Piping and instrumentation diagram: the process drawing showing equipment, pipes, valves and all instruments with their tag numbers.
I/O list
The list of all input and output signals with their tag, type, range, source, destination and safety class.
Setpoint list
The customer document giving the numerical values of setpoints, hysteresis and time delays, each with a reference to the safety analysis.
Technical query
A formal written question to the customer about an unclear or missing input, tracked until a written answer closes it.

Say it in English

  • "A good requirement is atomic, unambiguous, verifiable, necessary, consistent and traceable, and it always has a number with a unit."
  • "Functional requirements describe what the system does; non-functional requirements describe response time, accuracy, availability, environment and maintainability."
  • "From the I/O list I take the signal types and counts for sizing, and from the setpoint list I take the values and their justification."
  • "Every requirement gets a verification method: test, analysis, inspection or demonstration."
  • "If a requirement is unclear, I raise a technical query, propose a conservative interpretation and wait for written confirmation."
  • "The traceability matrix shows that every requirement is implemented and tested, and that nothing was built without a requirement."

Check yourself

Which of these is a non-functional requirement?

Response time describes how well the system performs, not what it does; the other three are functional requirements.

What is wrong with "The system shall process all signals accurately and display them clearly"?

"Accurately" and "clearly" are not measurable, and the sentence combines two separate demands joined by "and".

From which input document do you primarily take the signal types and counts needed for sizing?

The I/O list has one line per signal with its type, range and division, which is exactly what I/O sizing needs.

What does backward coverage in the traceability matrix check?

Backward coverage prevents "gold plating": design elements that nobody asked for and that nobody will test.

The customer confirms an open point by phone. What do you do?

In a safety project only written, traceable confirmation counts; the phone call is just a prompt to request the e-mail or formal answer.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

What makes a good requirement?

A good requirement is atomic, so it contains exactly one demand that one test can pass or fail. It is unambiguous, which means there is only one way to read it, and verifiable, which means it has a number, a unit and a condition. It is necessary, so it traces to a customer input, a standard or the safety analysis. It is consistent with the rest of the specification in terms and units, and it is traceable through a unique identifier. I write it as a "shall" sentence with a named subject, for example "The RPS shall generate the trip output within 300 ms after the input crosses the setpoint", and I fix the verification method at the same time.

Can you give an example of a bad requirement and improve it?

A typical bad one is "The system should quickly trip the reactor if the steam generator level is too low." It has no number, "quickly" and "too low" are not measurable, and "should" makes it optional. I would split it into three requirements. First, the functional one: the RPS shall generate the reactor trip when the narrow-range level of any steam generator is below 25 percent of span in at least two of four channels. Second, the performance one: the response time from input to output terminal shall be at most 300 milliseconds. Third, the permissive condition, if the customer wants one: the trip shall be bypassed below 10 percent power. Each gets an ID and a verification method.

What is the difference between functional and non-functional requirements?

Functional requirements describe what the system does: which inputs it reads, which logic it applies and which outputs it generates under which conditions. A trip on low level or the closing of an isolation valve are functional. Non-functional requirements describe how well and under which circumstances it must do that: response time, accuracy, availability or probability of failure on demand, environmental conditions like temperature and humidity, EMC immunity, seismic withstand, cybersecurity constraints and maintainability, for example replacing a module without disturbing the other divisions. In my experience the non-functional requirements drive most of the architecture and most of the qualification effort.

Which input documents would you expect from the customer and what do you do with them?

I would expect the P&IDs to see which instruments and actuators exist and where; the I/O list with every signal, its type, range and division, which I use for sizing and I/O allocation; the functional requirement documents and logic diagrams, which I translate into system requirements and later into function diagrams; the setpoint list with values, hysteresis and delays and their justification; interface specifications for the boundaries to other systems; and the site documents with environmental, seismic and EMC conditions. My first step is a consistency check across these documents, because a tag that exists on the P&ID but not in the I/O list is already an open point.

What do you do when a requirement from the customer is unclear?

I follow a fixed routine. I write the issue into the open point list with an identifier and quote the exact document and revision. I propose an interpretation and explain why, choosing the conservative reading when safety is involved, and I note what the impact would be if the interpretation is wrong. Then I send a technical query to the customer and mark the affected requirement in the SRS as an assumption linked to that open point, so that reviewers and testers can see it. When the written answer arrives I update the requirement and the traceability and close the point. I never close an open point on the basis of a phone call.

Day 3 · Hour 3

System design and sizing: architecture, I/O, cabinets and signals

Here the requirements turn into an architecture: how many divisions, cabinets and processing units, how much I/O with spare capacity, and how the parts talk to each other. The posting names "system architecture, sizing, interfaces" as your responsibilities, so an interviewer may hand you a small sizing exercise.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Sketch a four-division safety I&C architecture with acquisition, voting, priority, service and gateway functions
  • Size the I/O of one division with spare capacity and estimate cabinet count, power and heat
  • Read a KKS-style tag and explain each part of it
  • Explain how a safety system interfaces with non-safety systems and with the other divisions
  • List the contents of a System Design Description

From requirements to architecture

The architecture is driven mostly by the non-functional requirements. The single-failure criterion and 2oo4 voting lead to four redundant divisions (trains). Independence puts them in separate rooms with separate power supplies, connected only through isolated links. Testability means one division can be in periodic test while the other three keep protecting the plant. The Design Engineer's job in this phase is to turn these demands into a drawing with boxes and lines, and then to count: channels, modules, racks, cabinets, cables, watts.

The building blocks of a division

Every digital safety platform has roughly the same roles under different names; below are the names used for TELEPERM XS. The Acquisition and Processing Unit (APU) reads the sensors of its division, does the analog input processing and the limit monitoring and produces "partial trip" signals. The Actuation Logic Unit (ALU) receives the partial trips from all four divisions over fibre-optic links, performs the voting (2oo4) and generates actuation commands. The priority module sits in front of each actuator and combines commands from the safety system, from the operator and from the non-safety automation, with the safety command always winning. The Monitoring and Service Interface (MSI) and the gateway pass diagnostic and process data to the non-safety side in one direction only, and give a service unit (an engineering computer) controlled access for diagnostics and software loading. A qualified display in the control room shows safety information to the operator.

Communication and interfaces

Inside a division the modules talk over the rack backplane and serial point-to-point links. Between divisions the only data exchange is the set of partial trips needed for voting, carried over fibre-optic point-to-point links. Fibre gives electrical isolation, so an electrical fault in one division cannot reach another, and point-to-point means there is no shared network whose failure would hit all four divisions. Towards the non-safety side the rule is one-directional flow: the safety system sends, it never receives commands. Hardwired signals crossing a safety-class boundary pass through isolation devices (opto-couplers, relays, isolation amplifiers) so that a fault on the low-class side cannot propagate. Hour 8 explains the cyber side of this boundary.

Note

"Independence" is checked physically, not only logically. Two divisions must not share a power feeder, a cabinet, a cable tray, a ventilation duct or a grounding point that could fail both.

Sizing: what to count

Sizing itemInputRule of thumb
I/O channels per typeI/O list, per divisionAdd 20 % spare channels (customers often ask 10 to 20 %), round up to whole modules
Modules, racks, cabinetsChannels per module, slots per rackKeep at least one spare slot per rack; do not mix safety classes in one rack
Processing loadNumber of function diagrams and blocks, platform execution dataStay below about 70 % of the cycle time budget to leave margin for changes
MemoryCode size from tool, data tablesKeep margin for later modifications
PowerSum of module powers plus 25 % marginEach of the two redundant 24 V DC feeders must carry the full load alone
HeatAlmost all electrical power becomes heatEstimate the temperature rise; add fans only if natural convection is insufficient
Cables and terminalsSignal list, cable typesCount cable entries and terminal strips; keep spare terminals

A worked sizing mini-example

The I/O list for division 1 of a reactor protection system gives: 96 analog inputs 4 to 20 mA, 16 RTD inputs, 240 binary inputs 24 V DC, 64 relay outputs and 8 analog outputs. The customer requires 20 % spare capacity. Assume 8 channels per analog input module, 4 per RTD module, 32 per binary input module, 8 per relay output module and 4 per analog output module.

Channels with 20 % spares:  AI 96 x 1.2 = 115.2 -> 116   RTD 16 x 1.2 -> 20
                            DI 240 x 1.2 = 288           DO 64 x 1.2 -> 77
                            AO 8 x 1.2 -> 10
Modules:  AI 116/8 = 14.5 -> 15     RTD 20/4 = 5     DI 288/32 = 9
          DO 77/8 = 9.6 -> 10        AO 10/4 = 2.5 -> 3
          Total I/O modules = 42
Racks:    assume 12 I/O slots per rack after processor and link modules
          42/12 = 3.5 -> 4 racks -> 2 cabinets with 2 racks each
Power:    42 I/O modules x 3 W + 4 processors x 20 W + 6 links x 5 W = 236 W
          +25 % margin -> about 300 W per division
          at 24 V DC: 300/24 = 12.5 A, carried by each feeder alone
Heat:     about 150 W per cabinet; steel cabinet 2.0 x 0.8 x 0.6 m,
          surface about 6 m2, k about 5.5 W/(m2.K)
          temperature rise = 150 / (5.5 x 6) = about 4.5 K -> natural cooling
Load:     180 function diagrams x 25 blocks = 4500 blocks x 5 us = 22.5 ms
          with a 50 ms cycle -> 45 % load, below the 70 % limit

None of these numbers is a product fact; they are assumptions of the kind you take from the platform documentation and record in the sizing calculation. What matters is the method: count, add margin, round up to whole modules, check racks, power, heat, cycle time and cables, and write every assumption down.

Common mistake

Sizing exactly to the I/O list. During detailed design new signals always appear (test signals, status feedback, alarms), and a cabinet without spare slots forces a change request for each of them.

Signal lists and KKS tags

Every signal gets one line in the signal list: tag, description, type, range and unit, source, destination (module and channel), division, safety class, function diagram and cable number. The tag follows the plant's naming convention. Many European plants use the KKS system (Kraftwerk-Kennzeichensystem, "power plant identification system"), which originated in Germany. A KKS tag has levels. Take 1LAB10CP001: the leading 1 is the unit; LAB is the system (L = steam, water and gas cycles; LA = feedwater system; LAB = feedwater piping); 10 is the numbering of that system, and in many plants 10, 20, 30 and 40 indicate the redundant trains; CP is the equipment type (C = direct measuring circuit, P = pressure, so a pressure measurement), and 001 is the sequential number. Similar codes are CF for flow, CL for level and CT for temperature. So 1LAB10CP001 reads "unit 1, feedwater piping, train 10, pressure measurement number 1". Plants may add a further level for the signal, and some use other conventions (ISA-style LT-101 for a level transmitter), so always ask for the customer's tagging specification.

Writing the System Design Description

The SDD is read by the customer, V&V, licensing engineers and detailed designers. A typical structure: purpose and scope; summary of functions with reference to the SRS; architecture overview with block diagrams; divisions, cabinets and units; I/O allocation per division; communication links and interfaces to other systems; power supply and grounding concept; HMI and displays; monitoring, self-diagnostics and periodic test concept; sizing and margins with the calculation above; qualification references; and a compliance table showing which section satisfies which requirement.

Tip

Every non-obvious design decision in the SDD deserves one sentence of justification: "Two cabinets per division are used so that the ALU rack can be isolated for test without disturbing acquisition." Reviewers and regulators value these sentences.

Interview angle

If you are asked to size something, do it aloud: "I would take the I/O list for one division, add the required spare, round up to modules, then check racks, power, heat and cycle time." The interviewer wants to see the method and the margins, not an exact number.

Key terms

Division (train)
One of the redundant, independent parts of a safety system, with its own sensors, cabinets, power supply and actuators.
Acquisition and Processing Unit (APU)
The processing unit of a division that reads sensors, processes analog inputs and produces partial trip signals.
Actuation Logic Unit (ALU)
The unit that receives partial trips from all divisions, performs the voting and generates actuation commands.
Priority module
A device in front of an actuator that combines commands from safety and non-safety sources and always gives priority to the safety command.
Gateway / Monitoring and Service Interface
The one-way exit of data from the safety system to the non-safety side, also the controlled access point for the service unit.
Spare capacity
Unused channels, slots, processing time and power deliberately kept for later changes, typically 10 to 20 %.
Cycle time
The fixed period in which a safety processor reads inputs, executes all function diagrams and writes outputs, typically tens of milliseconds.
Heat dissipation
The electrical power of a cabinet that becomes heat and must leave through the cabinet surface or by ventilation.
KKS
Kraftwerk-Kennzeichensystem, a structured tagging system for power plants: unit, system, equipment type and sequential number.
Signal list
The table of every signal with tag, type, range, source, destination, division, safety class and function diagram reference.

Say it in English

  • "The architecture has four independent divisions; partial trips are exchanged over isolated fibre-optic links and voted two out of four in each division."
  • "I size the I/O per division from the I/O list, add twenty percent spare and round up to whole modules, then I check racks, power, heat and processing load."
  • "Almost all the electrical power of a cabinet becomes heat, so I estimate the temperature rise before deciding on ventilation."
  • "Data leaves the safety system through a gateway in one direction only; nothing on the non-safety side can send commands to it."
  • "In a KKS tag such as 1LAB10CP001, LAB is the feedwater piping system, 10 is the train and CP001 is pressure measurement number one."

Check yourself

Why are fibre-optic point-to-point links used between divisions?

Fibre carries no electrical fault between divisions, and point-to-point links have no common element whose failure could stop all four.

A division needs 96 analog inputs. With 20 % spares and 8 channels per module, how many modules?

96 x 1.2 = 115.2 channels, rounded up to 116; 116 / 8 = 14.5, rounded up to 15 modules.

In the KKS tag 1LAB10CP001, what does "CP" indicate?

C stands for direct measuring circuit and P for pressure; CF, CL and CT are flow, level and temperature.

A cabinet dissipates 150 W, has about 6 m² of cooling surface and k ≈ 5.5 W/(m²·K). What is the approximate temperature rise?

150 / (5.5 x 6) ≈ 4.5 K, which natural convection handles comfortably.

What is the role of a priority module?

The priority module sits in front of the actuator and makes sure a safety actuation can never be overridden by operational control.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

Describe the architecture of a typical digital reactor protection system.

I would describe four independent divisions, each in its own room with its own power supply and its own sensors. In each division an acquisition and processing unit reads the sensors, does the analog processing and the limit monitoring, and produces partial trip signals. These partial trips are exchanged between the divisions over isolated fibre-optic point-to-point links, and in each division an actuation logic unit votes them two out of four and commands the actuators through priority modules. Data goes to the non-safety side only in one direction through a gateway, and a qualified display gives the operator the safety information. The whole design is driven by the single-failure criterion, independence and testability.

How would you size the I/O for one division?

I would start from the I/O list filtered for that division and count channels per type: analog inputs, RTD inputs, binary inputs, relay outputs, analog outputs. Then I would add the spare capacity required by the customer, typically twenty percent, round up to whole modules using the channel count per module, and keep at least one spare slot per rack. From the module count I derive racks and cabinets, then I sum the module powers with a margin to get the load on the redundant 24 volt feeders, estimate the heat and the temperature rise, and finally check the processing load against the cycle time. Every assumption goes into a sizing calculation document.

How do you interface a safety system with the non-safety plant computer?

The principle is one-directional flow. The safety system sends process values and diagnostics to the non-safety side through a gateway behind the monitoring and service interface, and there is no path for commands or data to come back into the safety processing units. Hardwired signals that cross the boundary go through isolation devices so that an electrical fault on the non-safety side cannot propagate. Where the two sides must command the same actuator, a priority module gives the safety command priority. In the design description I would show this boundary explicitly, because the regulator and the V&V team will check it both for electrical independence and for cybersecurity.

Can you explain how a KKS tag is built?

KKS is the German power plant identification system that many European plants use. The tag has levels. Take 1LAB10CP001. The first character is the unit. Then comes the system code: L for steam, water and gas cycles, LA for the feedwater system and LAB for feedwater piping. The two digits, 10, number the system and in many plants identify the redundant train, so 10, 20, 30 and 40. Then the equipment code: C for a direct measuring circuit and P for pressure, so a pressure measurement, and 001 is the sequential number. Codes like CF, CL and CT are flow, level and temperature. Plants add their own signal level, so I always ask for the customer's tagging specification.

Why do you add spare capacity and how much?

Because the I/O list at the start of a project is never the I/O list at the end. During detailed design new signals appear: status feedback, test signals, alarms, and changes from the customer. If the cabinets are sized exactly, every new signal becomes a hardware change request with re-qualification and new drawings. So I add spare channels, typically ten to twenty percent depending on the contract, spare slots in each rack, spare terminals, and I keep the processing load below about seventy percent of the cycle time. The spare also helps later in the plant life, when modifications have to fit into the installed cabinets without new ones.

Day 3 · Hour 4

Application software design: function diagrams and deterministic logic

The application software of a safety system is specified as function diagrams and turned into code by a qualified tool, not typed by hand. This hour explains the IEC 61131-3 languages, the typical blocks, the rules of deterministic execution, and walks through one complete trip function. Interviewers like to give exactly this kind of small design task.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Explain what a function diagram is and why it is specification and source code at the same time
  • Name the IEC 61131-3 languages and say which ones suit safety systems and why
  • Describe the typical blocks: analog input processing, limit monitor with hysteresis, timers, voting, drive control with priority, set/reset
  • Explain cycle time and deterministic execution and list what is forbidden in safety software
  • Design the logic for a 2oo4 trip with a time delay and a power-dependent bypass

Function diagrams: specification and code in one

In a digital safety platform the application software is drawn, not written. A function diagram (FD) is a sheet with inputs on the left (names from the signal list), function blocks in the middle and outputs on the right. Every block comes from a qualified library: it is documented and type-tested once, and the application engineer only connects and parameterises it. The engineering tool stores the diagrams and a qualified code generator turns them into executable code for the processing units (for TELEPERM XS this environment is called SPACE). The consequence: the function diagram is the software specification, the design and the source at the same time. When you review a function diagram, you are reviewing the software. Nobody hand-writes application code for a Category A function.

The IEC 61131-3 languages in plain English

IEC 61131-3 is the international standard for programmable controller languages: two graphical, two textual, one for sequences.

LanguageWhat it looks likeFit for safety application software
Function Block Diagram (FBD)Boxes connected by lines; data flows left to rightBest fit: graphical, type-tested blocks, no hidden control flow, comparable with the customer's logic diagrams
Ladder Diagram (LD)Relay-style rungs with contacts and coilsReadable for electricians, good for simple interlocks, weak for analog processing
Structured Text (ST)Pascal-like text with IF, CASE, loopsPowerful but hard to review; loops and branches hide execution time, so it is avoided or strictly restricted
Instruction List (IL)Assembly-like list of instructionsDeprecated in the current edition of the standard; not used for new designs
Sequential Function Chart (SFC)Steps and transitions, like a state machineGood for start-up sequences in operational control; protection logic has no sequences

Safety platforms use FBD or something very close to it, for reviewability (a reviewer can follow every signal with a finger), predictability (each block has a fixed execution time, so the cycle time can be calculated) and the one-to-one correspondence with the plant designer's logic diagrams.

The typical building blocks

  • Analog input processing. Converts the raw 4 to 20 mA value into engineering units, checks the range (below about 3.6 mA or above about 21 mA means a transmitter or cable fault), optionally filters, and outputs the value with a validity flag. Every downstream block must know what to do when validity is FALSE.
  • Limit monitor with hysteresis. For a "low" limit the output becomes TRUE when the value falls below the setpoint and returns to FALSE only when the value rises above setpoint plus hysteresis. Without hysteresis a value hovering around the setpoint makes the output chatter.
  • Timers. On-delay (TON): output TRUE only after the input has been TRUE for the preset time. Off-delay (TOF): output stays TRUE for the preset time after the input drops. Pulse (TP): fixed-length pulse.
  • Voting. 2oo4, 2oo3, 1oo2 blocks that count TRUE inputs, with a defined rule for invalid inputs (treat as TRUE, or exclude and degrade to 2oo3).
  • Drive control with priority. Combines commands from protection, operator and automation for one pump or valve, gives protection priority, and monitors the feedback (a valve commanded closed whose limit switch does not confirm within the run time raises a fault).
  • Set/reset flip-flop (RS/SR). Latches a trip until the operator resets it; you must choose whether set or reset dominates when both are TRUE (for a trip, set).
  • Selection and logic. Minimum, maximum, median, comparators, AND, OR, NOT.

Cycle time and deterministic execution

A safety processor runs a fixed loop: read all inputs, execute all function diagrams in a fixed order, write all outputs, exchange data with the other units, run self-tests, wait for the next cycle. The cycle time is constant, typically tens of milliseconds, and a watchdog checks that each cycle finishes in time. This is deterministic execution: the same inputs always produce the same outputs after the same time, and the worst-case response time is calculable as input filtering plus a few cycles plus communication and output delay. To keep this guarantee, several things are forbidden: dynamic memory allocation (no memory requested at run time), recursion, loops whose iteration count depends on data, interrupts that drive the application logic, and anything whose execution time cannot be bounded on paper.

Note

A feedback path in a function diagram (an output used as an input of the same diagram) always takes one cycle. Design rules usually require an explicit one-cycle delay block there, so that a reviewer sees the timing.

Design rules and why they exist

Each project has a design rule document for function diagrams. Typical rules: a maximum number of blocks per sheet; signals always flow left to right; every constant carries a reference to the setpoint list; every analog input has a defined behaviour for invalid values; no unused outputs and no dead logic; signal names follow the signal list exactly; each sheet has a header with identifier, revision, author, checker and the requirement it implements; only library blocks may be used. The rules exist so that two engineers produce the same diagram for the same requirement, so that reviews are fast and complete, and so that the code generator only meets constructs it has been qualified for.

Common mistake

Getting the hysteresis direction wrong. For a low limit the reset threshold is above the setpoint; for a high limit it is below. The other way round, the monitor trips later than the safety analysis assumed.

Worked example: low steam generator level trip

Requirement: trip the reactor when the steam generator level is low, voted 2oo4 across the four divisions, with a 2 s delay, bypassed while reactor power is below 10 %. (Pedagogical example; real setpoints, delays and permissives come from the safety analysis.) Following Hour 3, each division computes its own partial trip from its own sensor and the voting happens in each actuation logic unit.

(* --- Division n, Acquisition and Processing Unit --- *)
SG1_LVL_RAW : REAL;   (* 4..20 mA narrow-range level, 0..100 % *)
PWR_RAW     : REAL;   (* reactor power, 0..120 % *)
LVL_SP := 25.0;  LVL_HYS := 1.0;   PWR_BYP := 10.0;  PWR_HYS := 0.5;
                                   (* source: setpoint list SPL-003 rev B *)
(* 1: analog input processing -> value plus validity *)
SG1_LVL, SG1_LVL_VALID := AI_PROC(SG1_LVL_RAW, 0.0, 100.0);
PWR,     PWR_VALID     := AI_PROC(PWR_RAW, 0.0, 120.0);
(* 2: limit monitoring with hysteresis *)
LVL_LOW := LIMIT_LOW(IN := SG1_LVL, SP := LVL_SP, HYS := LVL_HYS);
                      (* TRUE below 25 %, FALSE again above 26 % *)
PWR_LOW := LIMIT_LOW(IN := PWR, SP := PWR_BYP, HYS := PWR_HYS);
(* 3: validity handling, conservative direction *)
LVL_LOW_N := LVL_LOW OR NOT SG1_LVL_VALID;   (* invalid level = low *)
BYPASS_N  := PWR_LOW AND PWR_VALID;          (* invalid power = no bypass *)
(* 4: partial trip of this division, sent to all four ALUs *)
PT_SG1_LOW_N := LVL_LOW_N AND NOT BYPASS_N;

(* --- Every division, Actuation Logic Unit --- *)
(* partial trips 1..4 arrive via isolated fibre; a failed link = TRUE *)
VOTE  := VOTE_2oo4(PT_SG1_LOW_1, PT_SG1_LOW_2, PT_SG1_LOW_3, PT_SG1_LOW_4);
DELAY := TON(IN := VOTE, PT := T#2s);
TRIP  := RS(SET := DELAY.Q, RESET := OP_RESET AND NOT DELAY.Q); (* set dominant *)
RT_BREAKER_OPEN_CMD := TRIP;                 (* to reactor trip breakers *)
BlockInputsParametersOutputLocated in
AI_PROC (x2)Raw mA valueRange, fault limitsValue, validityAPU
LIMIT_LOW (x2)ValueSetpoint, hysteresisBooleanAPU
OR, NOT, ANDBooleansnonePartial tripAPU
VOTE_2oo44 partial tripsInvalid-input ruleVoted tripALU
TONVoted trip2 sDelayed tripALU
RS flip-flopDelayed trip, resetSet dominantLatched trip commandALU

Three decisions are worth saying aloud at an interview. An invalid level channel counts as "low", because the safe direction for a trip is to trip. An invalid power measurement removes the bypass, for the same reason. The 2 s delay sits after the vote, so there is one timer instead of four and the response-time budget includes it explicitly.

Tip

Before drawing anything, write down the behaviour for every abnormal case: invalid input, lost link, power-up, operator reset while the condition is still present. The normal case is the easy part.

Interview angle

You may get "design the logic for trip X on a whiteboard". Draw left to right: analog processing with validity, limit monitor with hysteresis, validity handling, voting, timer, latch, output. Then say which direction is fail-safe for each decision. That order shows you have done it before.

Key terms

Function block
