A type-tested unit of logic from a qualified library (timer, voter, limit monitor) that the application engineer connects and parameterises but never modifies.
IEC 61131-3
The international standard for programmable controller programming languages: FBD, LD, ST, IL and SFC.
Function Block Diagram (FBD)
The graphical language of connected boxes with data flowing left to right; the preferred form for safety application software.
Structured Text (ST)
A Pascal-like textual language with IF and loops; powerful but hard to review, so restricted in safety applications.
Hysteresis
The gap between the switching threshold and the reset threshold of a limit monitor, which prevents chattering around the setpoint.
On-delay timer (TON)
A block whose output becomes TRUE only after its input has been TRUE continuously for the preset time.
Validity flag
A Boolean carried with each analog value that says whether the value may be trusted; downstream logic must define its behaviour when the flag is FALSE.
Cycle time
The constant period of the processor loop: read inputs, execute all diagrams, write outputs, communicate, self-test.
Deterministic execution
Behaviour in which the same inputs always give the same outputs after the same, calculable time, with no data-dependent loops, dynamic memory or interrupts.
Code generator
The qualified tool that translates function diagrams automatically into executable code, removing hand-coding from the application level.

Say it in English

  • "The application software is specified as function diagrams built from a qualified block library, and the code is generated automatically, so the diagram is both the specification and the source."
  • "We use the function block diagram language because it is graphical, reviewable and has predictable execution time; structured text with loops is avoided."
  • "Every analog input delivers a value and a validity flag, and I define the fail-safe behaviour for an invalid value before I draw the logic."
  • "A limit monitor needs hysteresis; for a low limit the reset threshold is above the setpoint."
  • "Deterministic execution means no dynamic memory, no unbounded loops and no interrupts in the application logic, so the worst-case response time can be calculated."

Check yourself

Why is Function Block Diagram preferred over Structured Text for safety application software?

Reviewability and predictable timing are the reasons; the standard itself allows all languages.

A low-limit monitor has setpoint 25 % and hysteresis 1 %. When does its output return to FALSE?

For a low limit the reset threshold is setpoint plus hysteresis, so 26 %; this prevents chattering around 25 %.

Which of these is forbidden in deterministic safety software?

Memory requested at run time can fail or fragment unpredictably; the other three are exactly what makes execution deterministic.

In the worked example, why does an invalid level channel count as "low"?

A failed channel must not be able to prevent a trip; treating it as a trip demand is the safe direction, and 2oo4 tolerates one such channel without a spurious trip.

What does an on-delay timer (TON) with PT = 2 s do?

That is the TON definition; the off-delay is TOF and the pulse timer is TP.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

How is application software developed for a safety I&C platform?

It is developed as function diagrams, not as hand-written code. I take the customer's logic diagrams and the signal list, and I draw the logic with blocks from a qualified library: analog input processing, limit monitors, timers, voters, flip-flops, drive control. Each block is type-tested once, so I only connect and parameterise it. The engineering tool stores the diagrams and a qualified code generator produces the executable code for the processing units. That is why the function diagram is the specification, the design and the source at the same time, and why a document review of the diagram is a software review. The diagrams then go through independent verification and are tested on a test bench.

Which IEC 61131-3 languages do you know and which are suitable for safety systems?

The standard defines Function Block Diagram and Ladder Diagram, which are graphical, Structured Text and Instruction List, which are textual, and Sequential Function Chart for sequences. For safety application software the function block diagram is the right choice. It is graphical, every block has a known execution time, there is no hidden control flow, and it maps one-to-one onto the logic diagrams the plant designer delivers, which makes reviews straightforward. Structured text is powerful but loops and branches make execution time and review harder, so it is avoided or restricted. Sequential function chart suits start-up sequences in operational control but protection logic does not have sequences.

Design the logic for a trip on low steam generator level, two out of four, with a two-second delay and a bypass below ten percent power.

In each division I start with analog input processing for the level and for reactor power, which gives me a value and a validity flag. A low-limit monitor with hysteresis on the level produces the low signal; another one on power produces the bypass condition. For validity I choose the safe direction: an invalid level counts as low, and an invalid power removes the bypass. The partial trip is low AND NOT bypass, and it is sent to all four actuation logic units. There, a two-out-of-four voter combines the four partial trips, an on-delay timer of two seconds follows the vote, and a set-dominant flip-flop latches the trip to the reactor trip breakers until the operator resets it.

What does deterministic execution mean and why does it matter?

Deterministic execution means that the processor runs a fixed cycle: read inputs, execute all function diagrams in a fixed order, write outputs, communicate, self-test, and that this cycle always takes the same time. The same inputs give the same outputs after the same delay, every time. It matters because the safety analysis assumes a maximum response time, and we can only prove that time if nothing in the software has an unbounded duration. That is why dynamic memory allocation, recursion, data-dependent loops and interrupt-driven application logic are forbidden, and why a watchdog monitors the cycle. It also makes testing meaningful, because a test result today is valid tomorrow.

Why do you need design rules for function diagrams?

For three reasons. First, consistency: two engineers should produce essentially the same diagram for the same requirement, so that the system looks the same everywhere and maintenance engineers can read it twenty years later. Second, reviewability: limits on blocks per sheet, left-to-right signal flow, references to the setpoint list for every constant and a defined behaviour for every invalid input make a review fast and complete. Third, tool qualification: the code generator has been qualified for a defined set of constructs, and the rules keep the diagrams inside that set. A rule like "every feedback loop needs an explicit one-cycle delay block" exists so that timing is visible, not hidden.

Day 3 · Hour 5

Hardware design: cabinets, power, grounding, I/O modules and qualification

Software gets the attention, but the trip signal travels through terminals, fuses, modules and cables that somebody had to design. This hour covers cabinet design, I/O module selection, the hardware documents, and how cabinets are qualified and accepted. Interviewers for a Design Engineer role check that you can think in both worlds.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Describe the layout of a safety I&C cabinet and the rules for separating classes and divisions
  • Explain redundant 24 V DC power distribution with fuses per circuit, and the grounding and shielding concept
  • Select the right I/O module type for a given field signal and explain isolation and loop powering
  • Name the hardware documents and say what each one contains
  • Explain environmental, seismic and EMC qualification and what happens at a hardware FAT

What the hardware designer is responsible for

The System Design Description says "two cabinets per division with these modules". The hardware designer turns that sentence into something a workshop can build: which module sits in which slot, how every field signal enters the cabinet, where it is terminated, fused, isolated and wired to its channel, how power is distributed, how heat leaves, how the cabinet survives an earthquake, and which drawings the manufacturer and the site electricians need. The inputs are the signal list and I/O allocation from Hour 3, the module catalogue, the room layout, the environmental conditions, the plant grounding concept and the customer's installation standards.

Cabinet layout and separation

A typical I&C cabinet is about 2 m high, 0.6 to 0.8 m wide and 0.6 to 0.8 m deep. Field cables enter from the bottom (raised floor) or the top (cable trays) and land on terminal strips; this area is called marshalling: the field wiring is re-ordered here to match the channel allocation. From the terminals, internal wires run in cable ducts to the racks that hold processor, communication and I/O modules. Power distribution sits in its own area, often at the top. Warm components go high, cool air enters low, and about 20 % of the space stays free.

Separation rules are strict. One cabinet belongs to one division, never two. Within a division, equipment of different safety classes is kept in separate cabinets or at least in separate racks with separate cable ducts and a physical barrier. Cables of different divisions run in separate trays and enter through separate openings. For seismic reasons the cabinet is anchored to the floor or to a steel frame, modules are locked in their slots and heavy items sit low.

Note

Separation is a hardware requirement that comes straight from the independence principle of Day 2. A reviewer will ask of every shared tray, duct, feeder and earth connection: "which failure here affects two divisions?"

Power distribution

Safety cabinets are fed from two independent, battery-backed 24 V DC sources of their division. The two feeders are combined by diode decoupling (two diodes in an "OR" arrangement) so that the loss or short circuit of one source does not interrupt the load, and each feeder alone can carry the full cabinet current. Downstream, every circuit has its own fuse or miniature circuit breaker: each rack, each group of transmitter loops, each relay output group. The purpose is selectivity: a short circuit in one field cable blows one small fuse and takes out one loop, not the cabinet. Each fuse is monitored and raises an alarm when blown. Transmitter loop power is fused separately from module power, so a field fault cannot starve a processor.

Many transmitters are two-wire devices: one pair carries both the 24 V supply and the 4 to 20 mA signal, and the analog input module (or a separate supply) provides the loop power. The loop has a voltage budget: the supply of about 24 V minus the drop across the module input resistance (a 250 Ω input drops 5 V at 20 mA) minus the cable drop must still leave the transmitter its minimum operating voltage, often around 12 V.

Grounding, shielding and EMC inside the cabinet

Two kinds of earth exist. Protective earth (PE) protects people: every metal part of the cabinet is bonded to the PE bar so that a fault cannot leave a door at a dangerous voltage. Functional earth (instrument earth) gives the signals a clean reference and a place to connect cable shields; depending on the plant concept it is a separate bar connected to PE at one defined point. Shields of analog signal cables are usually earthed at one end only, in the cabinet, so that no current flows through the shield; where high-frequency immunity requires it, the plant concept may call for both ends through a 360° gland. Power cables and signal cables are kept apart in separate ducts. I/O modules provide galvanic isolation between the field side and the processor side, and often between channels, so that a fault voltage on one cable cannot reach the electronics.

Common mistake

Earthing a shield "wherever it is convenient". A shield earthed at both ends without a concept forms a loop in which plant earth currents flow, and the analog signal picks up noise. Follow the plant grounding concept and document every deviation.

Choosing I/O modules

Field signalModule typeDesign points
Pressure, flow, level transmitter, 4 to 20 mA, two-wireAnalog input with loop powerRange check for fault detection (< 3.6 mA, > 21 mA), loop voltage budget, channel isolation
Pt100 resistance thermometerRTD input, 3- or 4-wireLead resistance compensation; alternatively a field transmitter converts to 4 to 20 mA
Limit switch, pressure switch (dry contact)Binary input, 24 V DC (48 V in some plants)Cabinet supplies the wetting voltage; line monitoring can detect an open cable
Command to valve or pumpRelay output or solid-state output, via priority module for safety actuatorsGalvanic isolation, contact rating for the coil voltage, fail-safe state on loss of power
Indicator, positionerAnalog output 4 to 20 mALoad resistance range
Speed, pulsesCounter or frequency inputMaximum frequency, debounce

Only modules from the platform's qualified catalogue may be used; the qualification evidence is attached to the module type.

Documents and tools

The hardware package normally contains: the cabinet layout drawing (front view with racks, terminal strips, dimensions, weights); the wiring diagram (every internal connection); the terminal plan (each terminal strip with wire numbers and the cable and core connected to it); the cable list (cable number, type, from, to, length, route); the power distribution diagram; the grounding diagram; the parts list; and the label list. These documents are generated from a database with electrical CAD tools such as EPLAN, so that a renamed signal changes consistently in all drawings. Consistency between signal list, I/O allocation and drawings is a standard review item.

Qualification and the hardware FAT

A safety cabinet must prove that it works in its environment. Environmental qualification (IEC 60780 is the nuclear standard) covers temperature and humidity ranges and ageing. Seismic qualification (IEC 60980) is done on a shaking table with the plant's response spectrum, or by analysis of similarity with a tested cabinet; the cabinet must stay functional during and after the earthquake. EMC qualification (IEC 62003 for nuclear I&C, built on the IEC 61000-4 test series) covers immunity to electrostatic discharge, radiated and conducted radio frequency, fast transients, surges and magnetic fields, and limits on emission. Platform modules carry type-test evidence; the project cabinet adds a cabinet-level test or analysis.

The hardware Factory Acceptance Test checks the built cabinet against the drawings: visual inspection, point-to-point wiring check, insulation test, power-up with both feeders and with each feeder alone, fuse coordination, a loop check of every I/O channel (inject 4 to 20 mA at the terminal, read the value in the processor), label check and document check. The customer witnesses, and every deviation goes on a punch list that must be closed before shipment.

Tip

When you review a hardware package, follow one signal with your finger from the field terminal through the fuse, the terminal plan, the wire number and the module channel to the signal list. If three random signals are consistent, the package is usually good; if not, you have found a systematic error.

Interview angle

Hardware questions are usually simple but concrete: "How do you power a safety cabinet?" or "Where do you earth a cable shield?" Answer with the principle (redundancy with diode decoupling, selectivity with a fuse per circuit, one grounding concept for the whole plant) and one number.

Key terms

Marshalling
The terminal area of a cabinet where field cables are landed and re-ordered so that each signal reaches its allocated module channel.
Terminal strip
A row of numbered terminals where field cores and internal wires are connected; documented in the terminal plan.
Diode decoupling
Two diodes combining two power feeders so that either one can supply the load and a fault on one does not affect the other.
Selectivity
Fuse sizing such that a fault trips only the smallest fuse closest to it and leaves the rest of the cabinet in service.
Loop power
The 24 V DC supplied over the same two wires that carry the 4 to 20 mA signal of a two-wire transmitter.
Protective earth (PE)
The earth connection that protects people by bonding all metal parts; distinct from the functional earth used as signal reference.
Galvanic isolation
Separation of two circuits so that no conductive path exists between them, typically by opto-couplers, relays or transformers.
RTD
Resistance temperature detector, usually Pt100: a sensor whose resistance changes with temperature, read by a dedicated 3- or 4-wire input.
EMC
Electromagnetic compatibility: the equipment neither disturbs its environment nor is disturbed by it, proven by immunity and emission tests.
Seismic qualification
Proof by shaking table test or analysis that a cabinet stays functional during and after the design earthquake.

Say it in English

  • "The cabinet is fed by two independent 24 volt DC feeders with diode decoupling, and every circuit has its own fuse so that a field fault takes out one loop, not the cabinet."
  • "One cabinet belongs to one division; different safety classes are separated by cabinets or at least by racks and ducts."
  • "A two-wire transmitter is loop-powered, so I check the voltage budget: supply minus the module input drop minus the cable drop must still leave the transmitter its minimum voltage."
  • "Shields are earthed according to the plant grounding concept, usually at the cabinet end only for analog signals."
  • "Cabinets are qualified for environment, seismic loads and EMC, and the hardware FAT checks the built cabinet against the drawings channel by channel."

Check yourself

Why does each circuit in a safety cabinet get its own fuse?

This is selectivity: the smallest fuse nearest the fault opens first and the rest of the division stays in service.

Which module type is right for a Pt100 sensor connected directly to the cabinet?

A Pt100 is a resistance thermometer; the RTD input measures its resistance and compensates the lead resistance.

What is the purpose of diode decoupling of the two 24 V DC feeders?

The diodes form an OR: the load is supplied as long as one feeder is healthy, and a short on one side is blocked from the other.

A 24 V loop supply, a 250 Ω input at 20 mA and a cable drop of 2 V. How much voltage is left for the transmitter?

250 Ω x 0.02 A = 5 V across the input; 24 - 5 - 2 = 17 V, which is above the typical minimum of about 12 V.

Which test series does EMC immunity qualification of I&C cabinets build on?

IEC 61000-4 defines the immunity tests (ESD, RF, burst, surge); IEC 60980 is the seismic standard and IEC 61131-3 is about PLC languages.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

How is a safety I&C cabinet powered?

From two independent, battery-backed 24 volt DC sources belonging to the same division. Inside the cabinet the two feeders are combined with diode decoupling, so that if one source fails or is short-circuited the other carries the full load alone; each feeder is sized for that. Downstream, the distribution is split into many fused circuits: one per rack, one per group of transmitter loops, one per output group. This gives selectivity, so a short circuit in a field cable blows a small fuse and affects one loop, not the processor. Every fuse is monitored and raises an alarm when it blows.

How do you select I/O modules for the signals in the I/O list?

I look at each signal type in the I/O list and map it to a module type from the qualified catalogue of the platform. A two-wire pressure or level transmitter goes to an analog input with loop power and range checking. A Pt100 goes to an RTD input with three or four wires for lead compensation, unless a field transmitter already converts it to 4 to 20 milliamps. Dry contacts from limit switches go to binary inputs with the cabinet supplying the wetting voltage. Commands to valves and pumps go to relay outputs, through a priority module for safety actuators. I check isolation, contact ratings and the fail-safe state on loss of power, and I only use qualified modules.

What is the grounding and shielding concept you would apply?

I would apply the plant's grounding concept rather than invent one, because grounding only works if the whole plant follows one rule. In general there is protective earth for the safety of people, with every metal part bonded to the PE bar, and a functional earth as the clean reference for signals and shields, connected to PE at one defined point. Shields of analog signal cables are typically earthed at one end in the cabinet, so that no earth current flows through them; where high-frequency immunity needs it the concept may require both ends with a 360 degree connection. Signal and power cables run in separate ducts, and the I/O modules add galvanic isolation.

How are cabinets qualified for a nuclear plant?

Three kinds of qualification are needed. Environmental qualification shows the cabinet works in the temperature and humidity range of its room and over its lifetime; the nuclear standard is IEC 60780. Seismic qualification, according to IEC 60980, is done on a shaking table using the required response spectrum, or by analysis of similarity with a cabinet that was already tested, and the cabinet must stay functional during and after the earthquake. EMC qualification follows IEC 62003 and the IEC 61000-4 test series: immunity to electrostatic discharge, radiated and conducted radio frequency, fast transients, surges and magnetic fields, plus emission limits. The platform modules bring type-test evidence, and the project cabinet is qualified on top of it.

What is checked during a hardware factory acceptance test?

The FAT checks that the cabinet was built as drawn and that it works. We start with a visual inspection against the layout drawing and the label list, then a point-to-point wiring check against the wiring diagram and terminal plan, and an insulation test. Then we power up with both feeders and with each feeder alone and verify fuse coordination. The main part is the loop check: for every input channel we inject a signal at the field terminal, for example 4, 12 and 20 milliamps, and read the value in the processor, and for every output we command it and measure at the terminal. The customer witnesses, and deviations go into a punch list closed before shipment.

Day 3 · Hour 6

Verification and validation: proving that the system is right

Everything you produce will be checked by someone else, and you will check theirs. This hour explains the V&V activities from document review to site acceptance, teaches you to write a proper test case and shows how findings are handled. "How would you test that?" is one of the most common interview questions for this role.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Explain the difference between verification and validation with a concrete example
  • List the V&V activities from review to periodic test and say what each one checks
  • Write a complete test case with preconditions, steps, expected results, pass/fail criteria and traceability
  • Explain test coverage for requirements and for function diagrams
  • Describe how a finding is handled from detection to regression test

Verification versus validation

Verification answers "did we build it right?" It compares the output of one phase with its input: does the System Design Description satisfy the SRS, does the function diagram implement the logic diagram, does the cabinet match the drawing. It happens at every step of the V. Validation answers "did we build the right thing?" It takes the complete, integrated system and tests it against the original requirements under realistic conditions, usually with a plant simulator. The difference matters because a system can be perfectly verified and still wrong. Suppose a logic diagram was misread as 2oo3 instead of 2oo4. The function diagram implements 2oo3 exactly as the designer intended, every module test passes, and verification finds nothing. Only a validation test derived from the requirement "2oo4" exposes the error.

The ladder of V&V activities

ActivityWhat it checksWho, when
Document reviewCompleteness, consistency with inputs, traceability, formal rulesChecker, then independent V&V; before release of each document
Design reviewArchitecture decisions against requirements and standardsMultidisciplinary meeting at milestones
Function diagram review / static analysisLogic against logic diagrams and design rules; unconnected inputs, type mismatches, namingSecond engineer and V&V, with tool support
Module (unit) testOne function diagram or block with simulated inputs, all pathsV&V, on an engineering station or test rig
Integration testUnits together with real communication linksV&V, on integrated hardware
System validation testComplete system against the SRS, closed loop with plant simulator, timingIndependent V&V, test bench
FATSelected validation tests repeated with the customer witnessingSupplier and customer, before shipment
SAT and commissioningLoop checks sensor to actuator, interfaces to other systems, integrated plant testsSite team, utility, regulator may witness
Periodic testFunction still works after years of operationPlant maintenance, at fixed intervals

Reviews and static analysis

A document review uses a checklist and a review sheet. The reviewer writes each comment with a location, a statement of what is wrong and a reference to the input that was violated. The author answers every comment in writing: accepted and corrected, or rejected with a reason. The reviewer then closes or escalates. Nothing is discussed only orally. A design review is a meeting at a milestone where the architecture is challenged by people from software, hardware, V&V, licensing and the customer. For function diagrams, review is supported by static analysis: tool checks for unconnected inputs, type mismatches, forbidden blocks, naming violations and unused outputs. For the platform's own software, static analysis means code checks against coding rules and complexity limits.

Tests from module to periodic

A module test exercises one function diagram with simulated inputs: every Boolean in both states, every limit crossed in both directions, every timer at its boundary, every invalid-input path. An integration test puts the processing units together with their real links and checks that signals arrive where the design says, including behaviour when a link is disconnected. System validation runs on a test bench where a plant simulator feeds realistic process signals and reacts to the system's outputs; here response times are measured with an event recorder and the trip functions are exercised with simulated transients. The FAT repeats a selected set of these tests with the customer present. After installation, the SAT checks every loop from the real sensor through the cabinet to the real actuator, the interfaces to other plant systems, and finally integrated tests with the plant itself. During operation, periodic tests at fixed intervals prove that the function still works; digital self-diagnostics reduce the manual effort but do not remove the obligation.

Note

The test specification for each level is written when the corresponding specification on the left leg of the V is written. Validation test cases are derived from the SRS by the V&V team, who deliberately do not read the function diagrams first.

Writing a test case

A test case has a fixed anatomy: identifier and revision; title; the requirements it covers; the test level; preconditions that put the system into a known state; numbered steps, each with an expected result that contains a value and a tolerance; pass/fail criteria; and what must be recorded. Here is a full example for the trip function of Hour 4.

Test case     TC-VAL-0412-1  rev A        Level: system validation
Title         Reactor trip on low SG1 level, 2oo4, 2 s delay, bypass
Covers        SRS-0412, SRS-0413, SRS-0414
Preconditions
  P1  All four divisions powered, no faults, no channel in test
  P2  Simulated reactor power = 100 % (bypass inactive)
  P3  SG1 level channels D1..D4 = 60 %; trip breakers closed
  P4  Event recorder running, 1 ms time stamps
Steps and expected results
  S1  Ramp SG1 level D1 from 60 % to 20 % within 10 s
      E1  Partial trip D1 = TRUE when level < 25 %; no reactor trip
  S2  Ramp SG1 level D2 to 20 %
      E2  Voted 2oo4 TRUE in all four ALUs; trip output 2.0 s +/- 0.1 s
          after the vote; both trip breakers open
      E3  Time from D2 crossing 25 % to trip output <= 2.3 s
  S3  Return D1 and D2 to 60 %
      E4  Trip remains latched
  S4  Operator reset
      E5  Trip resets; breakers can be closed
  S5  Set simulated power = 8 %; repeat S1 and S2
      E6  No partial trip, no reactor trip; bypass shown on display
  S6  Power = 100 %; D3 channel open circuit (< 3.6 mA); D1 = 20 %
      E7  Trip after 2.0 s +/- 0.1 s (invalid channel counts as low)
Pass/fail     PASS only if E1..E7 are all met; any deviation -> anomaly report
Record        Measured delays, event list, software version, test bench
              configuration ID, tester, witness, date

Common mistake

Writing "expected result: the system works correctly". An expected result is a value with a tolerance and a time, observed at a defined place. If two testers could disagree about whether the result was met, the test case is not finished.

Coverage

Requirements coverage means every requirement in the SRS is linked to at least one test case that has passed; the traceability matrix proves it. Functional coverage of a function diagram means every block, every input and output, both states of every Boolean, every limit crossed in both directions and every timer tested at its boundary. For hand-written platform software, structural coverage is measured on the code: statement coverage (every line executed) and branch coverage (every decision taken both ways). Good test design also uses boundary values (setpoint minus tolerance, setpoint plus tolerance), invalid values and timing cases. A test that cannot fail is not a test.

Findings, defects and independence

A review comment or a failed test produces a finding, written in an anomaly report. Each finding is categorised (documentation, minor, major, safety-relevant), analysed for its root cause (was the requirement wrong, the design, the implementation or the test itself?), and fixed through the change process of Hour 7. The fix is re-tested, and a regression test repeats the affected tests plus a sample of others to show that the fix broke nothing. The finding is closed only with evidence attached. Findings are tracked in a database and reported to the customer. Independence (IEC 60880) means the V&V team decides whether a finding is closed; the design engineer answers it, but does not close it.

Tip

When you receive a finding, thank the finder. Then answer in three parts: what you understand the finding to be, what you will change, and which other documents or tests are affected. That last part is what distinguishes a careful engineer.

Interview angle

"How would you test this function?" wants structure: preconditions, steps with expected values and tolerances, abnormal cases (invalid input, lost link), pass/fail criteria and traceability. Mention that you would test the bypass both active and inactive. That is the answer of someone who has written test cases.

Key terms

Verification
Checking that the output of a phase is consistent with its input: "did we build it right?"
Validation
Testing the complete system against the original requirements under realistic conditions: "did we build the right thing?"
Static analysis
Tool-based checking of diagrams or code without executing them: unconnected inputs, type mismatches, rule violations.
Module (unit) test
A test of one function diagram or block in isolation with simulated inputs covering every path.
System validation test
A test of the integrated system on a test bench with a plant simulator, derived from the SRS by the independent V&V team.
Site Acceptance Test (SAT)
Tests at the plant after installation: loop checks from sensor to actuator and interface tests with other systems.
Test case
A specified test with identifier, covered requirements, preconditions, numbered steps, expected results with tolerances, pass/fail criteria and records.
Requirements coverage
The proportion of requirements linked to at least one passed test case; the goal is 100 %.
Regression test
Repeating affected tests and a sample of others after a change to show that nothing else was broken.
Anomaly report
The formal record of a finding: description, category, root cause, corrective action, re-test evidence and closure.

Say it in English

  • "Verification checks each step against its input; validation checks the whole system against the requirements."
  • "A test case needs preconditions, numbered steps, expected results with tolerances, pass/fail criteria and a link to the requirement."
  • "I would test the trip at the setpoint boundary, with the bypass active and inactive, and with one channel invalid."
  • "After a fix we re-run the affected tests and a regression sample to show nothing else moved."
  • "The V&V team is independent: I answer a finding, but they decide when it is closed."

Check yourself

A function diagram implements 2oo3 exactly as the designer intended, but the requirement said 2oo4. Which activity is most likely to catch this?

Tests derived from the design share the designer's error; a test derived independently from the requirement fails and exposes it.

Which of these is an acceptable expected result in a test case?

An expected result needs a value, a tolerance and an observation point; the other options cannot be objectively judged.

What does a regression test demonstrate?

Regression testing repeats affected tests and a sample of others after a modification.

Where is the system validation test normally performed?

Validation needs the integrated system and realistic process signals, which the test bench with simulator provides before shipment.

Who decides that a V&V finding is closed?

Independence requires that the finder, not the author, confirms closure with evidence.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

What is the difference between verification and validation?

Verification asks whether we built it right: at each step of the lifecycle we check that the output matches its input, for example that the design description satisfies the requirements specification or that a function diagram implements the logic diagram. Validation asks whether we built the right thing: the complete integrated system is tested against the original requirements under realistic conditions, typically on a test bench with a plant simulator. The distinction matters because a misunderstanding in the design passes all verification steps, since the design is implemented exactly as intended. Only validation derived independently from the requirements catches it. Both are performed by an independent V&V team for Category A systems.

How would you test a reactor trip function?

I would write a validation test case traced to the requirements. Preconditions: all divisions healthy, simulated power at full load so the bypass is inactive, all level channels normal, event recorder running. Then numbered steps: lower one channel below the setpoint and expect a partial trip but no reactor trip; lower a second channel and expect the two-out-of-four vote and the trip output after the specified delay with a tolerance, measured by time stamps. I would check latching and operator reset, repeat with the bypass active to show no trip, and test one channel invalid to show the fail-safe behaviour. Every expected result has a value, a tolerance and an observation point, and the record includes the software version.

What happens when a test fails?

The tester writes an anomaly report: what was expected, what was observed, the configuration and software version. The finding is categorised, for example minor or safety-relevant, and analysed for its root cause: a wrong requirement, a design error, an implementation error or an error in the test itself. The correction then goes through the change process with an impact analysis, because a fix in one function diagram can affect others. After the fix, the failed test is repeated and a regression set is run to show nothing else changed. The V&V team, not the designer, reviews the evidence and closes the finding. The whole chain is documented and visible to the customer.

What does test coverage mean for a safety I&C system?

It means several things at once. Requirements coverage: every requirement in the specification is linked to at least one test case that has passed, which the traceability matrix proves. Functional coverage of the function diagrams: every block and every signal exercised, every Boolean in both states, every limit crossed in both directions, timers tested at their boundaries, and every invalid-input path. For hand-written platform software, structural coverage is measured on the code as statement and branch coverage. On top of that I would use boundary values around setpoints, invalid values and timing cases, because errors live at the edges, not in the normal operating range.

Why are V&V test cases derived from the requirements and not from the design?

Because a test derived from the design can only confirm that the design does what the designer intended, and the most dangerous errors are those where the designer intended the wrong thing. If a logic diagram was misread, the function diagram, the module tests written from it and the designer's own checks all share the same mistake and all pass. A V&V engineer who reads only the requirement and writes the expected result from it will see the test fail. That is why IEC 60880 asks for independent V&V, and why the test specifications are written before the testers look at the implementation.

Day 3 · Hour 7

Quality, configuration and change management: keeping the system under control

A safety system is not only logic and cabinets; it is thousands of documents, versions and decisions that must stay consistent for forty years. This hour covers quality management, document control, configuration management, change management and non-conformances, and explains why "a small change is never small". Interviewers use this topic to test your attention to detail.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Explain what a project quality plan is and how ISO 9001 and IAEA GSR Part 2 relate to it
  • Describe document control: numbering, revisions, approval workflow and as-built documentation
  • Explain configuration management: baselines, versions and consistency between hardware, software and documents
  • Walk through change management from change request to re-verification and documentation
  • Explain non-conformance reports, independent assessment and licensing documentation

Quality management: the frame around the work

ISO 9001 is the generic quality management standard: documented processes, defined responsibilities, control of documents and records, internal audits, corrective actions and management review. A nuclear supplier needs more. The IAEA safety requirement GSR Part 2 ("Leadership and Management for Safety") asks for an integrated management system in which safety is the overriding priority, with a graded approach (more control for more safety-significant items) and attention to safety culture. In Europe, ISO 19443 adds nuclear-specific requirements on top of ISO 9001 for suppliers of items important to nuclear safety. The company applies these through its management system; each project applies them through a project quality plan. That plan names the organisation and responsibilities, the applicable standards and customer requirements, the list of documents to be produced, the review and approval rules, the V&V plan, the configuration management plan, the control of sub-suppliers, the planned audits and the records to be kept. You work inside this plan every day, mostly without noticing, until an auditor asks you to show the evidence.

Note

Graded approach does not mean "less quality for lower classes". It means the depth of independent review, testing and documentation is proportional to the safety significance: a Category A function receives the full programme, a Category C function a lighter one.

Document control

Every document has a unique number, a revision index, a status, a revision history and three signatures. The number encodes project, system, document type and sequence. The revision index distinguishes drafts (often letters A, B, C) from released issues (often numbers 0, 1, 2). The status moves through draft, for review, for approval, released, superseded. Only a released revision may be used for manufacturing or testing, and a superseded revision is withdrawn from use. Documents live in an electronic document management system whose workflow enforces author, checker and approver, and go to the customer with numbered transmittals. After commissioning, the drawings are updated to show what was actually installed, including every red-line correction made on site: this is the as-built revision, the one the plant will live with.

ElementExampleWhy it matters
Document numberNPP1-RPS-SDD-0001Unique identity across the project
Revision indexRev B (draft), Rev 1 (released)Everyone works on the same issue
Revision history"Rev 1: setpoint SG level changed to 24 %, see CR-0045"Changes are traceable to their reason
SignaturesAuthor, checker, approver with datesFour-eyes principle, accountability
StatusReleased / supersededPrevents work on an obsolete issue

Common mistake

Working from a copy on your own disk. A colleague releases revision 2, you keep editing revision 1, and two weeks later two inconsistent documents exist. Always open the controlled copy from the document management system and check its status first.

Configuration management

Configuration management (CM) answers the question "what exactly is the system, right now?" It identifies configuration items: documents, function diagrams, software modules, parameter sets, hardware modules with their types, serial numbers and firmware versions. It fixes baselines at milestones: a requirements baseline, a design baseline, a FAT baseline, an as-built baseline. Each item has a version, and the software load as a whole has an identifier and a checksum that the system itself can display, so a maintenance engineer can compare the running software with the released baseline. The central demand is consistency: at any moment the hardware installed, the software loaded and the documents released must describe the same system. CM also keeps the status accounting (the current list of all items and versions) and runs configuration audits: a physical audit compares the cabinet with the drawings, a functional audit compares the behaviour with the documents.

Change management

1  Change request (CR): who asks, what, why, source (customer, finding, obsolescence)
2  Classification: safety relevance, affected safety category, urgency
3  Impact analysis using the traceability matrix:
     requirements, design, function diagrams, hardware, parameters,
     documents, test cases, qualification, licensing, other systems
4  Approval by the change control board (customer and regulator if required)
5  Implementation under new document and software revisions
6  Re-verification: review, re-test, regression test
7  Update of all affected documents and of the baseline; CR closed with evidence

Why is a small change never small? Take the request to move the low steam generator level setpoint from 25 % to 24 %. The parameter itself is one number. But that number lives in the setpoint list, the SRS, the function diagram, the validation test specification, the test report, the display scaling, the operator procedures and the licensing documentation, and it must stay consistent with the safety analysis that justified it. The software load changes, so the system must be re-validated on the test bench and the modified software loaded into four divisions, each followed by a test. And because the same change is made identically in all four divisions, a mistake in it would be a common cause failure. That is why every change, however small, goes through the seven steps above. Temporary or urgent changes have their own, equally formal, procedure.

Non-conformances and independent assessment

A non-conformance report (NCR) is raised whenever a product or process does not meet a requirement: a wrong module delivered, a wiring error found at FAT, a review skipped, a test run with an unreleased procedure. The NCR describes the deviation, and a disposition is decided: use as is (with technical justification and usually customer approval), rework to the specification, repair to an accepted alternative, or reject. The root cause is analysed and a corrective action is defined to prevent recurrence. Trends in NCRs are reviewed by management. Independent assessment means that people outside the project line check that the processes are followed: internal quality audits, customer audits, and inspections by the regulator. The auditor does not ask whether the design is good; the auditor asks whether you can show the evidence that the process was followed.

Licensing documentation

The utility holds the licence and must demonstrate to the regulator that the new I&C system is safe. The supplier delivers the evidence: the safety demonstration of the system, compliance matrices to the standards (IEC 61513, IEC 60880, IEC 60987), qualification reports (environmental, seismic, EMC), the V&V reports, failure mode and effects analyses, reliability and common cause failure analyses, the cybersecurity assessment and references to the generic assessments the platform has received in other countries. The regulator may ask questions through a formal request process and may witness FAT and SAT. Every answer becomes part of the licensing record, which is one more reason to write documents that a stranger can understand.

A short case study

During SAT a technician notices that the relay output module in division 2 shows a firmware version different from divisions 1, 3 and 4. Investigation shows that after FAT a defective module was replaced from stock without a change request; the spare carried a newer firmware. An NCR is raised. A configuration audit of all cabinets follows and finds no other deviation. The supplier analyses the firmware difference, performs a regression test on the test bench, and the customer accepts a "use as is" disposition with the documents updated to the real versions. Root cause: spare parts stock had no version control. Corrective action: version labels on all spares and a procedure requiring a CR for any module replacement. Nothing had failed; the configuration had simply become unknown, and in a safety system "unknown" is already a defect.

Tip

For every impact analysis, use a fixed checklist: requirements, design, function diagrams, hardware, parameters, documents, tests, qualification, licensing, training and procedures, other systems. The checklist is what stops you from forgetting the operator procedure.

Interview angle

"The customer asks you to change one setpoint quickly. What do you do?" The interviewer is testing whether you will say "I just change it". Describe the change request, the impact analysis with the traceability matrix, approval, re-verification and documentation, and explain the common cause risk of a change that goes into all four divisions.

Key terms

ISO 9001
The generic international standard for quality management systems: processes, responsibilities, document control, audits and corrective actions.
IAEA GSR Part 2
The IAEA safety requirement on leadership and management for safety, demanding an integrated management system with safety as the overriding priority and a graded approach.
Project quality plan
The document that applies the company management system to one project: organisation, standards, document list, review rules, V&V and configuration management plans, audits.
Document control
The system of unique numbers, revision indexes, statuses, signatures and transmittals that ensures everyone works on the released issue.
As-built
The final revision of drawings and documents updated to show exactly what was installed and commissioned at the plant.
Configuration item
Anything whose version must be controlled: a document, a function diagram, a software load, a parameter set, a hardware module with its firmware.
Baseline
A frozen, approved set of configuration item versions at a milestone, against which later changes are measured.
Change request
The formal start of any modification, stating what should change and why, followed by impact analysis and approval.
Impact analysis
The systematic identification of everything affected by a change, using the traceability matrix and a fixed checklist.
Non-conformance report (NCR)
The record of a deviation from a requirement, with its disposition (use as is, rework, repair, reject), root cause and corrective action.

Say it in English

  • "Our work follows the project quality plan, which applies the company management system and the customer's nuclear quality requirements to this project."
  • "I only work on the released revision from the document management system, and every change is described in the revision history with its reason."
  • "Configuration management keeps hardware, software and documents consistent at every baseline."
  • "A change starts with a change request and an impact analysis; it is implemented under new revisions and re-verified, including regression tests."
  • "A small change is never small, because the same modification goes into all four divisions and touches documents from the setpoint list to the licensing file."

Check yourself

What is a project quality plan?

It defines organisation, standards, documents, review rules, V&V and configuration management for that specific project.

A colleague released revision 2 of the SDD while you continued editing your local copy of revision 1. What is this?

Only the controlled, released revision may be worked on; local copies lead to two versions of the truth.

What does the "configuration triangle" require?

Consistency between the three corners is the core demand of configuration management.

Which step comes immediately after a change request is classified?

Nothing is approved or implemented before the impact on requirements, design, documents, tests and licensing is known.

A module was replaced after FAT without a change request and now has a different firmware version. What is the correct reaction?

An unknown configuration is itself a defect in a safety system; the NCR process restores known, documented consistency.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

The customer asks you to change a setpoint quickly. What do you do?

I would explain that even a one-number change goes through the change process, and then make that process fast rather than skip it. I would open a change request with the reason, classify it as safety-relevant, and run an impact analysis with the traceability matrix: setpoint list, SRS, function diagram, validation test cases, display, operator procedures, licensing documents and consistency with the safety analysis. After approval by the change control board and the customer, the change is implemented under new revisions, re-verified by review and re-test with a regression set, and all documents and the baseline are updated. I would also point out that the change goes into all four divisions, so an error would be a common cause failure.

What is configuration management and why is it important in nuclear I&C?

Configuration management means knowing exactly what the system is at any moment: which document revisions, which function diagram versions, which software load with its checksum, which hardware modules with which firmware. We fix baselines at milestones and control every change against them. It is important because the safety case rests on the evidence we produced: the tests were run on a specific software version in a specific hardware configuration. If the plant runs something else, even slightly, the evidence no longer applies. So hardware, software and documents must stay consistent, and configuration audits check that the cabinet matches the drawings and the behaviour matches the documents.

What do you do if you find a non-conformance?

I raise a non-conformance report immediately, describing the deviation factually: what was required, what was found, where, and in which configuration. I do not try to fix it quietly, because the value of the NCR is that the deviation becomes visible and is dispositioned by the right people. The disposition can be use as is with a technical justification, rework, repair or reject, and the customer is usually involved for anything safety-relevant. Then the root cause is analysed and a corrective action is defined so that the same deviation does not happen again. In the case I know, a module swap without a change request led to version labels on all spares and a new procedure.

How does your work support the licensing of the system?

The utility holds the licence, and the regulator needs evidence that the system is safe. My documents are part of that evidence. The requirements specification and the design description show what the system does and why, the compliance matrices show how we meet IEC 61513 and IEC 60880, and the traceability matrix shows that every requirement was implemented and tested. Qualification reports, V&V reports and the analyses for failure modes, reliability and common cause failure complete the file. Because the regulator may ask formal questions years later, I write every document so that a stranger can follow the reasoning without asking me.

How do you make sure you are working on the right version of a document?

I only open documents from the electronic document management system, never from a local folder or an e-mail attachment, and I check the status and revision index before I start. If the status is "released" and I need to change it, I create the next revision inside the system so that the workflow with author, checker and approver applies. I describe every change in the revision history with its reason, typically a change request or a finding number, so a reader can see why the document moved. Before a review or a test, I confirm that all input documents are the released revisions listed in the baseline. It is a small habit that prevents large inconsistencies.

Day 3 · Hour 8

Cybersecurity, communication and human factors, with the Day 3 review

Three topics that cut across all the earlier hours: how to keep attackers out of a safety system, how data moves between units and plant systems, and how to design for the human in the control room. The hour ends with the ten most important ideas of Day 3. Expect at least one cybersecurity question in any modern I&C interview.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Explain zones, conduits and defence in depth as applied to cybersecurity in nuclear I&C
  • Explain why one-way gateways and "no remote access" are used for safety systems
  • Compare point-to-point links, buses and Ethernet, and explain deterministic communication and time stamping
  • Describe good alarm management and the main human factors principles for a control room HMI
  • Summarise the ten key ideas of Day 3 in your own words

Cybersecurity: standards and mindset

Safety engineering protects against random failures and defined hazards; cybersecurity protects against an intelligent adversary who looks for the one path you forgot. The general industrial standard is the IEC 62443 series for the security of industrial automation and control systems; it introduces zones, conduits and security levels for asset owners, integrators and product suppliers. For nuclear plants, IEC 62645 defines the security programme for computer-based I&C systems, and the IAEA Nuclear Security Series gives guidance, notably NSS No. 17-T (computer security techniques for nuclear facilities) and NSS No. 33-T (computer security of I&C systems). The design mindset that follows from them: assume the non-safety network is already compromised, and make sure that this cannot matter for the safety function.

Zones, conduits and defence in depth

A zone is a group of assets with the same security requirements; a conduit is a controlled communication path between zones. The safety systems sit in the most protected zone, operational control in a less protected one, the office network far outside. Defence in depth, known from Day 2 as a safety concept, is applied again: physical access control to rooms and cabinets; network segmentation; one-way data flow out of the safety zone; hardening of every device; monitoring and logging; and administrative procedures for everything that touches the system. No single measure is trusted alone.

The one-way gateway or data diode is the key element of the conduit out of the safety zone. A data diode is hardware that physically allows transmission in one direction only, for example a fibre link with a transmitter on the safety side and only a receiver on the other. The safety system sends process values and diagnostics for display and archiving, but no packet, acknowledgement or command can travel back. This also satisfies the requirement of Hour 3 that nothing on the non-safety side can influence a safety function.

Protective measures for safety systems

  • No remote access, no wireless, no internet. Maintenance happens locally, by authorised persons.
  • Protected service interface. The engineering or service computer connects only locally, under administrative control: locked cabinet, key switch or physical enable for any write access, two-person rule, and logging of every session.
  • Hardening. Safety processors run a dedicated, minimal runtime environment, not a general-purpose operating system; unused ports and services do not exist; the configuration is fixed and its integrity is checked with checksums of the software load.
  • Removable media control. Any USB device is scanned on a dedicated station before it comes near the system; many plants forbid them entirely in safety areas.
  • Secure development environment. The engineering tools and code generator are themselves protected, because a manipulated tool could insert errors into every function diagram.
  • Supply chain. Modules and software come from verified sources with controlled manufacturing, so nothing is tampered with before it arrives.

Common mistake

Treating a safety system like an office computer: "just install the security patch". A patch is a software change, and in a safety system it goes through the full change process with re-verification. That is why safety platforms minimise the attack surface by design instead of relying on patching.

Communication in plain English

TechnologyWhat it isWhere it fits
Point-to-point linkA dedicated cable or fibre between exactly two devicesSafety systems: isolated, deterministic, no shared element
Fieldbus (e.g. Profibus)Several devices share one serial cable; a master polls the slaves in a fixed cycleOperational control, remote I/O; deterministic if the cycle is fixed
ModbusA simple, open request-response protocol over serial lines or TCP/IPInterfaces to third-party equipment and gateways
Ethernet (TCP/IP)The office network technology with switches, high bandwidthNon-safety DCS and plant computer; not deterministic by itself

For safety, communication must be deterministic: telegrams are sent cyclically with fixed content and length, so the receiver knows when the next one must arrive. Each telegram carries a sender identification, a sequence number and a checksum; the receiver checks all three plus a time-out. If a telegram is late, corrupted or missing, the receiver falls back to a defined safe value (for a partial trip, usually TRUE). The link itself need not be perfect; the end-to-end checks make its failures detectable. Time stamping gives every event a time at its source, typically with about 1 ms resolution, from a clock synchronised across the plant. After a transient, the sequence of events shows which signal came first. The time signal enters the safety system in a way that cannot affect the safety function: a wrong clock may spoil the event list, never the trip.

Human factors: the operator is part of the system

Human factors engineering designs the system around how people perceive, decide and act; IEC 60964 (control room design) and ISO 11064 (ergonomic design of control centres) apply. Three principles dominate. First, the operator must understand the plant state at a glance: displays are organised in a hierarchy from overview to system to component, with consistent colours, symbols and units, and a dedicated safety parameter display. Second, alarm management: an alarm is a signal that requires an operator action; everything else is information. Alarms are prioritised (often three levels), consequential alarms are suppressed, and the first alarm of an event stays visible. Guidance such as EEMUA 191 sets targets to avoid alarm floods: less than about one alarm per ten minutes in steady operation, and a manageable rate after an upset. Third, human error prevention: critical commands use a select-then-confirm sequence, interlocks prevent dangerous combinations, every action gives clear feedback, and automatic protection acts without operator intervention in the first minutes of an event, so the human has time to think rather than being forced to react.

Tip

When you design a display or an alarm list, ask for each item: "what does the operator do when they see this?" If the answer is "nothing", it is information, not an alarm, and it should not flash.

Interview angle

A typical question: "How do you protect a safety system against cyber attacks?" Answer in layers: zones with the safety system in the most protected one, one-way gateway out, no remote access, hardened minimal platform, controlled local service access, secure development environment, and change control for any software. Name IEC 62443 and IEC 62645.

Day 3 review: the ten most important ideas

  1. A safety I&C system is engineered through the IEC 61513 lifecycle, drawn as a V: specifications go down the left leg, tests come up the right leg, and each test level verifies the specification level opposite to it.
  2. The Design Engineer works on the left leg: requirements (SRS), architecture and sizing (SDD), function diagrams, hardware documents and interfaces. Independent V&V works on the right leg.
  3. A good requirement is atomic, unambiguous, verifiable, necessary, consistent and traceable, written as a "shall" sentence with a number, a unit and a verification method.
  4. Traceability links every requirement to its source, its design elements and its test cases, in both directions.
  5. Architecture follows the non-functional requirements: four independent divisions, isolated fibre links for voting, one-way flow to the non-safety side, and sizing with spare capacity.
  6. Application software is drawn as function diagrams from a qualified block library and generated automatically; deterministic execution forbids dynamic memory, unbounded loops and interrupts in the application logic.
  7. Hardware design means redundant fused power, a plant-wide grounding concept, separation of divisions and classes, qualified modules and cabinets qualified for environment, seismic loads and EMC.
  8. Verification asks "did we build it right?" at every step; validation asks "did we build the right thing?" against the requirements, on a test bench with a simulator, by an independent team.
  9. A test case has preconditions, numbered steps, expected results with tolerances, pass/fail criteria and traceability; a finding is categorised, analysed, fixed, re-tested and regression-tested before closure.
  10. Quality, configuration and change management keep hardware, software and documents consistent; a small change is never small, and security is designed in through zones, one-way gateways and no remote access.

Key terms

IEC 62443
The series of standards for cybersecurity of industrial automation and control systems, introducing zones, conduits and security levels.
IEC 62645
The nuclear standard for security programmes of computer-based I&C systems.
Zone and conduit
A zone is a group of assets with the same security requirements; a conduit is a controlled communication path between zones.
Data diode
A hardware device that physically allows data to pass in one direction only, used to let data out of the safety zone without any way back in.
Hardening
Reducing the attack surface of a device: minimal software, no unused services or ports, fixed configuration with integrity checks.
Deterministic communication
Cyclic telegrams of fixed content checked for sender, sequence and integrity, with a time-out and a defined safe value on failure.
Time stamping
Marking each event with the time at its source from a synchronised clock, so the sequence of events after a transient can be reconstructed.
Alarm
A signal that requires an operator action; information that needs no action should not be presented as an alarm.
Alarm flood
A burst of alarms during an upset that exceeds what an operator can read and act on, which good alarm management prevents by prioritisation and suppression.
Human factors engineering
Designing displays, controls and procedures around human perception, decision and action to prevent errors and support the operator.

Say it in English

  • "The safety system sits in the most protected zone; data leaves through a one-way gateway and there is no remote access at all."
  • "Service access is local only, physically enabled, logged and under the two-person rule."
  • "Safety communication is cyclic and deterministic: each telegram is checked for sender, sequence number and checksum, and a missing telegram falls back to a safe value."
  • "An alarm must require an operator action; otherwise it is information and belongs on a display, not in the alarm list."
  • "We apply defence in depth to cybersecurity as we do to safety: physical, network, device and procedural layers."

Check yourself

What does a data diode do?

A data diode is hardware with a transmitter on one side and only a receiver on the other, so nothing can travel back into the safety system.

Why is a security patch not simply installed on a safety processor?

A patch is a modification of qualified software; it needs impact analysis, approval, testing and documentation like any other change.

Which checks make safety communication failures detectable?

These end-to-end checks let the receiver detect late, missing, corrupted or misrouted telegrams and fall back to a safe value.

According to good alarm management, what defines an alarm?

If no action is needed, the signal is information and should not be presented as an alarm, which keeps the alarm list meaningful.

Which statement about Ethernet is correct for safety I&C?

Standard Ethernet with switches and retransmissions has no guaranteed timing; safety data exchange uses dedicated links with cyclic, checked telegrams.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

How do you protect a safety I&C system against cyber attacks?

In layers, following IEC 62443 and the nuclear standard IEC 62645. The safety system is placed in the most protected zone. Data leaves that zone only through a one-way gateway or data diode, so the non-safety network can be compromised without any path back into the safety processors. There is no remote access, no wireless and no internet connection. The service interface is local, physically enabled by a key switch, used under the two-person rule and logged. The platform itself is hardened: a minimal dedicated runtime, no unused services, fixed configuration with integrity checks. The development environment and the supply chain are protected too, and every software change goes through change control.

What is the difference between a bus and a point-to-point link, and which do safety systems use?

A point-to-point link connects exactly two devices with a dedicated cable or fibre; a bus lets several devices share one medium, usually with a master polling the slaves. Buses save cable and are fine for operational control, but a shared medium is a shared failure point and timing depends on the number of participants. Safety systems prefer point-to-point links, especially fibre between divisions, because they are electrically isolated, their timing is fixed and a fault affects only one connection. Whatever the medium, safety communication is cyclic with fixed telegrams checked for sender, sequence and checksum, and a missing telegram leads to a defined safe value at the receiver.

What does deterministic communication mean?

It means the receiver knows exactly when the next message must arrive and what it must look like. Telegrams are sent cyclically with fixed content and length. Each one carries a sender identification, a sequence number and a checksum, and the receiver checks all three and applies a time-out. If a telegram is late, duplicated, corrupted or comes from the wrong sender, the receiver detects it and uses a predefined safe value, for example treating a missing partial trip as a trip demand. Standard Ethernet with switches and retransmissions does not give this guarantee by itself, which is why safety systems use dedicated links or protocols with these end-to-end checks.

What is good alarm management?

Good alarm management starts from one definition: an alarm is something that requires an operator action. Everything else is information and belongs on a display, not in the alarm list. Alarms are prioritised, usually in about three levels, so the operator sees what matters first. Consequential alarms, for example all the low-flow alarms that follow a pump trip, are suppressed or grouped, and the first alarm of an event stays visible. Guidance such as EEMUA 191 gives rate targets: well under one alarm per ten minutes in steady state, and a manageable rate during an upset. The aim is to avoid alarm floods, in which an operator cannot read, let alone act.

Summarise what a Design Engineer for safety I&C does, in one minute.

I turn the customer's plant-level requirements into a safety I&C system that can be licensed. I read the P&IDs, I/O lists, logic diagrams and setpoint lists and write the system requirements specification in verifiable language. I design the architecture and size it: divisions, cabinets, I/O with spare capacity, communication and interfaces, documented in the system design description. I design the application software as function diagrams and support the hardware design of cabinets and power distribution. I keep traceability from requirement to test, answer findings from independent V&V, and run every modification through change and configuration management. And I write everything so that a reviewer, a regulator or my successor can follow it.

Day 4 · Hour 1

How the interview works and how to open it well

This hour explains what happens in each interview round at an engineering company in Slovakia and gives you a 90-second self-introduction you can deliver without notes. Interviewers form a strong opinion in the first five minutes, so this is where preparation pays off most.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Describe the typical rounds of a hiring process and what each round tests in a junior engineer
  • Deliver a 90-second "Tell me about yourself" using the present, past, why this role, why now structure
  • Handle a question you cannot answer by reasoning aloud instead of bluffing
  • Use a whiteboard or a sheet of paper to support a technical explanation
  • Apply the basic rules of body language and video-call etiquette

The typical process in Slovakia

Most engineering companies in Slovakia, including international companies with an engineering centre in Bratislava, run a process with three or four steps. It usually takes two to five weeks from the first call to an offer. The order can vary, but expect the pattern below.

RoundWhoLengthWhat they assessWhat to prepare
HR screening callRecruiter or HR partner20 to 30 min, phone or videoCV facts, English level, motivation, salary range, notice period, start dateYour 90-second introduction, a salary range, your availability
Technical interviewTeam lead and a senior engineer60 to 90 minFundamentals, structured thinking, rigour, honesty about limits, how you learnDays 1 to 3, the drills of Hours 2 and 3, whiteboard practice
Written test or caseSent by e-mail or done on site30 to 60 minReading a diagram, writing a requirement, a small calculation, documentation styleHour 4 exercises
Final roundDepartment manager, sometimes with HR30 to 45 minFit with the team, long-term motivation, contract detailsYour questions, your five-year answer, Hour 7 logistics

In the HR call the recruiter is usually not an engineer. Keep technical words simple and give clear facts: what you studied, when you can start, what salary range you expect. In the technical interview a senior engineer will often ask "why" three times in a row. This is not aggression. It is how engineers check whether you understand something or only remember it.

What they want from a junior

Nobody expects a graduate to know TELEPERM XS or IEC 61513 in detail; the company will train you for months. What they cannot train easily is the list below, and that is what the technical interview measures.

  • Fundamentals. Ohm's law, what a 4 to 20 mA signal is, what a reactor trip does, what redundancy means.
  • Structured thinking. You answer in a visible order: claim, reason, example, limit. The interviewer can follow you even when you are wrong.
  • Rigour. You notice units, ranges and edge cases. You ask "what happens if the sensor fails?" before anyone asks you.
  • Honesty about limits. You say "I do not know" and then reason from first principles. In a safety culture this is called a questioning attitude, and it is valued more than a quick guess.
  • English. Understandable, not perfect. Short sentences and clear technical terms count more than grammar.
  • Motivation for long projects. A nuclear I&C project runs for years. They want someone who will still be there when the system is commissioned at the plant.

Interview angle

When the senior engineer asks a basic question such as "What is a trip?", they are not testing knowledge, they are testing whether you can explain simply. Answer as if speaking to a smart colleague from another field: one sentence of definition, one sentence of purpose, one concrete example.

The 90-second "Tell me about yourself"

This is the first question in almost every round. Ninety seconds is about 180 to 200 spoken words. The structure that works best is present, past, why this role, why now. Present: who you are today, one or two sentences. Past: your education, your thesis and one or two relevant projects, three or four sentences. Why this role: two sentences linking what you can do to what safety I&C needs. Why now: one sentence. Finish with a sentence that invites the next question. Fill in the template and practise it aloud.

PRESENT   I am a [degree] graduate in [field] from [university].
          Right now I am [finishing my thesis / working as ... / looking for ...].
PAST      During my studies I focused on [control, electronics, measurement].
          My thesis was about [topic], where I [designed / built / tested ...].
          I also [internship / student project / lab work], which taught me [skill].
WHY ROLE  I want to work on safety I&C because [reason linked to rigour,
          documentation, systems that must not fail].
          I am comfortable with [logic, signals, writing structured documents].
WHY NOW   I am available from [date] and I am looking for a long-term position
          where I can grow with the team.
CLOSE     I would be happy to go into any of these points in more detail.

A complete sample for a graduate with no nuclear experience: "I am an electrical engineering graduate from the Slovak University of Technology. During my studies I focused on automation and measurement. My thesis was a PLC-based control and monitoring system for a small laboratory heat exchanger: I specified the sensors, designed the logic in function block diagrams, wrote the test procedure and documented the whole system. Before that I had a summer internship in an electrical design office, where I learned how to read wiring diagrams and keep documents under revision control. I want to work in safety I&C because I like systems where every signal has to be justified and every function has to be tested. I enjoy writing clear technical documents, and I am used to working in English from my courses and from my thesis supervisor. I am available from the first of next month and I am looking for a long-term position in a team where I can learn from experienced engineers."

Tip

Record yourself on your phone and time it. If you are over two minutes, cut the past section first. If you are under one minute, add one concrete number or one named tool to the thesis sentence.

When you cannot answer

You will be asked at least one question you cannot answer. This is planned: the interviewer wants to see what you do under uncertainty. Follow four steps and never skip the last one.

  1. Say what you do know. "I have not worked with ESFAS directly, but I know it is the system that actuates safety features such as safety injection."
  2. Reason aloud from principles. "If it has to work after a single failure, I would expect redundant trains and some form of voting, similar to the reactor protection system."
  3. Ask a clarifying question. "Are you asking about the actuation logic or about how the actuators are controlled?"
  4. Name the limit. "That is where my knowledge ends. I would have to check the standard or ask a colleague for the rest."

Common mistake

Bluffing. A senior engineer recognises an invented answer within seconds, and in a nuclear company it is a safety-culture red flag, not a small mistake. "I do not know, but here is how I would find out" is a strong answer. A confident wrong answer is the weakest answer possible.

Using a whiteboard or paper

Ask "May I draw this?" and then draw before you talk. Build the picture from left to right in the direction of the signal: sensor, transmitter, input module, processing, voting, output, actuator. Use boxes and arrows only, label every box with one or two words, and say what you are drawing while you draw it. If you make a mistake, cross it out cleanly and continue, do not erase everything. On a video call, hold a sheet of paper to the camera for two seconds or describe the structure in words: "On the left we have four pressure channels, in the middle the 2oo4 voter, on the right the trip breakers."

Body language and video calls

Sit upright, keep your hands visible and still, and look at the person who asked the question.
