Speak a little slower than feels natural, especially in English; a pause of two seconds before a technical answer sounds thoughtful, not slow. If you did not understand, ask immediately rather than guessing. For a video call: camera at eye level, light in front of you and not behind you, headphones with a microphone, a quiet room, a neutral background. Join five minutes early, test audio, and have paper, a pen and a glass of water ready. If the connection drops, rejoin calmly and summarise in one sentence where you were.

Note

Bring a printed CV, a notebook and two questions written down. Writing a short note while the interviewer speaks shows attention. Reading long notes while you answer does not, so keep your notes to keywords.

Key terms

HR screening call
A short first conversation with a recruiter to check basic facts, English level, salary expectations and availability before engineers spend time on you.
Technical interview
The main round, led by engineers, which tests fundamentals, structured thinking and how you reason under uncertainty.
Case or written test
A short practical task, for example reading a function diagram or writing a requirement, used to see how you work rather than what you remember.
Hiring manager
The person who will be your boss and who makes the final decision, usually the team lead or department manager.
Clarifying question
A question you ask the interviewer to make sure you understood what they want before you answer.
Probing question
A follow-up question, often "why?", used by interviewers to check whether your understanding is real or memorised.
Bluffing
Giving a confident answer you have invented. In a safety culture this is treated as a serious warning sign.
Questioning attitude
A safety-culture habit of asking "is this right, and how do we know?" instead of assuming. Interviewers reward it.
Signposting
Telling the listener the structure of your answer before you give it, for example "There are three reasons. First ...".

Say it in English

  • "Before I answer, may I check that I understood the question correctly?"
  • "I have not worked with that directly, but my understanding is the following, and I will tell you where my knowledge ends."
  • "Let me draw the signal path from left to right, from the sensor to the actuator."
  • "I am looking for a long-term position where I can grow with the team on a multi-year project."
  • "That is a good question. Let me think for a second before I answer."

Check yourself

In the HR screening call, what is the recruiter mainly checking?

The recruiter is usually not an engineer; the screening call filters on basic facts, communication and expectations before engineers get involved.

What is the recommended order for a 90-second self-introduction?

Starting with the present gives context, the past gives evidence, and the two "why" parts link you to the job and to the moment.

An interviewer asks about a system you have never heard of. What is the best response?

The four-step method shows honesty and reasoning, which is exactly what the interviewer is testing with an unknown question.

When drawing a signal chain on a whiteboard, which habit is recommended?

Left-to-right signal flow with labels and commentary lets the interviewer follow your thinking as it develops.

Why do interviewers at nuclear companies care so much about motivation for long projects?

Safety I&C projects last several years; losing a trained engineer midway is expensive, so stability is a real hiring criterion.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

Tell me about yourself.

I am an electrical engineering graduate with a focus on automation and measurement. My master's thesis was a PLC-based control and monitoring system for a laboratory heat exchanger, where I specified the sensors, designed the logic in function block diagrams and wrote the test procedure. I also did an internship in an electrical design office, which taught me to read wiring diagrams and work under revision control. I am interested in safety I&C because I like systems where every signal and every function must be justified and tested. I am available from next month and I am looking for a long-term role where I can learn from experienced engineers.

What do you think this role actually involves day to day?

My understanding is that a design engineer for safety I&C spends most of the day on documents and diagrams rather than on hardware. That means collecting functional and non-functional needs from the customer, writing specifications for the system architecture, the I/O, the safety functions and their interfaces, defining how the system is monitored and periodically tested, and then reviewing documents written by colleagues. There is also coordination with software, hardware and V&V teams, and sometimes travel to the plant. I expect the first months to be mostly learning the platform, the standards and the document templates.

How do you react when you do not know the answer to a technical question?

I say so, and then I try to be useful anyway. First I state what I do know about the topic, so you can see where I am starting from. Then I reason aloud from basic principles, for example from redundancy, fail-safe behaviour or signal flow, and I make clear which parts are reasoning and which are facts. If the question is ambiguous I ask a clarifying question. Finally I say where my knowledge ends and how I would find the answer, usually by checking the standard or asking a senior colleague. In a safety environment I think a wrong confident answer is worse than an honest gap.

Can you explain a simple control loop to me on the whiteboard?

Yes. On the left I draw the process, for example a tank with a level. A level transmitter measures it and sends a 4 to 20 mA signal to the controller. The controller compares the measured value with the setpoint, calculates the error, and uses a PID algorithm to produce an output. The output goes as another 4 to 20 mA signal to the control valve on the inlet, which changes the flow. The flow changes the level, and the loop closes. If the level is too high, the controller closes the valve; if too low, it opens it. I would also mark where the signal could fail and what the valve does on loss of signal.

What is your biggest strength for this position?

I would say structured rigour. When I write a document or design a piece of logic, I like to make every item traceable: where did this requirement come from, how will we test it, what happens if the input is wrong. In my thesis I kept a table that linked every function to its test case, and it caught two functions I had implemented but never tested. I know that is a small example compared with a nuclear project, but I think the habit is the same. The second strength is that I ask questions early instead of guessing, which I understand is important in a safety culture.

Day 4 · Hour 2

Technical drill I: fundamentals of plant, signals and logic

Twenty-five fundamental questions with model answers, covering what you learned on Day 1. The technical interview starts with questions like these, and a weak answer here ends the interview early, so the goal is fluent, structured answers in 60 to 90 seconds each.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Answer any fundamental question using the claim, reason, example, limit structure
  • Give at least one correct number or concrete example for each of the 25 topics
  • Keep a spoken technical answer between 60 and 90 seconds
  • Identify your own weak topics and mark them for tomorrow's review

How to drill

Do not read the model answers first. Cover them, read the question aloud, start a timer, and answer aloud in English for 60 to 90 seconds. Then open the model answer and compare. Mark each question green, yellow or red: green if your claim matched and you gave at least one correct number or example; yellow if the claim was right but the answer was thin; red if you were wrong or blank. Tomorrow morning you repeat only the red and yellow ones. The model answers are not scripts to memorise; they show the depth a senior engineer expects from a junior: short, correct, with one number and one limit.

The four-part technical answer

Every technical answer follows the same skeleton. Claim: one sentence that answers the question directly. Reason: why it is so, or what it is for. Example: a number, a plant situation or a device. Limit: a boundary, an exception, or where your knowledge ends. The limit is the part juniors skip, and it is the part that makes you sound like an engineer.

PartWhat you sayExample: "What is live zero?"
ClaimDirect one-sentence answer"Live zero means 0 percent of the range is 4 mA, not 0 mA."
ReasonWhy, or what for"So a broken wire, which gives 0 mA, can be told apart from a genuinely low value."
ExampleA number, a case, a device"Below about 3.6 mA the input module declares the channel failed."
LimitBoundary or exception"It does not catch a transmitter drifting inside the range; for that you compare redundant channels."

Interview angle

Interviewers listen for the claim in your first sentence. If you start with background and history, they lose patience and interrupt. Answer first, explain second. Then stop and let them ask the follow-up.

Pace and length

Ninety seconds is about 150 to 180 words at a careful non-native pace. If you are still talking after two minutes, you are explaining more than was asked. If you finish in twenty seconds, you have given a claim with no reason. Say the numbers slowly and with units: "four to twenty milliamps", "about fifteen point five megapascals". Pause after the claim; it gives you time to choose the reason and gives the interviewer a chance to redirect.

Numbers to have ready

Tip

Have a short list of numbers ready that you can drop into any answer: 4 to 20 mA with fault limits near 3.6 and 21 mA; primary pressure about 15.5 MPa at about 300 °C; decay heat about 6 to 7 percent of power right after shutdown; 24 V DC cabinet supply; 2oo4 voting; about ten decades of neutron flux. One correct number is worth a paragraph of adjectives.

Common mistake

Trying to say everything you know. The interviewer asked one question. Give the four parts and stop; if they want more, they will ask, and the follow-up tells you what they care about.

Key terms

Live zero
A signal range whose lowest valid value is not zero, such as 4 mA for 0 percent, so that a dead signal (0 mA) is distinguishable from a low measurement.
Pt100
A platinum resistance thermometer with 100 ohms at 0 °C; the standard RTD for accurate temperature measurement.
Hysteresis
A deliberate gap between the switch-on and switch-off thresholds of a comparator to prevent chattering on a noisy signal.
Latch (seal-in)
A memory element that holds a trip or command once set until it is deliberately reset, even if the triggering condition disappears.
On-delay timer
A timer that passes a true input only after it has been true for a set time; it filters short spikes.
Voting logic
Logic that combines redundant channels so that an action occurs only when a defined number agree, for example two out of four.
Fail-safe position
The position an actuator moves to on loss of power or signal, chosen so that the failure moves the plant toward safety.
Priority logic
Logic at the actuator that decides which command wins when safety, operational and manual commands conflict; the safety command has priority.
Decay heat
Heat released by radioactive fission products after shutdown, about 6 to 7 percent of full power at first, which must be removed for days.

Say it in English

  • "Let me answer directly first and then give you the reason and an example."
  • "The signal is four to twenty milliamps, and anything below about three point six milliamps is treated as a wire break."
  • "With two out of four voting, one channel can be in test and the system still tolerates a single failure."
  • "The valve fails closed on loss of air, because closed is the safe direction for a steam isolation valve."
  • "That is the limit of what I know from first principles; the exact value would be in the project specification."

Check yourself

A 4 to 20 mA input reads 0 mA. What is the most likely meaning?

With live zero, 0 percent is 4 mA; a reading of 0 mA cannot be a valid process value and indicates a loop fault.

Why is 2oo4 voting preferred over 2oo3 in many protection systems?

Four channels keep full single-failure tolerance during testing of one channel; three channels do not.

Which statement about a Pt100 RTD compared with a thermocouple is correct?

RTDs are accurate and stable to roughly 600 °C; thermocouples are faster and reach higher temperatures but are less accurate and need cold junction compensation.

What is the purpose of hysteresis in a trip comparator?

Hysteresis separates the set and reset thresholds so that noise around the setpoint does not switch the output on and off repeatedly.

Decay heat immediately after shutdown is roughly what fraction of full power?

Fission products keep decaying after shutdown; the heat starts around 6 to 7 percent and falls to about 1 percent after an hour, so cooling must continue.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

What is nuclear fission and where does the heat come from?

Fission is the splitting of a heavy nucleus, mainly uranium-235, after it absorbs a neutron. The nucleus breaks into two lighter fragments and releases two or three new neutrons and about 200 MeV of energy. Most of that energy is the kinetic energy of the fragments, which stop inside the fuel and heat it. The new neutrons can cause further fissions, which is the chain reaction, and control rods and boron absorb neutrons to control it. The heat passes from the fuel to the coolant and on to the steam generators.

What is decay heat and why does it matter for I&C?

After the reactor is shut down the fission products are still radioactive and keep releasing energy. Immediately after shutdown this decay heat is about 6 to 7 percent of full power, falling to about 1 percent after an hour and below about 0.5 percent after a day. It cannot be switched off, so the core must be cooled long after any trip. For I&C this means the systems that start and monitor cooling, such as emergency feedwater or safety injection, must stay available for days, with their power supplies and measurements.

Describe the circuits of a PWR or VVER plant.

A pressurised water reactor has three circuits. The primary circuit carries water through the core at high pressure, about 15.5 MPa in a VVER-1000 and about 12.3 MPa in a VVER-440, so it does not boil at around 300 °C. The water flows through steam generators and back via the main coolant pumps, and a pressuriser keeps the pressure stable. The secondary circuit takes heat in the steam generators, produces steam at roughly 4 to 7 MPa, drives the turbine, condenses and returns as feedwater. The tertiary circuit is cooling water for the condenser.

What are the three barriers against the release of radioactivity?

The barriers are physical layers between the fission products and the environment. The first is the fuel cladding, the metal tube around the fuel pellets. The second is the primary circuit pressure boundary: the reactor vessel, pipes and steam generator tubes. The third is the containment, or the confinement with bubbler condenser on VVER-440 units. Some people count the ceramic fuel matrix as an extra barrier. Safety I&C exists to protect these barriers: a trip on low coolant flow protects the cladding, and containment isolation protects the third barrier.

What is I&C?

Instrumentation and control. Instrumentation is everything that measures the process: sensors, transmitters, signal conditioning and indication. Control is everything that acts on the process based on those measurements: controllers, protection logic, operator commands and the signals to actuators such as valves, pumps and breakers. In a nuclear plant I&C is often called the nervous system, because it connects the physical process to the operators and to the automatic safety functions. In the design role we specify how these systems are built, interfaced, monitored and tested.

What is the difference between operational and safety I&C?

Operational I&C runs the plant in normal operation: control loops, sequences, alarms, turbine control. It matters for availability, but its failure does not by itself endanger safety. Safety I&C protects the barriers when something goes wrong: the reactor protection system and the engineered safety features actuation. It is classified, redundant, qualified, independent from the operational systems and designed to fail safe. The two are separated so a fault in the operational system cannot disable protection, and when both command the same actuator the safety command has priority.

What is the difference between a DCS, a PLC and a safety platform?

A PLC is a programmable logic controller: a compact industrial computer that scans inputs, runs logic and sets outputs, originally for machines. A DCS is a distributed control system: a plant-wide set of controllers, networks, operator stations and engineering tools for continuous process control. A safety platform such as TELEPERM XS is qualified hardware and software designed for safety functions: deterministic cyclic processing, built-in self-monitoring, restricted communication, and a development process that meets nuclear standards such as IEC 60880. Technically it resembles a PLC; the qualification and the process make the difference.

Why do we use 4 to 20 mA signals, and what is live zero?

Current signals are used because they are not affected by cable resistance, resist electrical noise better than voltage signals, and can power a two-wire transmitter over the same pair. Live zero means that 0 percent of the range is 4 mA, not 0 mA. A reading of 0 mA therefore means a broken wire or a dead transmitter, not a low process value. Values below about 3.6 mA or above about 21 mA are treated as a fault by the input module, so the logic can distinguish a failed signal from a genuine low measurement.

RTD versus thermocouple: when would you use each?

An RTD, typically a Pt100, is a platinum resistor whose resistance rises with temperature. It is accurate, stable and nearly linear, but slower and limited to roughly 600 °C. A thermocouple is two wires of different metals producing a small voltage that depends on the junction temperature. It is fast, robust, cheap and works above 1000 °C, but is less accurate and needs cold junction compensation. In the primary circuit I would expect RTDs for accurate coolant temperature, and thermocouples where speed or high temperature matters, such as core exit temperatures.

How do you measure level with a differential pressure transmitter?

The transmitter measures the hydrostatic pressure of the liquid column: pressure equals density times gravity times height. The high-pressure side connects near the bottom of the vessel and the low-pressure side to the top, so the gas pressure cancels out. In a closed pressurised vessel such as a steam generator the low side has a reference leg filled with water, so the difference changes in the opposite direction and is compensated in the calculation. Density changes with temperature, so the calibration is valid for one condition and corrections apply for others. Impulse lines can block or freeze.

What are the neutron flux measurement ranges?

Power is proportional to neutron flux, but flux spans about ten decades from shutdown to full power, so no single detector covers all of it. The source range uses pulse counting at very low flux during start-up. The intermediate range uses a logarithmic scale across the middle decades and gives the rate of change, often expressed as reactor period. The power range uses ion chambers on a linear scale, typically 0 to about 120 percent power, for operation and the high flux trip. The ranges overlap so the protection system always has a valid measurement.

What does the fail-safe position of a valve mean?

It is the position the valve takes when it loses its power or control signal, usually driven by a spring. A fail-closed valve shuts on loss of air or current; a fail-open valve opens. Which one is safe depends on the function: a steam isolation valve should fail closed, while a valve in a cooling path should fail open or at least stay in position. Many motor-operated valves simply stay where they are, called fail-last. The designer chooses the position so that losing the signal moves the plant toward safety.

How are conflicting commands to an actuator resolved?

An actuator can receive commands from the protection system, the operational control system and the operator, so a priority logic decides which wins. The rule is that the higher safety class wins: a protection actuation cannot be overridden by an operational controller or an ordinary operator command. Within protection, the safe direction normally has priority, for example close over open for an isolation valve. In TELEPERM XS this is handled by dedicated priority modules close to the actuator. The priority rules are specified for each actuator in the design documents.

Why 24 V DC, and how are power supplies made redundant?

24 V DC is low enough to be safe to touch and is the standard supply for I&C modules, relays and transmitters. Redundancy means a cabinet is fed from two independent supplies, each able to carry the full load, coupled through diodes so one failed supply cannot pull the other down. Behind them sit battery-backed distributions, so the protection system keeps working during a loss of normal power. Each supply is monitored and a failure raises an alarm. Full loss of power is itself a defined safe state, because de-energise-to-trip means loss of power causes actuation.

What are the basic rules of grounding and EMC in I&C cabinets?

Grounding has two purposes: personal safety and a stable reference for signals. Cabinets have a protective earth and a separate instrument earth joined at one point, so no currents flow through signal paths. Cable shields are earthed, usually at one end for low-frequency signals and at both ends for high-frequency noise, according to the project rules. Power and signal cables are routed separately. Galvanic isolation in input modules prevents ground loops. EMC qualification tests the equipment for emissions and immunity, for example to surges and radio fields, so it works in a noisy plant.

What is a truth table and when do you use it?

A truth table lists every combination of binary inputs and the resulting output; for n inputs it has two to the power n rows. I use it to define a logic function unambiguously before implementation, to check for a missing case, and to derive test cases, because every row is a test. For a 2oo3 vote with inputs A, B and C the output is true in exactly four of the eight rows: any two inputs true, or all three. For large functions I combine small truth tables with a block diagram instead of one huge table.

What is a latch and why do protection systems use it?

A latch is a memory element with a set input and a reset input. When set goes true the output goes true and stays true after set returns to false, until reset is applied. Protection systems use latches so that a trip, once initiated, stays initiated until the operator deliberately resets it, even if the process parameter returns to normal. This is called seal-in. The designer must decide which input dominates if both are true; for safety, set usually dominates so a trip cannot be cancelled by a simultaneous reset.

What timer types do you know and where are they used?

The three basic timers are on-delay, off-delay and pulse. An on-delay timer passes a true input only after it has been true continuously for the set time; it filters short spikes and lets the process settle. An off-delay timer keeps the output true for a set time after the input goes false; it holds a command, for example keeping a pump running after the demand ends. A pulse timer produces a fixed-length output from an edge. In protection logic delays are kept short and justified, because every delay adds to the response time.

What is the difference between 2oo3 and 2oo4 voting?

Both are voting schemes where an action occurs when at least two channels demand it. With three channels, 2oo3 tolerates one failed channel in either direction: one spurious signal does not trip, and one stuck channel does not block the trip. But when one channel is in test the remaining pair gives only limited tolerance, so a second failure matters. With four channels, 2oo4 keeps full tolerance with one channel bypassed, because the remaining three still vote 2oo3. That is why four-train protection systems are common for the most important functions.

What is hysteresis in a limit comparator?

Hysteresis is a deliberate gap between the switch-on and switch-off thresholds of a comparator. If a trip threshold is 13.0 MPa, the signal might reset only when pressure rises back above 13.1 MPa. Without it, a noisy signal sitting at the threshold would switch on and off many times per second, which is called chattering; it stresses relays and confuses operators. The gap must be small enough not to delay the reset or hide a real change, and it is a documented design parameter. In protection logic it is usually combined with a latch anyway.

Explain PID control intuitively.

A PID controller produces an output from the error between setpoint and measurement. The proportional part reacts to the present error: bigger error, bigger correction, but a pure P controller always leaves a small steady offset. The integral part sums the error over time and slowly pushes the output until the offset disappears, but too much of it causes overshoot and oscillation. The derivative part reacts to how fast the error is changing and brakes early, but it amplifies noise, so it is often switched off. Tuning is a trade-off between speed and stability.

What is the difference between an alarm and a trip?

An alarm informs the operator that a value is abnormal and leaves the decision and the action to a human. A trip is an automatic action by the protection system, for example dropping the control rods or starting a pump, that does not wait for anyone. Alarms are usually set closer to the normal range so the operator has time to react before the trip threshold is reached. Alarms belong mostly to operational I&C and the human-machine interface; trips belong to safety I&C and must meet the full set of safety requirements.

How do you read a function block diagram?

I read it left to right, from inputs to outputs. On the left are the incoming signals, analogue measurements and binary states, each with a name, a unit and a range. Then come the processing blocks: limit comparators, timers, AND and OR gates, voters, latches. Each block has a type, inputs on the left, outputs on the right, and parameters such as setpoints or delays written next to it. On the right are outputs to actuators or to other diagrams, with cross-references. I check names, units, the sense of each signal, and what happens on a failed input.

What is the difference between a sensor and a transmitter?

A sensor is the element that physically responds to the process, for example a diaphragm that deflects under pressure or a platinum resistor whose resistance changes with temperature. A transmitter converts that small raw response into a standardised signal, typically 4 to 20 mA, and often adds linearisation, range setting, diagnostics and a local display. In practice they are packaged together and people say transmitter for the whole unit. In specifications I separate them, because accuracy, response time and qualification apply to the whole measuring chain, including impulse lines and cable.

What is galvanic isolation and why is it needed?

Galvanic isolation means there is no direct electrical conductor between two circuits; the signal crosses through a transformer, an optocoupler or a capacitor. It is needed so that a fault, a high voltage or a ground potential difference on one side cannot damage or disturb the other. In I&C we isolate between field signals and electronics, between redundant divisions, and between safety and non-safety systems, so a short circuit or a lightning surge in one place stays there. Isolation is what makes the independence required by the standards physically true, not just a line on a diagram.

Day 4 · Hour 3

Technical drill II: safety principles, standards and the engineering process

Twenty-five questions on the material of Days 2 and 3: safety principles, classification, platforms, the life cycle and quality. These are the questions that separate a candidate who studied I&C from one who understands why nuclear I&C is different.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Explain each safety principle with its purpose, one plant example and one limit
  • Name the standard or document where each principle is written down
  • Describe the engineering life cycle steps and the role of V&V, FAT, SAT and configuration management
  • Answer process questions in the first person, as the engineer who will do the work

Principles are "why" questions

Yesterday's drill asked "what is X". Today's questions are mostly "why do we do X" and "what happens if we do not". The claim, reason, example, limit structure still works, but the reason becomes the heart of the answer. For a principle, the reason is always a failure that the principle prevents. Redundancy exists because single components fail. Diversity exists because identical components can fail together. Independence exists because failures spread. If you can name the failure, you understand the principle.

PartExample: "What is the single failure criterion?"
Claim"The system must still perform its safety function after any single random failure."
Reason"Because components do fail, and a protection system that depends on one component is not protection."
Example"Four pressure channels with 2oo4 voting: one channel can fail high or low and the trip still works."
Limit"It covers random failures only; a software bug in all four channels is a common cause failure and needs diversity."

Link every principle to a document

A design engineer does not apply principles from memory; they apply the standard the project requires. Interviewers like to hear the name of the standard after the principle, because it shows you know where to look. Keep this map in your head: IEC 61513 for the system life cycle, IEC 61226 for categorisation, IEC 60880 and IEC 62138 for software, IEC 60987 for hardware, IEC 62340 for common cause failure, IEC 60709 for separation, IEC 62645 for cybersecurity, IEEE 603 for the US criteria, and IAEA SSG-39 as the international guide for I&C. You do not need to quote clauses. The name and the topic are enough.

Interview angle

A typical follow-up after any principle is "Give me an example from a real system." Have one example per principle ready from the reactor protection system, because it is the system everyone knows: four trains, 2oo4 voting, diverse backup for the trip, separated cabinets, periodic channel tests, data diode to the plant computer.

Process questions are about you

Questions about requirements, traceability, V&V and change management are really asking "will you be disciplined?". Answer in the first person: "I would write the requirement as ...", "I would check the traceability matrix for ...". Mention the document type you would produce. This shows you understand that the job is producing reviewable documents, not only knowing theory.

Tip

For process questions, end with how the step is checked: a review, a test, a signature. In a nuclear project nothing exists until it is verified and recorded, and saying so in one sentence makes you sound like someone who has worked in a quality system.

Two sentences never to swap

Common mistake

Mixing up verification and validation, or saying that the V-model is "waterfall with testing". Verification checks each step against the previous step; validation checks the whole system against the plant's needs. Keep these two sentences ready and never swap them.

Key terms

Defence in depth
Several independent levels of protection between a fault and a release of radioactivity, so that no single failure or error leads to harm.
Category A function
An I&C function in IEC 61226 that is needed to reach and maintain a controlled plant state after an initiating event, such as the reactor trip.
Single failure criterion
The requirement that a system still performs its safety function after any single random failure, including its consequences.
Common cause failure (CCF)
The failure of several redundant channels from the same cause at the same time, for example a software fault or a wrong calibration procedure.
Determinism
The property that the system's response to an input is predictable and bounded in time, achieved by fixed cyclic processing without interrupts.
Maintenance bypass
Taking one redundant channel out of service for test or repair, with the voting degraded in a defined way and the state indicated.
Probability of failure on demand (PFD)
The probability that a safety function does not work when needed; it grows with the undetected failure rate and the test interval.
Traceability matrix
A table linking each requirement to the design elements and tests that implement and verify it, in both directions.
Data diode
A one-way communication gateway that is physically unable to receive data, used to protect a high-security zone.

Say it in English

  • "Redundancy protects against random failures; diversity protects against common cause failures."
  • "The protection system must meet the single failure criterion even with one channel in maintenance bypass."
  • "Verification is building the product right; validation is building the right product."
  • "I would trace every requirement forward to a test case and backward to a customer need."
  • "Data may flow from the protection system to the plant computer, but never in the other direction."

Check yourself

Which measure protects against a common cause failure of four identical processing channels?

Identical channels share the same weaknesses; only diversity gives protection when the cause is common to all of them.

In IEC 61226, a function needed to reach a controlled state after an initiating event is in which category?

Category A is the highest category and includes functions such as the reactor trip; B and C have lesser or indirect roles.

What does validation check?

Validation is performed on the whole system against the original requirements; verification is the step-by-step check against the previous specification.

If the test interval of a single channel is halved, what happens to its average PFD?

For a single channel the average PFD is about the undetected dangerous failure rate times the test interval divided by two, so it scales with the interval.

Which statement about a data diode is correct?

A data diode is a one-way hardware gateway; the protection system can send status out, but nothing can be sent in.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

What is defence in depth?

Defence in depth means placing several independent levels of protection between a fault and a release of radioactivity, so that no single failure or human error leads to harm. The IAEA describes five levels: prevention of abnormal operation by good design, control of abnormal operation by control and limitation systems, control of design basis accidents by protection systems, management of severe accidents, and mitigation of consequences by emergency planning. Safety I&C mostly works at level three, with limitation functions at level two. The limit is that the levels must really be independent, or they fail together.

What are the fundamental safety functions?

There are three: control of reactivity, removal of heat from the fuel, and confinement of radioactive material. Every safety I&C function can be mapped to one of them. The reactor trip controls reactivity. Emergency feedwater and safety injection remove heat. Containment isolation confines activity. When I look at a new function I ask which fundamental function it supports and what happens to that function if the I&C fails. That question also decides its safety category and therefore how much redundancy, qualification and testing it needs.

What are the IEC 61226 categories and how do they relate to safety classes?

IEC 61226 categorises I&C functions by importance to safety. Category A functions are needed to reach and maintain a controlled state after a postulated initiating event, for example the reactor trip. Category B functions complement A or prevent challenges to it, such as some limitation and post-accident monitoring functions. Category C functions have an indirect role, such as alarms and some monitoring. The systems implementing them are assigned classes 1, 2 and 3 in IEC 61513, and IAEA guidance uses safety classes 1 to 3 in a similar way. The category drives redundancy, qualification, software standard and testing.

What is the single failure criterion?

A system meets the single failure criterion if it still performs its safety function when any single component fails, including the consequences of that failure, and while one redundant part may be out of service for maintenance. In practice this leads to at least two independent trains, usually three or four, with voting that tolerates one failure in each direction. The criterion covers random failures, not common cause failures, which need diversity. In a design document I would show compliance with a failure analysis listing each single failure and the remaining capability.

What is the difference between redundancy and diversity?

Redundancy means more than one identical channel or train, so a random failure of one does not lose the function; four pressure channels with 2oo4 voting is redundancy. Diversity means achieving the same goal by different means, so a shared weakness cannot fail everything at once: a different measured parameter, a different sensor technology, a different platform or a hardwired backup. Redundancy protects against random failures; diversity protects against common cause failures. Both cost money, so the design justifies how much of each is needed by the category of the function.

What do independence and separation mean?

Independence means a failure or disturbance in one part cannot propagate to another. We achieve it with physical separation, so a fire or flood in one room does not hit two trains; electrical isolation, so a short circuit or surge cannot cross; functional independence, so the logic of one train does not depend on another; and communication independence, with one-directional or decoupled data links. Independence applies between redundant trains and between safety and non-safety systems. IEC 60709 is the standard for separation of I&C systems.

What does fail-safe mean in a protection system?

A fail-safe design is one where the most likely failures drive the system or the actuator to the safe state. The classic example is de-energise-to-trip: the trip breakers are held closed by energised coils, so loss of power, a broken wire or a stopped processor opens them and the rods drop. Failed-signal handling follows the same idea: an invalid 4 to 20 mA input is treated as a partial trip. The limit is that fail-safe increases spurious trips, so it is combined with redundancy and voting to keep availability acceptable.

What is determinism and how does a safety platform achieve it?

Deterministic behaviour means the response to a given input is predictable and bounded in time, regardless of load or history. A safety platform achieves it with a fixed cyclic programme: in every cycle it reads inputs, runs the complete logic and writes outputs in the same order, with no process-driven interrupts, no dynamic memory allocation and no operating system scheduling decisions. Communication is decoupled through buffers so the network cannot delay the cycle. This lets us prove the worst-case response time, which is a key requirement for a protection system.

What is a common cause failure and what does IEC 62340 require?

A common cause failure is the failure of several redundant channels from the same cause at the same time: a software fault, a design error, a wrong calibration procedure or an environmental event. Redundancy alone does not help, because identical channels fail together. IEC 62340 gives requirements for coping with CCF in systems performing category A functions: an analysis of potential CCF, functional and equipment diversity where needed, a diverse backup for the most important functions, and defensive measures in the platform such as self-monitoring and strict determinism. The result is documented in a CCF assessment.

Describe the reactor protection system chain from sensor to rods.

Sensors measure process parameters such as pressure, neutron flux, flow and temperature. Transmitters convert them to 4 to 20 mA. Redundant acquisition and processing units check validity, compare with setpoints and produce partial trip signals. Voting logic, typically 2oo4, combines the redundant channels. The result de-energises the reactor trip breakers, which cut power to the control rod drive mechanisms, so the rods fall by gravity. The same partial trips can also start engineered safety features. Every step is redundant, independent and periodically tested.

What is ESFAS?

ESFAS is the engineered safety features actuation system. While the reactor trip stops the chain reaction, ESFAS starts the equipment that removes heat and confines radioactivity: safety injection pumps, emergency feedwater, containment isolation valves, containment spray and emergency diesel generators. It uses the same kind of redundant sensing and voting as the trip, often in the same platform, but its outputs are many actuators with defined sequences and priorities rather than a few breakers. Some actuations depend on plant state, so they have permissive and interlock conditions.

What kinds of bypass exist in a protection system?

Two kinds. A maintenance bypass takes one channel out of service for test or repair; only one channel per function may be bypassed at a time, the voting degrades in a defined way, for example 2oo4 to 2oo3, and the bypass is indicated in the control room. An operating bypass automatically removes a trip when it is not needed in a given plant state, for example a low pressure trip during start-up, and is automatically reinstated when the permissive condition disappears. Both are designed and documented so that protection is never silently lost.

Why is periodic testing needed and how is it done?

Periodic testing detects latent failures that self-monitoring cannot see, for example a stuck relay or a drifted transmitter. The protection system is divided so that one channel at a time can be tested while the plant runs: the channel is bypassed, test signals are injected, the response is checked and the channel is returned to service. Test intervals come from the probabilistic analysis and the plant's technical specifications. As a design engineer I would specify what is tested, how, with what overlap between tests, and what the system does while a channel is under test.

What are the roles of IEC 61513, IEC 60880, IEC 61508 and IEEE 603?

IEC 61513 is the top-level nuclear standard for I&C systems important to safety: it defines the system life cycle and architecture requirements. IEC 60880 gives software requirements for category A functions, and IEC 62138 does the same for categories B and C. IEC 61508 is the generic functional safety standard for industry; nuclear projects use it as a reference, for example for pre-developed components, but not as the primary standard. IEEE 603 is the American standard with criteria for safety systems, used in plants following US rules. Which set applies depends on the customer and the regulator.

What are the key properties of TELEPERM XS?

TELEPERM XS is a digital safety I&C platform, originally developed by Siemens and now owned by Framatome, qualified for category A functions and used in many plants worldwide. Its key properties are strictly cyclic, deterministic processing without interrupts; self-monitoring of hardware and software; qualified modules for input, processing, output and communication; decoupled communication so that network faults cannot disturb processing; and the SPACE engineering tool, where functions are drawn as diagrams and the code is generated automatically. The platform and its process have been assessed by several regulators.

What is SPACE and what is a function diagram?

SPACE is the engineering system for TELEPERM XS. The engineer does not write source code; the application is specified as function diagrams built from a library of qualified function blocks: comparators, timers, voters, latches and arithmetic. SPACE generates the application code and the hardware configuration data, and it supports verification, for example by simulation and by comparing the generated code with the specification. For a design engineer this means the function diagram is at the same time the specification, the implementation and the test reference, which simplifies traceability.

Explain the V-model.

The V-model is a picture of the life cycle. The left side goes down from plant requirements to system requirements, system design, software and hardware design, and implementation. The right side goes up through unit tests, integration tests, system validation and site acceptance. Each level on the right verifies the corresponding level on the left: unit tests check the module design, system validation checks the system requirements. Writing tests while writing requirements forces the requirements to be testable. In nuclear I&C the V-model is the backbone of IEC 61513 and of the V&V plan.

What makes a good requirement?

A good requirement is a single, unambiguous, verifiable statement of what the system shall do, with a measurable value and the conditions under which it applies. It uses "shall", one requirement per sentence, defined terms and units, and avoids words like quickly, adequate or user-friendly. It has an identifier so it can be traced, and a note on how it will be verified. For example: the protection system shall initiate the reactor trip signal within 300 ms after the primary pressure in two of four channels falls below 13.0 MPa.

What is traceability and why does it matter?

Traceability means every requirement can be followed forward to the design elements and tests that implement and verify it, and every design element and test can be followed backward to the requirement that justifies it. It is kept in a traceability matrix. Forward tracing proves nothing was forgotten; backward tracing proves nothing was added without a reason, which matters in safety systems where unused functions are a risk. In a project I would maintain traceability from the customer specification through system and software requirements down to test cases.

What is the difference between verification and validation?

Verification asks: did we build it according to the specification of the previous step? It is done at every life cycle stage by review, analysis and test, for example checking that the function diagram implements every system requirement. Validation asks: does the finished system do what the plant actually needs? It is done on the integrated system against the original requirements, typically with realistic scenarios on a test bay. A short way to say it: verification is building the product right, validation is building the right product.

What is the difference between FAT and SAT?

The factory acceptance test is performed at the supplier's premises on the complete integrated system, often with a plant simulator, before shipping. It demonstrates to the customer that the system meets its specification. The site acceptance test is performed after installation at the plant, with the real cabling, sensors and actuators. It confirms that nothing was damaged in transport, that the interfaces to the plant work, and that the installed system behaves as it did in the factory. SAT is usually shorter and relies on the FAT results.

What are configuration management and change management?

Configuration management means knowing at every moment exactly which versions of documents, software, hardware and parameters make up the system, and keeping them consistent. Every item has an identifier and a version, and baselines are frozen at milestones. Change management is the process for modifying a baseline: a change request, an impact analysis covering safety, documents, tests and interfaces, approval, implementation, re-verification and update of every affected document. In safety I&C an unrecorded change is a non-conformance even if it is technically correct.

How is cybersecurity handled, and what is a data diode?

Nuclear I&C cybersecurity follows IEC 62645, which assigns systems to security degrees by consequence, with the protection system at the highest degree. The architecture is divided into zones, and data may flow from a higher security zone to a lower one but not back. The physical implementation of that rule is a data diode, a one-way gateway that can transmit but is physically unable to receive. The protection system can send status to the plant computer, but nothing from the office network can reach it. Access control, hardening and removable media rules complete the picture.

What is an FMEA?

A failure modes and effects analysis takes each component of a system, lists the ways it can fail, and for every failure mode asks what the local effect is, what the effect on the system function is, how it is detected, and what measures reduce it. For an I&C channel the components are the transmitter, the cable, the power supply, the input module and the processor. The result shows whether every single failure is detected or tolerated and feeds the single-failure demonstration and the reliability analysis. Its value lies in the completeness of the failure modes.

What is PFD and how does the test interval affect it?

PFD is the probability of failure on demand: the chance that the safety function does not work when it is needed. It depends on the rate of dangerous failures that self-monitoring does not detect and on how long such a failure can stay hidden. For a single channel the average PFD is about the undetected dangerous failure rate times the test interval divided by two, so halving the test interval roughly halves the PFD. Redundancy and voting reduce it further, but common cause failures put a floor under the improvement. That is why test intervals are a design input.

Day 4 · Hour 4

Whiteboard and design exercises with worked solutions

Five small design tasks of the kind used in written tests and whiteboard sessions: trip logic, a measurement chain, requirement rewriting, a test case and an FMEA. Interviewers use them to watch how you work, so the method matters as much as the result.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Design and explain 2oo4 trip logic with a maintenance bypass and a hardwired manual trip
  • Specify a redundant measurement chain and its I/O list for a control loop
  • Turn a vague requirement into a verifiable one with a value, a condition and an identifier
  • Write a complete test case with preconditions, steps, expected results and pass criteria
  • Perform a small FMEA and propose detection measures for each failure mode

How to work any exercise

Read the task twice; every noun is an input, an output or a constraint. Ask one clarifying question if a value is missing, state your assumptions, then work from inputs to outputs and finish with the failure cases: invalid signal, lost power, conflicting commands.

Interview angle

Say your assumptions before you draw: "I assume four independent divisions, de-energise-to-trip breakers and a setpoint of 13.0 MPa." This protects you if the interviewer had something else in mind and shows which decisions a designer must make explicit.

Exercise 1: low primary pressure trip, 2oo4, bypass, manual trip

Task. Design the logic for a reactor trip on low primary pressure with four measurement channels, 2oo4 voting, a maintenance bypass of one channel for testing, and a manual trip. Approach. Per channel: validity, comparison, bypass. Then vote in every division, latch, output, and show the manual trip as a hardwired path downstream of the software. Decide what a bypassed channel does to the vote.

INPUTS (channel i = 1..4, one per division)
  I_i      transmitter current, 4-20 mA, range 10..18 MPa  -> P_i in MPa
  BYP_i    maintenance bypass key switch, channel i
  MAN_A, MAN_B   manual trip pushbuttons, two, hardwired
PARAMETERS
  SP_LOW = 13.0 MPa   trip setpoint        HYS = 0.1 MPa (reset at 13.1)

CHANNEL LOGIC (in division i)
  VALID_i = (I_i >= 3.6 mA) AND (I_i <= 21.0 mA)
  LOW_i   = SET when P_i < SP_LOW, RESET when P_i > SP_LOW + HYS
  PART_i  = LOW_i OR NOT VALID_i          -- invalid signal = partial trip
  ONE_BYP = count(BYP_1..BYP_4) <= 1      -- second bypass refused + alarm
  TRIP_i  = PART_i AND NOT (BYP_i AND ONE_BYP)

VOTING (in every division, on TRIP_1..4 via isolated links)
  N        = count(TRIP_1..TRIP_4)
  AUTO     = (N >= 2)                     -- 2oo4; with one bypass -> 2oo3
  RT_LATCH = SET(AUTO), RESET(OPER_RESET AND NOT AUTO)   -- set dominant
  RT_OUT   = NOT RT_LATCH                  -- energised = healthy

FINAL ACTUATION (hardwired, per trip breaker)
  COIL = RT_OUT AND NOT MAN_A AND NOT MAN_B
  -- loss of power or either button opens the breaker

Decisions to state. A bypassed channel is forced to "no trip", so 2oo4 becomes 2oo3, which still tolerates one failure in each direction. The alternative, forcing it to "trip" so the vote becomes 1oo3, is more conservative but raises the spurious trip risk; either is acceptable if documented. The bypass is indicated in the control room. The manual trip does not pass through software, so it works even during a complete platform failure.

Exercise 2: steam generator level control chain

Task. Specify the measurement chain and I/O for steam generator (SG) level control with redundancy. Approach. List what the controller needs (level, feedwater flow, steam flow), how many of each, how redundancy is used, and how the valve is driven. Then separate control from protection.

Signal (per SG)TypeQtySignalUse
Narrow range levelDP transmitter, AI34 to 20 mA, 0 to 100 % of narrow spanMedian select for control; deviation alarm
Feedwater flowDP across flow element, AI24 to 20 mA, square-root extractedAverage of two; third element
Steam flowDP, AI24 to 20 mAAverage of two; feedforward
Control valve positionAI1 per valve4 to 20 mAFeedback and deviation alarm
Valve commandAO1 per valve4 to 20 mA to positionerMain valve at power, bypass valve at low power
Valve end positionsDI2 per valve24 V DC dry contactOpen and closed limit switches
Transmitter supplyPower2 feeds24 V DC, diode coupledTransmitters split across feeds

Decisions to state. Level control is an operational function in the DCS. The SG low level trip and high level feedwater isolation are protection functions with their own transmitters, physically and electrically separated from the control ones. Narrow range DP measurement needs density compensation. At low power the controller uses level only, because flow measurements are inaccurate there.

Exercise 3: rewrite three bad requirements

Task. Rewrite the three requirements below. Approach. Find the missing value, condition and vague word; write "shall", a number with unit, the condition, an identifier and a verification method.

BAD 1  "The system shall respond quickly to a low pressure."
GOOD   RPS-F-012  The RPS shall initiate the reactor trip command within
       300 ms after the primary pressure in at least two of four channels
       falls below 13.0 MPa, measured from transmitter terminals to trip
       breaker coil.                      Verification: test (TC-RPS-012)

BAD 2  "The operator should be able to easily bypass the channel if needed."
GOOD   RPS-F-031  Each protection channel shall provide a key-operated
       maintenance bypass. Only one channel per protection function shall
       be in bypass at a time; a second bypass request shall be rejected
       and alarmed. The bypass state shall be indicated in the main
       control room.                      Verification: test, inspection

BAD 3  "The cabinet must work in all environmental conditions and be reliable."
GOOD   RPS-E-005  The cabinet shall perform all specified functions at an
       ambient temperature of 5 to 40 °C and a relative humidity of 10 to
       90 % non-condensing.               Verification: type test
       RPS-R-002  The MTBF of the cabinet, excluding I/O modules, shall be
       at least 100 000 h.
                                          Verification: analysis

Tip

Quickly, easily, all conditions and reliable are the words that always fail a requirement review. Replace each with a number and a condition. If you do not know the number, write a bracketed placeholder and raise an open point.

Exercise 4: test case for a high neutron flux trip

Task. Write a complete test case for the high neutron flux reactor trip. Approach. Start from the requirement, define preconditions, write numbered steps with expected results, and add failure cases: single channel, latch, reset, invalid signal.

FieldContent
Test case IDTC-RPS-021, revision A
RequirementRPS-F-021: trip when power range flux exceeds 109 % in at least two of four channels, within 200 ms
ObjectiveVerify setpoint, 2oo4 voting, response time, latch, reset and fail-safe behaviour
PreconditionsTest bay with released software version; four channels healthy, no bypass; simulated flux 100 % on all channels; trip reset
Step 1Raise channel 1 to 112 %. Expected: partial trip channel 1 only, alarm, no reactor trip
Step 2Raise channel 2 to 112 %. Expected: reactor trip; coil de-energised within 200 ms of crossing 109 %
Step 3Return both channels to 100 %. Expected: trip remains latched
Step 4Operator reset. Expected: trip clears
Step 5Repeat steps 1 to 4 with channels 3 and 4
Step 6Set channel 1 to 108.5 %. Expected: no partial trip (setpoint accuracy)
Step 7Channel 1 to 2 mA (invalid), channel 2 to 112 %. Expected: reactor trip (invalid counts as partial trip)
Pass criteriaAll expected results observed; response time below 200 ms in every trial
RecordMeasured values, times, software version, tester, date

Exercise 5: small FMEA of a pressure transmitter loop

Task. Perform an FMEA for one pressure transmitter loop (transmitter, cable, 24 V supply, input module) feeding a 2oo4 trip, and propose detection measures. Approach. Component by component: failure mode, local effect, effect on the trip, detection, measure.

ItemFailure modeEffect on channelEffect on 2oo4 tripDetectionMeasure
TransmitterOutput out of range (above 21 or below 3.6 mA)Invalid, partial tripNone; a second failure could trip spuriouslyRange check in input moduleAlarm, replace
TransmitterDrift inside rangeWrong value, not self-detectedChannel trips late or earlyChannel-to-channel deviation alarm, periodic calibrationCalibration interval
Impulse lineBlocked or frozenValue does not follow processChannel fails to tripDeviation during transients, periodic testHeat tracing, blowdown
CableOpen or short circuit0 mA or over-range, invalid, partial tripNoneRange check, fused loop supplySeparate routing, mechanical protection
24 V supplyLoss of one feedNone if second feed healthyNoneSupply voltage monitoringRedundant diode-coupled feeds
Input moduleConverter faultWrong digital valueOne channel affectedModule self-testSelf-monitoring, replace
ParametersWrong scaling in all four channelsSystematic errorCommon cause: trip at wrong pressureIndependent parameter review, validation testVerification in life cycle

Common mistake

Listing only failures that the range check detects. The dangerous failures are inside the valid range: drift, a frozen impulse line, a wrong parameter. Always include an undetected failure mode and how the design catches it: channel comparison and periodic testing.

Key terms

Median select
Taking the middle value of three redundant measurements, which rejects one failed transmitter without a step in the output.
Three-element control
Steam generator level control using level, feedwater flow and steam flow together, so the controller reacts to flow mismatch before the level changes.
Impulse line
The small pipe connecting the process to a pressure or differential pressure transmitter; it can block, freeze or leak.
Narrow range and wide range
Two level spans: a narrow one around the operating point for accurate control, a wide one covering the whole vessel for start-up and accidents.
Set-dominant latch
A latch where set wins if set and reset are both true, so a trip cannot be cancelled while the condition persists.
Test case
A documented procedure with identifier, requirement reference, preconditions, steps, expected results and pass criteria.
Pass criterion
The measurable condition that decides whether a test step passed, for example a response time below 200 ms.
Failure mode
One specific way a component can fail, such as output high, output low, drift or stuck.

Say it in English

  • "Let me state my assumptions first: four divisions, de-energise-to-trip breakers and a setpoint of thirteen megapascals."
  • "A bypassed channel is forced to no-trip, so the voting degrades from two out of four to two out of three."
  • "The manual trip is hardwired downstream of the software, so it works even if the whole platform fails."
  • "I replaced 'quickly' with a value: within three hundred milliseconds, measured from the transmitter terminals to the breaker coil."
  • "The dangerous failures are the ones inside the valid range, so I added a channel deviation alarm and periodic calibration."

Check yourself

In Exercise 1, why is the manual trip connected downstream of the digital logic?

A hardwired manual trip is a diverse path that remains available during a complete failure of the processing system.

Why does steam generator level control use the median of three transmitters?

The median ignores the highest and lowest values, so a transmitter failing high or low does not disturb the controller.

Which of these is a verifiable requirement?

Only the last option has a value with a unit, a clear condition and a defined measurement point, so a test can pass or fail it.

In the test case, why is step 7 (one channel at 2 mA, one at 112 %) included?

An invalid input must be treated conservatively; the test proves that one failed channel plus one real demand still produces the trip.

Which failure mode in the FMEA is NOT detected by the input range check?

Drift keeps the signal between 4 and 20 mA, so only comparison with redundant channels or periodic calibration can reveal it.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

Draw the logic for a 2oo4 trip with one channel in test. What changes?

I draw four channels on the left, each with a range check and a limit comparator, producing a partial trip. Each partial trip goes to a voter in every division. Normally the voter trips when at least two of the four partial trips are present. When channel three is in test, its bypass switch forces its partial trip to false, and the voter is now effectively two out of three on the remaining channels. That still tolerates one spurious channel and one stuck channel. I would also show that a second bypass is rejected and that the bypass state is alarmed in the control room.

Why not simply put the bypassed channel into the trip state?

That is a valid alternative. If the bypassed channel is forced to trip, the vote becomes one out of three: any single remaining channel trips the reactor. It is more conservative for safety, because no failure can block the trip, but it makes a spurious trip much more likely during the test, which has its own cost and its own risk from the transient. Forcing the channel to no-trip keeps two out of three, which still meets the single failure criterion. The choice depends on the plant's technical specifications and must be documented and justified either way.

How would you specify the transmitters for a steam generator level control loop?

I would specify three narrow range differential pressure transmitters per steam generator, 4 to 20 mA, with a median select in the controller so that one failed transmitter is rejected automatically and a deviation alarm tells the operator. I would add two feedwater flow and two steam flow transmitters for three-element control, position feedback on the control valve, and redundant 24 V supplies with the transmitters split across them. I would state the accuracy, the response time and the temperature compensation. And I would make clear that the protection trips use separate transmitters, independent from the control ones.

What is wrong with the requirement "The system shall respond quickly to low pressure"?

Three things. "Quickly" has no value, so no test can pass or fail it. "Low pressure" has no setpoint and no statement about how many channels must see it. And there is no definition of where the response is measured from and to. I would rewrite it as: the protection system shall initiate the reactor trip command within 300 ms after the primary pressure in at least two of four channels falls below 13.0 MPa, measured from the transmitter terminals to the breaker coil. Then I would give it an identifier and link it to a test case.

In your FMEA, which failure worries you most and why?

The failures inside the valid signal range, because the range check cannot see them. A transmitter that drifts by two percent still sends a plausible current, and a frozen impulse line sends a value that simply stops following the process. In a single channel these would delay or prevent the trip. The design catches them by comparing the four redundant channels and raising a deviation alarm, and by periodic calibration and testing with a pressure source. The other failure I always include is a wrong parameter common to all channels, because that is a common cause failure and only independent verification catches it.

Day 4 · Hour 5

Behavioural and motivation questions in English

Technical knowledge gets you to the final round; behavioural answers decide whether the team wants to work with you for the next five years. This hour gives you a structure, eight prepared stories and honest answers to the motivation questions every nuclear employer asks.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Structure any behavioural answer with STAR in under two minutes
  • Tell prepared stories about teamwork, detail, mistakes, learning, disagreement, long projects, international teams and unclear instructions
  • Explain why nuclear and why Framatome Controls with concrete, informed reasons
  • Answer the weakness and no-experience questions sincerely without damaging yourself
  • Apply three speaking habits that make a non-native speaker easier to follow

Why behavioural questions exist

A behavioural question asks for a real past event: "Tell me about a time when ...". The idea is that past behaviour predicts future behaviour better than opinions do. Nobody can check whether you "work well in teams"; everybody can judge a specific story with a specific result. For a junior, the stories come from university projects, the thesis, internships, part-time jobs or volunteering. Small is fine. Specific is essential.

The STAR structure

Situation: one or two sentences of context. Task: what you personally had to achieve. Action: what you did, step by step, in the first person. Result: what happened, with a number or a visible outcome, and one sentence of what you learned. Spend most of the time on Action and Result; juniors spend too long on Situation. Target length is 90 to 120 seconds.

SITUATION  "During [project / internship] at [place], we had to [goal]."
TASK       "My job was to [your specific responsibility]."
ACTION     "First I [...]. Then I [...]. When [obstacle], I [...]."
RESULT     "As a result [measurable outcome]. What I took from it is [lesson]."
QuestionSituation and taskActionResult
TeamworkFour-person student project building a monitoring system; I owned the sensor interface.Agreed interfaces in writing on day one, held a 15-minute sync twice a week, tested my part against the others' simulator before integration.Integration took one afternoon instead of the planned week; the interface document became the user manual.
Attention to detailInternship review of wiring diagrams; my task was only to check cable numbers.Noticed a thermocouple pair with reversed polarity on two drawings, checked the datasheet, reported it with evidence.Corrected before manufacturing; polarity was added to the review checklist.
Learning fastThesis required a PLC platform I had never used, with three months to deliver.One week on the vendor's examples, then the smallest working loop, then one function at a time with a test for each.Delivered on time; this incremental method is now how I learn any new tool.
DisagreementA teammate wanted a long moving average on a noisy signal; I expected it to hide fast changes.Plotted both filters on recorded data, showed the delay in numbers, proposed a shorter filter with hysteresis.We decided on data, not opinion; the shorter filter went into the design.
Long projectFour months of thesis documentation and testing with no new features.Split the work into weekly deliverables, kept a visible checklist, treated every completed test as a result.Finished on schedule; small visible steps keep motivation up.
International teamSemester project with students from three countries, working in English.Wrote decisions down after each meeting, asked for repetition when unsure, proposed a shared glossary.Fewer misunderstandings; a habit I would bring to a multinational project.
Unclear instructionsInternship task "prepare the I/O list" with no template or scope.Asked two specific questions, drafted a structure within an hour and had it approved before filling it.No rework; the supervisor said the draft-first approach saved a day.
A mistakeSee the full model answer in the interview questions below.Own it, fix it, change the process.A lesson you still apply.

Tip

Prepare six stories, not twenty. Each story can answer several questions: the reversed-polarity story works for attention to detail, for taking initiative and for communicating with seniors. Before the interview, write the six titles on one card.

"Why nuclear?" and "Why Framatome Controls?"

These two questions test whether you applied to this job or to any job. Your answer should contain facts that only someone who prepared would know. For why nuclear: the safety mission, where your care about details has real consequences; a sector that thinks in decades, which suits someone who wants deep expertise rather than fast job changes; and the fact that nuclear produces more than half of Slovakia's electricity, so the work matters locally. For why Framatome Controls: it is a specialist in safety I&C, not a general IT company; it works on the TELEPERM XS platform, which is used in many plants worldwide, so you learn a tool with a long future; the Bratislava engineering centre works on international projects while being part of a large French group; the projects are long-term and stable; and Slovakia operates VVER-440 units at Bohunice and Mochovce, so the local nuclear community is real. Then add one personal sentence, for example which course or project made you interested.

Interview angle

Interviewers can tell the difference between "your company is a leader in the field" and "you work on TELEPERM XS, which is used for category A functions, and I want to learn a platform that will still exist in twenty years". The first sentence is flattery. The second is evidence of preparation.

Five years, weaknesses, no experience

"Where do you see yourself in five years?" Say something realistic and inside the company: a design engineer who can own a system specification from requirements to validation, who knows the platform and the standards deeply, and who is starting to mentor a junior or lead a small work package. Do not say "in management" and do not say "I do not know".

"What are your weaknesses?" Give a real one, show you know it, and show what you do about it. Good examples: "I go into details before I have the overall picture, so I now start every task with a one-page overview"; "my spoken English slows down under stress, so I prepare and practise aloud"; "I find it hard to stop improving a document, so I agree a deadline with the reviewer in advance". Avoid fake weaknesses such as "I am a perfectionist" and avoid weaknesses that are core to the job, such as "I dislike documentation".

"Why should we hire you with no experience?" Do not apologise. The company advertised a junior position, so it expects to train. Offer what you do have: solid fundamentals, a documented project where you specified, designed and tested something, proof that you learn tools quickly, a long-term intention, and the honesty to say when you do not know. Finish with the fact that a junior who stays five years is worth more than a senior who leaves in one.

Common mistake

Criticising a previous employer, professor or teammate in a story. Even when the other person was wrong, describe what you did, not what they failed to do. Interviewers assume you will talk about them the same way one day.

Tips for non-native speakers

  • Short sentences. One idea per sentence. "I checked the datasheet. The polarity was reversed. I reported it." is clearer than a long sentence with three clauses.
  • Pause. A pause of two seconds before the Action part sounds like thinking, not like a problem with English.
  • Signpost. Say the structure: "The situation was ... My task was ... What I did was ... The result was ...". The interviewer can follow even if your grammar slips.
  • Do not apologise for your English. Say "Let me rephrase that" instead of "Sorry for my English".
  • Ask to repeat. "Could you repeat the second part of the question?" is normal and costs nothing.

Key terms

Behavioural question
A question that asks for a real past event, usually starting "Tell me about a time when", to predict future behaviour from past behaviour.
STAR
Situation, Task, Action, Result: a structure for telling a behavioural story in 90 to 120 seconds with most of the time on Action and Result.
Competency
A behaviour the employer wants to see evidence of, such as teamwork, rigour, learning ability or communication.
Quantified result
An outcome with a number or a visible change, for example "integration took one afternoon instead of a week".
Motivation question
A question such as "Why nuclear?" or "Why us?" that tests whether you prepared for this specific employer.
Signposting
Announcing the structure of your answer with phrases like "First", "My task was", "The result was".
Rephrase
To say the same idea again in different words, a useful tool when a sentence goes wrong in English.
Work package
A defined piece of project work with its own scope, documents and schedule, often the first thing a junior is given to lead.

Say it in English

  • "The situation was a four-person student project; my task was the sensor interface."
  • "What I did was plot both filters on recorded data, so we could decide on numbers rather than opinions."
  • "As a result, integration took one afternoon instead of the week we had planned."
  • "I want to work on a platform that will still be in service in twenty years, and TELEPERM XS is such a platform."
  • "My weakness is that I go into details too early, so I now start every task with a one-page overview."
  • "Let me rephrase that."

Check yourself

In a STAR answer, which two parts should take most of the time?

The interviewer wants to know what you personally did and what came of it; context should be short.

Which answer to "What are your weaknesses?" is recommended?

A real weakness plus a concrete countermeasure shows self-awareness; fake weaknesses and job-critical weaknesses both hurt you.

Which statement about Framatome Controls shows real preparation?

Only the last option contains facts specific to the company's work; the others could be said to any employer.

What should you avoid in a story about a disagreement?

Interviewers judge how you talk about colleagues; focus on your own actions and on how the decision was reached.

A good answer to "Where do you see yourself in five years?" for this role is:

A realistic path inside the company matches the employer's need for people who stay through multi-year projects.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

Why do you want to work in nuclear?

Three reasons. First, the safety mission: in nuclear, attention to detail has real consequences, and I want my care about a wiring polarity or a requirement wording to matter. Second, the time horizon: nuclear thinks in decades, which suits someone who wants deep expertise in one field rather than a new technology every two years. Third, it is relevant here: nuclear produces more than half of Slovakia's electricity, and the Mochovce and Bohunice units will need I&C engineering for a long time. Personally, the moment I got interested was a course on protection systems, where I understood that logic I could draw on paper decides whether a reactor trips.

Why Framatome Controls specifically?

Because you are a specialist in safety I&C, not a general automation or IT company. You design on the TELEPERM XS platform, which is qualified for category A functions and used in many plants worldwide, so the skills I would build have a long future. The Bratislava engineering centre works on international projects while being part of a large French group, which means both exposure and stability. Your projects are long-term, which matches how I want to work. And you are close to the Slovak VVER units, so the local nuclear community is real. I also read your postings carefully: basic design, specification, periodic testing, and that is the kind of document-driven engineering I enjoy.

Tell me about a mistake you made and what you learned.

During my thesis I changed a scaling parameter in the PLC after the test phase, because a sensor was replaced. I did not repeat the related test, and the final demonstration showed a wrong temperature reading. I corrected the parameter, repeated the full test for that loop, and then I introduced a simple rule for myself: any change to a parameter or logic triggers the linked test again, and both are recorded in a change log. The demonstration was repeated successfully a week later. What I learned is exactly what configuration management is for, and why a nuclear project treats an unrecorded change as a non-conformance.

What are your weaknesses?

I will give you a real one. I tend to go into technical details before I have the overall picture. In my thesis I spent the first two weeks optimising a filter before I had defined all the inputs and outputs, and I had to redo part of it. Now I start every task with a one-page overview: inputs, outputs, interfaces, open questions, and only then go into detail. A second one is that my spoken English slows down under stress, which you may notice today. I prepare and practise aloud, and I ask for a question to be repeated rather than guessing. I think both are manageable, and I prefer to name them rather than hide them.
