Why should we hire you if you have no experience in nuclear?

Because you advertised a junior position, so you are hiring potential, and I can show evidence of it. I have solid fundamentals in control, electronics and measurement. I have a documented project where I specified sensors, designed logic, wrote a test procedure and kept a change log, which is a small version of the work in your postings. I learn tools quickly and in a structured way, which I can describe with examples. I intend to stay and build expertise over years, and I would rather say "I do not know" than guess, which I understand is important in your culture. A junior who stays five years is worth more than a senior who leaves after one.

Day 4 · Hour 6

English for engineers: stress, numbers, verbs and phrases

The interview is in English with an international team, and small language habits decide whether a correct answer is understood. This hour fixes word stress, numbers and units, the verbs of technical documents, set phrases for the interview, and the typical mistakes of Slavic-language speakers.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Pronounce about 25 key I&C terms with the correct word stress
  • Say numbers, units, ranges and tolerances aloud without hesitation
  • Use the precise verbs of technical documents: specify, verify, validate, actuate, initiate, de-energise, isolate, monitor, trace
  • Use set phrases to explain, clarify, buy time, admit limits, disagree politely and describe a diagram
  • Recognise and correct the most common errors of Slavic-language speakers in English

Word stress

English listeners recognise a word mainly by its stressed syllable. If you stress the wrong one, a native speaker may not recognise a word you know perfectly. Capitals mark the stressed syllable. Practise each word three times aloud.

WordStressWordStress
instrumentationin-stru-men-TA-tionreliabilityre-li-a-BIL-i-ty
redundancyre-DUN-dan-cyavailabilitya-vail-a-BIL-i-ty
diversitydi-VER-si-tyarchitectureAR-chi-tec-ture
deterministicde-ter-mi-NIS-ticperiodicpe-ri-OD-ic
actuatorAC-tu-a-torparameterpa-RAM-e-ter
pressuriserPRES-su-ri-serspecificationspec-i-fi-CA-tion
containmentcon-TAIN-mentverificationver-i-fi-CA-tion
reactorre-AC-torvalidationval-i-DA-tion
reactivityre-ac-TIV-i-tycomponentcom-PO-nent

transmittertrans-MIT-termaintenanceMAIN-te-nance

Numbers, units and tolerances aloud

  • 4 to 20 mA: "four to twenty milliamps". 3.6 mA: "three point six milliamps". The decimal separator is "point", never "comma".
  • 15.5 MPa: "fifteen point five megapascals". 0.1 MPa: "zero point one megapascals".
  • ±0.5 %: "plus or minus zero point five percent" or "plus or minus half a percent".
  • 2oo4: "two out of four". 2oo3: "two out of three".
  • 50 Hz: "fifty hertz" (no plural ending). 24 V DC: "twenty-four volts DC".
  • 300 ms: "three hundred milliseconds". 100 000 h: "a hundred thousand hours".
  • 10 to the power of minus 6: "ten to the minus six". 290 °C: "two hundred and ninety degrees Celsius".
  • 1 600 EUR: "sixteen hundred euros" or "one thousand six hundred euros". In English, thousands take a comma or a space and decimals take a point.

Verbs of technical documents

Technical English uses a small set of precise verbs, and using the right one signals competence. Specify: state what the system shall do or be. Verify: check that a step matches its specification. Validate: confirm the whole system meets the real need. Actuate: make an actuator move. Initiate: start an automatic action, such as a trip. De-energise: remove power from a coil or circuit. Isolate: close a path, electrically or in a pipe. Monitor: observe continuously and report. Trace: link a requirement to its design and tests. Also useful: allocate a function to a system, qualify equipment for its environment, commission a system at site, derive lower-level requirements from higher ones, justify a design decision, release a document.

Common mistake

Saying "the system makes a trip" or "the valve makes closing". Use the precise verb: "the system initiates a trip", "the valve closes", "the output de-energises the coil". Likewise "I controlled the drawing" means you steered it; you mean "I checked the drawing".

Phrases for the interview

  • Explaining: "Let me start with the big picture and then go into detail." "There are three parts to this." "In other words, ..."
  • Clarifying: "Just to make sure I understood, you are asking about ..." "Do you mean the hardware or the software side?"
  • Buying time: "That is a good question, let me think for a second." "Let me structure my answer."
  • Admitting limits: "I have not worked with that directly, but my understanding is ..." "That is where my knowledge ends; I would check the standard."
  • Disagreeing politely: "I see it slightly differently, because ..." "That is one option; the risk I see with it is ..." "Could we look at the data on this?"
  • Describing a diagram: "On the left we have the inputs ..." "This block compares the value with the setpoint." "The output goes to ..." "The signal flows from left to right."
  • Correcting yourself: "Let me rephrase that." "Sorry, I meant the secondary circuit."

Interview angle

Interviewers do not mark grammar, but they notice confidence. A learner who says "Let me rephrase that" and continues sounds in control. A learner who stops, apologises for their English and looks down loses the room. The phrases above are tools for staying in control.

Common mistakes of Slavic-language speakers

WrongRightWhy

Tip

Two sounds to practise: "w" as in "water" and "valve" with a clear "v", so "valve" does not become "walve"; and "th" in "three" and "threshold", so it does not become "tree". Slow down on these words; clarity beats speed.

Key terms

Word stress
The syllable said louder and longer in a word; English listeners use it to recognise the word.
False friend
A word that looks like one in your language but means something else, such as "control" or "actual".
Uncountable noun
A noun with no plural form, such as information, equipment, software and advice.
Present perfect
The "have done" tense, used with "for" or "since" for something that started in the past and continues now.
Article
"A", "an" or "the"; English requires one before most singular nouns, Slavic languages do not have them.
Collocation
Words that naturally go together, such as "run a test", "initiate a trip", "release a document".
Decimal point
The dot in 15.5, said "point"; English never uses a comma for decimals.
Specify
To state in a document what a system shall do or be, with values and conditions.
Actuate
To make an actuator such as a valve, pump or breaker move or operate.
Initiate
To start an automatic action, for example "the RPS initiates a reactor trip".
De-energise
To remove electrical power from a coil or circuit, often causing a fail-safe action.
Isolate
To close off a path, electrically (galvanic isolation) or in process piping (isolation valve).
Trace
To link a requirement to the design elements and tests that implement and verify it.
Signpost
A phrase that announces the structure of what you will say, such as "There are three parts to this".

Say it in English

  • "The transmitter output is four to twenty milliamps, with an accuracy of plus or minus zero point two five percent of span."
  • "The primary pressure is about fifteen point five megapascals at roughly three hundred degrees Celsius."
  • "On the left we have the four pressure channels; this block votes two out of four; the output de-energises the breaker coil."
  • "Just to make sure I understood, you are asking about the maintenance bypass, not the operating bypass?"
  • "I see it slightly differently, because a long filter would delay the trip; could we look at the data?"
  • "I have worked with PLCs for two years, and I checked the current version of the drawing yesterday."

Check yourself

Which word has the stress on the first syllable?

AC-tu-a-tor is stressed on the first syllable; the other three are stressed on the second.

How do you say "15.5 MPa" aloud?

English uses "point" for the decimal separator and the unit takes a plural ending here.

Which sentence is correct?

"Check" not "control", "current" not "actual", and "for two days" not "since two days".

Which verb means to start an automatic action such as a trip?

"The RPS initiates a reactor trip" is the standard phrase; validate, isolate and trace have other meanings.

Which of these nouns is uncountable in English?

Equipment, information, software and advice have no plural form; say "items of equipment".

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

Could you describe this function diagram for me in English?

Of course. On the left we have the inputs: four pressure signals, each four to twenty milliamps, one per division. Each signal first passes a range check, and then a limit comparator with a setpoint of thirteen megapascals and a hysteresis of zero point one. The comparator output is the partial trip. In the middle, this block votes two out of four on the partial trips. The output goes to a set-dominant latch, and from there to the right, where it de-energises the trip breaker coil. The signal flows from left to right, and the manual trip joins the path here, after the software.

I did not follow your last point. Could you say it differently?

Yes, let me rephrase that. I was explaining why a bypassed channel is forced to the no-trip state. In other words: when one channel is in test, we do not want it to vote, so its output is set to zero. The remaining three channels still vote two out of three. That means one spurious signal does not trip the reactor, and one stuck channel does not block the trip. So even during testing we keep tolerance to a single failure. Is that clearer, or should I draw it?

How comfortable are you working in English every day, including writing documents?

Comfortable, with honesty about where I am. I studied from English textbooks and standards, I wrote my thesis summary and several reports in English, and I worked in an international student team where all meetings were in English. Reading technical documents is no problem. Writing is good when I use the structure of a template and precise verbs: specify, verify, initiate. Speaking under pressure is where I still slow down, as you may hear today, so I prepare, use short sentences and ask for a question to be repeated rather than guess. I expect my fluency to improve quickly in a team that works in English daily.

Read this value aloud for me: the accuracy is ±0.25 % of span at 4 to 20 mA.

The accuracy is plus or minus zero point two five percent of span, on a four to twenty milliamp signal. Span means the difference between the upper and lower range values, so if the range is ten to eighteen megapascals, the span is eight megapascals and zero point two five percent of that is zero point zero two megapascals, or twenty kilopascals. In the current signal, zero point two five percent of a sixteen milliamp span is zero point zero four milliamps. I would also ask whether the figure includes temperature effects or only reference conditions.

What is the difference between "verify" and "validate" as you would use them in a document?

I use "verify" when I check that a work product matches the specification of the previous step. For example, "the function diagram was verified against the system requirements specification by review". I use "validate" when the integrated system is tested against the plant's real needs. For example, "the protection system was validated on the test bay using the accident scenarios". In short, verification happens at every stage and asks "did we build it right"; validation happens on the complete system and asks "did we build the right thing". In a document I never use the two words interchangeably.

Day 4 · Hour 7

Questions to ask, salary talk and practical logistics

The last ten minutes of every round belong to you: your questions show how you think, and your answers on salary and availability decide whether an offer is easy to make. This hour prepares both, plus the follow-up email and the final checklists.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Ask three well-chosen questions at the end of each round, matched to who is in the room
  • Discuss salary in Slovakia confidently: gross versus net, a range anchored on the posting, total package
  • Answer questions on notice period, start date, probation, home office and business trips
  • Write a short, professional thank-you e-mail within 24 hours
  • Recognise red and green flags and run the day-before and day-of checklists

Twelve smart questions for the interviewer

"Do you have any questions for us?" is not a formality. A candidate with no questions looks uninterested; a candidate who asks about the lunch break looks unprepared. Choose three from the table depending on who is in the room: engineers for platform and process, the manager for team and career, HR for logistics. Listen to the answer and ask one follow-up.

TopicQuestionWhat the answer tells you
Team and projectsWhich project would I most likely join first, and in which phase is it?Whether you start with specification, V&V or documentation
Team and projectsHow is the team organised: by project, by discipline or by system?Who you report to and how work is assigned
Team and projectsHow much of the work is for Slovak plants and how much for projects abroad?Travel, languages, customer culture
Platform and toolsHow does a design engineer work with the SPACE tool and function diagrams in practice?How close the role is to the platform
Platform and toolsWhich tools do you use for requirements and traceability?Maturity of the process, tools you must learn
Training and mentoringHow does onboarding work for a junior in the first six months?Whether they have a plan or expect you to swim
Training and mentoringIs there platform training, and is there a mentor assigned to a new engineer?Investment in juniors
Process and qualityHow does a document go from draft to release: who reviews, who approves?How much review you will see and give
Process and qualityWhat is the most common type of finding in design reviews here?What to be careful about from day one
Process and qualityHow do you handle a customer change request late in a project?How change management works in reality
CareerWhat does a design engineer typically do after two or three years here?Growth paths: lead engineer, V&V, site, project
CareerHow is performance evaluated and how often do you talk about development?Feedback culture and salary review rhythm

Interview angle

The question "What is the most common finding in design reviews?" does two things: it shows you expect to be reviewed, and the answer gives you a concrete thing to mention in your thank-you e-mail. Interviewers remember candidates who ask about quality.

Salary talk in Slovakia

Slovak job postings must state a minimum basic salary, so you are never guessing blindly. Postings for junior and regular design engineers in this field state a minimum gross monthly salary of around 1 600 to 1 700 EUR, and about 2 400 EUR for seniors. Salaries are always discussed as gross monthly amounts. Net, what arrives in your account, is roughly three quarters of gross for a typical junior salary after social insurance, health insurance and income tax. A basic, mandatory meal contribution comes on top.

When asked "What are your salary expectations?", give a range, anchor it on the posting, and mention the total package. For example: "Based on your posting and my research, I would expect a gross salary in the range of 1 700 to 1 900 EUR per month for a junior role, and I am interested in the total package, including training and the review after probation." A range of about 200 EUR is credible. Do not give a single number, do not say "whatever you offer", and do not negotiate hard before the offer. If HR asks for your current salary and you have none, say so and return to the range.

Tip

Ask HR when and how salaries are reviewed: after the probation period, yearly, or by project. For a junior, the slope of the first two years matters more than the starting number. A company that trains you on a qualified platform is also paying you in skills.

Notice period, start, probation, home office, trips

  • Notice period. In Slovakia it is at least one month for an employee, two months after more than one year of service. A fresh graduate can usually start quickly; if you are employed, say your notice period precisely and the earliest realistic date.
  • Start date. Be concrete: "I can start on the first of the month after signing", not "as soon as possible".
  • Probation period. Three months is the standard maximum for a regular employee; either side can end the contract during it with short notice. Treat it as your first project: deliver visibly.
  • Home office. Postings in this field mention occasional home office. Safety I&C work often needs the office network, documents under access control and team reviews, so expect to be mostly on site.
  • Business trips. Design engineers travel to plants for data collection, FAT witnessing, SAT and commissioning support. Trips can be days or weeks; travel costs and a daily allowance are paid. Say clearly whether you are available for trips, including abroad.
  • Typical benefits. Meal contribution, extra vacation days above the legal minimum of four weeks, flexible working hours, language courses, a contribution to the third-pillar pension, a sports card, sick days and a yearly bonus. Ask, but do not make benefits the centre of the talk.

The thank-you e-mail

Send it within 24 hours to the HR contact, and to the engineers if you have their addresses. Keep it under 120 words, mention one concrete thing from the conversation and confirm your interest.

Subject: Thank you - Design Engineer Safety I&C interview, [date]

Dear [Name],

Thank you for the interview today. I enjoyed the discussion about
[one concrete topic, e.g. how maintenance bypasses are handled in the
2oo4 voting], and it confirmed my interest in the position.

If you need anything else from me, for example references or a
writing sample, I will send it immediately.

I look forward to hearing from you about the next step.

Kind regards,
[Full name]
[Phone]

Red flags and green flags

Red flagGreen flag
Nobody can say which project you would joinA named project, phase and first task
No onboarding or training plan for juniorsPlatform training and a mentor in the first months
Reviews described as a formality or a nuisanceReviews described as normal and useful; findings discussed openly
Pressure to accept an offer within a dayA written offer with a reasonable time to decide
Vague answers on overtime and travelClear statements on hours, trips and allowances
Interviewers who interrupt and never ask "why"Interviewers who probe your reasoning and listen

Common mistake

Treating a long, careful hiring process as a red flag. In nuclear, slow and thorough is the culture, in hiring as in engineering. A three-week gap between rounds is normal; silence after a promised date is not, and a polite follow-up e-mail is appropriate then.

Day-before and day-of checklists

  • Day before: confirm time, place or link and names of interviewers; print two CVs; prepare your three questions on a card; re-read the posting and your own CV; check the route or test the video tool; prepare clothes (smart casual is standard for engineering in Slovakia); run through your 90-second introduction once; sleep.
  • Day of: eat; arrive or join ten minutes early; phone on silent; pen, notebook, CVs, water; switch to English in your head on the way (read an article aloud); in the room: greet everyone, sit upright, breathe, claim before reason; at the end: ask your questions, confirm the next step and the timeline, thank them by name.

Key terms

Gross salary
The monthly amount before income tax and insurance contributions; Slovak postings and negotiations always use gross figures.
Net salary
What arrives in your bank account after tax and contributions, roughly three quarters of gross for a junior.
Total package
Salary plus bonus, meal contribution, pension contribution, extra vacation, training and other benefits.
Notice period
The time between resigning and leaving; at least one month in Slovakia, two after more than one year.
Probation period
The first months of a contract, at most three for a regular employee, when either side can end it with short notice.
Meal contribution
A mandatory employer contribution to lunch, paid as vouchers, a card or money.
Third-pillar pension
A voluntary supplementary pension scheme to which many employers contribute as a benefit.
Daily allowance
A fixed amount paid per day of business travel to cover meals, in addition to travel and hotel costs.
Red flag
A sign during the process that the job or the company may be a problem, such as vague answers on overtime.

Say it in English

  • "Based on your posting, I would expect a gross salary in the range of seventeen hundred to nineteen hundred euros per month."
  • "I am interested in the total package, including training and the salary review after the probation period."
  • "My notice period is one month, so I could start on the first of the month after signing."
  • "I am available for business trips to the plants, including abroad."
  • "Which project would I most likely join first, and in which phase is it?"
  • "Thank you for your time today; could you tell me what the next step is and when I can expect to hear from you?"

Check yourself

How are salaries normally stated in Slovak job postings and negotiations?

Slovak postings must state a minimum basic salary and it is always a gross monthly figure; net is roughly three quarters of it for a junior.

What is the standard maximum probation period for a regular employee in Slovakia?

Three months is the standard maximum for regular employees; it is longer only for managerial positions.

Which is the recommended way to answer "What are your salary expectations?"

A credible range tied to the posting shows preparation and leaves room for the offer without undervaluing yourself.

Which question is best asked to the engineers rather than to HR?

Process and quality questions belong to the technical round; benefits and reviews belong to HR or the manager.

Which of these is a red flag rather than normal nuclear-industry practice?

Careful, slow processes are normal in nuclear; pressure to decide immediately is not.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

What are your salary expectations?

Based on your posting and on what I have seen for junior design engineers in Bratislava, I would expect a gross salary in the range of seventeen hundred to nineteen hundred euros per month. I am a graduate without nuclear experience, so I understand I start at the junior level, and I am more interested in the total package: the platform training, the mentoring and how the salary is reviewed after the probation period. If you can tell me how your salary bands and reviews work, I am happy to be flexible within that range.

When could you start?

I am currently finishing my studies, so I have no notice period, and I could start on the first of the month after we sign the contract. If you need someone earlier, I can discuss a start within two weeks. I would only ask for a few days' notice to arrange the practical things, since I may need to move closer to Bratislava. If I had an employer, I would tell you my exact notice period, which in Slovakia is normally one month, and give you the earliest realistic date rather than a hopeful one.

Do you have any questions for us?

Yes, three. First, which project would I most likely join, and in which phase is it, so I understand whether my first months are specification, verification or documentation? Second, how does onboarding work for a junior: is there formal training on the platform and a mentor for the first six months? Third, for the engineers: what is the most common type of finding in your design reviews? I ask because I would like to know what to be careful about from the first document I write. And at the end I would like to ask about the next step and the timeline.

How do you feel about business trips to the plant, including abroad?

Positively. I understand that a design engineer sometimes needs to be at the plant: to collect data on the existing installation, to witness the factory or site acceptance tests, or to support commissioning. I think seeing the real cabinets and the real control room is the fastest way to understand what the documents mean. I am available for trips in Slovakia and abroad, and I have no constraints that would prevent a trip of a few weeks. I would only ask that trips are planned with reasonable notice, which I expect is normal practice.

Are you talking to other companies as well?

I am in contact with two other engineering companies, but this position is my first choice, because it is the only one that is specifically about safety I&C on a qualified platform. I am being open about it because I would rather you knew my situation than guessed it. I am not in a hurry to accept an offer elsewhere, and I can give you a clear answer on timing: if you can tell me roughly when you expect to decide, I can plan around that. Whatever happens, I will tell you promptly if my situation changes.

Day 4 · Hour 8

Full mock interview, self-scoring and final review

A 45-minute mock interview in realistic order, a rubric to score yourself honestly, a plan for the last two hours before the real thing, and the twenty ideas of this course in one sentence each. If you can run this hour cleanly, you are ready.

By the end of this hour you can

  • Run a 45-minute mock interview, alone or with a friend, in the order a real panel uses
  • Score your performance on five criteria and identify the one thing to fix
  • Execute a calm plan for the last two hours before the interview
  • Explain the twenty core ideas of the course in one sentence each

How to run the mock interview

Ask a friend to read the twelve questions below in order, or record them on your phone with 90-second gaps and play them back. Set a visible timer for 45 minutes. Wear what you will wear. No notes, no pausing the recording, answer aloud in English, and when you get stuck use the four-step method: say what you know, reason aloud, ask a clarifying question, name the limit. Afterwards, listen to the recording and score yourself with the rubric. Do this twice: once today, once as the first thing tomorrow if you have time. The second run is usually two to four points better, which is a real gain for one hour of work.

MinutesBlockQuestionsWhat the panel is doing
0 to 3Introduction1Forming a first impression of clarity and confidence
3 to 8Background2Checking the CV is real and finding something to probe
8 to 30Technical3 to 8Testing fundamentals, structure and honesty; asking "why" after each answer
30 to 38Behavioural9 and 10Judging teamwork and motivation for a long project
38 to 43Case11Watching how you think through an open problem
43 to 45Closing12Seeing whether you prepared questions and how you end

Interview angle

A real panel rarely follows the script exactly. They jump to a follow-up when an answer interests them and skip questions when time runs out. The order above is still realistic: easy start, technical core, behaviour, one open case, your questions. Train the transitions, especially from a technical answer to a personal story.

Self-scoring rubric

Score each criterion from 1 to 5 using the descriptions. Be honest; a generous score helps nobody. A total of 18 or more out of 25 means you are ready. Any score below 3 on honesty is a stop sign: fix that first, because it is the one thing a nuclear employer will not forgive.

Criterion135
Technical correctnessSeveral wrong claims or confused basicsClaims correct, few numbers, limits missingCorrect claims with a number, an example and a limit for each
StructureRambling, no clear first sentenceClaim first, but reason and example mixed upClaim, reason, example, limit every time; STAR for stories
English clarityLong pauses, listener would ask to repeat oftenUnderstandable with some wrong stress or false friendsShort sentences, correct key terms, signposted, easy to follow
HonestyBluffed at least onceAdmitted gaps but gave no reasoning afterwardsNamed limits clearly and reasoned from principles
EnthusiasmFlat, generic reasons, no questionsInterested, but reasons could fit any companySpecific reasons, informed questions, visible energy

Tip

After scoring, pick exactly one weakness to fix, not five. If structure is the problem, write "claim first" on your card. If English clarity is the problem, slow down and use the signpost phrases from Hour 6. Fixing one thing between two runs is realistic; fixing everything is not.

The last two hours

Evening before: in the first hour, review only your red and yellow flashcards from Hours 2 and 3, aloud, and stop when they are green or when the hour ends. In the second hour, re-read the Day 2 review and the Day 3 review from this course, then put every document and tool away. Do not learn anything new; a new fact learned at midnight replaces a settled one. Prepare clothes, two printed CVs, your question card and your numbers list. Sleep seven to eight hours. Morning of: say your 90-second introduction aloud once, read your twenty ideas below, eat, leave early. In the waiting room, breathe slowly and read the question card, nothing else.

Common mistake

Cramming standards numbers the night before. Nobody will ask you to recite IEC clause numbers. They will ask why four channels, what a trip is, and what you do when you do not know. Those answers are already in you after four days; sleep protects them better than another hour of reading.

Course review: twenty ideas in one sentence each

  1. Fission heats the fuel, and decay heat continues after shutdown, so cooling must never stop.
  2. A PWR or VVER has primary, secondary and tertiary circuits, and three barriers: cladding, pressure boundary, containment.
  3. I&C connects the process to operators and automatic actions; safety I&C protects the barriers, operational I&C runs the plant.
  4. A 4 to 20 mA signal with live zero lets the logic tell a dead loop from a low value.
  5. Every measurement chain has failure modes, and the dangerous ones are inside the valid range, so redundant channels are compared.
  6. Protection logic is built from comparators with hysteresis, timers, latches and voters, and the output de-energises to trip.
  7. 2oo4 voting tolerates one failure in either direction even while one channel is in maintenance bypass.
  8. The three fundamental safety functions are reactivity control, heat removal and confinement.
  9. Defence in depth places several independent levels between a fault and a release.
  10. IEC 61226 categories A, B and C, with the matching system classes, decide how much redundancy, qualification and testing a function needs.
  11. Redundancy meets the single failure criterion, diversity meets common cause failure, and both need independence to be real.
  12. Determinism, fixed cyclic processing without interrupts, is what makes a digital safety platform's response time provable.
  13. TELEPERM XS is a qualified platform with cyclic deterministic processing, self-monitoring, and function diagrams engineered in SPACE.
  14. The RPS chain runs from sensor through acquisition, setpoint comparison and voting to the trip breakers; ESFAS actuates safety features the same way.
  15. Bypasses and periodic tests are designed so protection is never silently lost, and the test interval drives the PFD.
  16. The life cycle is a V-model under IEC 61513: requirements go down, tests come up, and each level is verified against the previous one.
  17. A good requirement is one sentence with "shall", a value with a unit, a condition, an identifier and a verification method.
  18. Verification builds the product right, validation builds the right product; FAT proves it at the factory, SAT at the site.
  19. Configuration and change management mean every change is analysed, approved, re-verified and recorded.
  20. Cybersecurity follows IEC 62645 with security zones and one-way data diodes out of the protection system.

Key terms

Mock interview
A full rehearsal under realistic conditions: timed, aloud, in English, with no notes and a debrief afterwards.
Rubric
A scoring table with criteria and descriptions for each score level, used to make self-assessment consistent.
Debrief
The structured review after a mock or a real interview: what went well, what to fix, one action.
Case question
An open problem with no single right answer, used to watch how you structure thinking and ask questions.
Follow-up
The interviewer's next question that digs into your last answer, usually "why" or "give an example".
Closing
The last minutes of the interview: your questions, the next step, the timeline and a thank-you by name.
Impact analysis
The study of everything a proposed change affects: safety, documents, hardware, software, tests and interfaces.
Course review
A short list of the core ideas you must be able to explain simply, used as the final check before the interview.

Say it in English

  • "Before I go into the design, let me clarify the need: which event is this trip for, and which category is the function?"
  • "I would start with an impact analysis: sensors, I/O capacity, processing load, voting, interfaces, testing and documents."
  • "Let me give you the short answer first and then the reasoning."
  • "That is the limit of what I know; I would check IEC 61513 and ask a senior colleague."
  • "Thank you both for your time; could you tell me what the next step is and when I might hear from you?"

Check yourself

In the mock interview timing plan, which block takes the most time?

About 22 of the 45 minutes go to the six technical questions and their follow-ups, matching a real technical interview.

Which rubric result is described as a "stop sign" that must be fixed first?

Bluffing is the one behaviour a nuclear employer will not forgive; everything else can be improved gradually.

What should you do in the last two hours before the interview?

Consolidation and rest protect what you know; new material at the last minute displaces settled knowledge.

In the case question about adding a new trip signal, what is the first step?

Understanding the requirement and its category comes before any design or implementation step; everything else depends on it.

Which pair correctly matches principle and the failure it addresses?

Identical redundant channels cover random failures; only different means cover a cause common to all channels.

Flashcards

Likely interview questions

Good morning. Let us start simply: tell me about yourself.

Good morning, and thank you for the invitation. I am an electrical engineering graduate with a focus on automation and measurement. My thesis was a PLC-based control and monitoring system for a laboratory heat exchanger: I specified the sensors, designed the logic as function block diagrams, wrote the test procedure and documented everything under revision control. I also had an internship in an electrical design office, where I learned to read and check wiring diagrams. I am applying for safety I&C because I like systems where every signal has to be justified and every function tested, and I want to build deep expertise on one platform over years. I am available from next month.

Tell me about your thesis. What was the hardest technical problem in it?

The hardest problem was a noisy temperature signal that made the controller oscillate. My first instinct was a long averaging filter, but it delayed the response so much that the outlet temperature overshot. I recorded the signal, looked at the noise frequency, and used a shorter filter combined with hysteresis on the alarm thresholds and a slower derivative term in the controller. I tested each change separately against the recorded data before putting it on the real system. The oscillation disappeared and the response time stayed within the target. What I took from it is to measure before changing anything, and to change one thing at a time.

What is a reactor protection system, and how does a trip travel from the sensor to the rods?

The reactor protection system is the safety system that automatically shuts the reactor down when a monitored parameter shows that a barrier is threatened. The chain starts with sensors measuring pressure, flux, flow or temperature, and transmitters converting them to 4 to 20 mA. Redundant acquisition units check the signal is valid and compare it with a setpoint, giving a partial trip per channel. A voter, typically two out of four, combines the channels. The result de-energises the trip breakers, which cut power to the rod drive mechanisms, and the rods fall by gravity. Each step is redundant, independent and tested periodically.

We use two out of four voting. Why four channels and not three, and what happens during a test?

With three channels and two out of three voting, the system tolerates one failure in either direction, but only while all three are in service. When one channel is taken out for testing, you are left with two, and a single further failure either blocks the trip or causes a spurious one. With four channels, one can be bypassed for testing and the remaining three still vote two out of three, so the single failure criterion is met even during maintenance. During the test the bypassed channel is forced to the no-trip state, the bypass is indicated in the control room, and only one channel per function may be bypassed at a time.

Why is a 4 to 20 mA signal used instead of 0 to 10 V?

Three reasons. First, a current signal is not affected by the resistance of a long cable, while a voltage signal drops along the way. Second, current loops are more resistant to electrical noise, which matters in a plant full of motors and switchgear. Third, the live zero: zero percent is 4 mA, so if the input module sees 0 mA it knows the wire is broken or the transmitter is dead, rather than reading a false low value. Values below about 3.6 mA or above about 21 mA are declared faults. The same two wires can also power the transmitter. The limit is that it does not detect drift inside the range.

What is a common cause failure and how do we defend against it?

A common cause failure is when several redundant channels fail at the same time from one shared cause: a software fault, a design error, a wrong calibration procedure, or an environmental event such as a fire. Redundancy does not help, because the identical channels share the weakness. The defences are diversity, meaning different parameters, different technologies or a different platform for a backup; independence, so one cause cannot reach all channels; a rigorous development process with independent verification to prevent design faults; and defensive platform features such as self-monitoring and deterministic processing. IEC 62340 is the standard for this, and the analysis is documented in a CCF assessment.

What makes a safety platform like TELEPERM XS different from an industrial PLC?

Technically they look similar: inputs, cyclic logic, outputs. The difference is in what has been proven and how. A safety platform is qualified for category A functions: its hardware is tested for seismic, temperature and EMC conditions, and its software was developed and verified under nuclear standards such as IEC 60880. Its processing is strictly cyclic and deterministic, with no interrupts, so the response time can be proven. It monitors itself continuously and fails safe. Communication is decoupled so a network problem cannot disturb the logic. And the application is engineered as function diagrams in a qualified tool, with generated code, which keeps the implementation traceable to the specification.

How would you write a requirement for a trip function so that a tester can test it?

I would write one sentence with "shall", a measurable value, a condition and a defined measurement point, and I would give it an identifier. For example: RPS-F-012, the protection system shall initiate the reactor trip command within 300 ms after the primary pressure in at least two of four channels falls below 13.0 MPa, measured from the transmitter terminals to the breaker coil. Then I would add the verification method, in this case a test on the test bay with injected signals and a timing recorder, and link it in the traceability matrix to the test case. If I did not know the number, I would put a placeholder in brackets and raise an open point.

Tell me about a time you found an error that others had missed.

During my internship I was asked to check cable numbers on a set of wiring diagrams, a routine task. While doing it, I noticed that a thermocouple pair had its polarity reversed on two drawings, which would have given a wrong temperature sign. My task was only the cable numbers, so I checked the sensor datasheet first to be sure, and then I showed the senior engineer both drawings and the datasheet together. The drawings were corrected before manufacturing, and polarity was added to the review checklist. What I learned is to look at the whole drawing, not only at the column I was assigned, and to report findings with evidence.

This job means years on one project with a lot of documentation. How do you stay motivated?

By making progress visible in small units. In my thesis I had four months of testing and documentation with no new features to build, and I kept motivation by splitting the work into weekly deliverables and treating every completed test as a result. I also think the documentation in safety I&C is not separate from the engineering; the specification and the test record are the product, because that is what the regulator and the plant will rely on for thirty years. And I like the idea of seeing a system I helped specify actually commissioned at a plant. That is a long cycle, but it is a real result, and few jobs offer it.

Case: a customer wants to add a new trip signal to an existing protection system. Walk me through what you would do.

First I would clarify the need: which initiating event is the trip for, which parameter and setpoint come from the safety analysis, and which category the function has, probably A. Then an impact analysis: new or existing sensors, I/O capacity in each division, what does the extra logic do to processing load and cycle time, how does it join the voting, what are the interfaces to the HMI and plant computer, and how will it be bypassed and tested. Then the documents: system requirements, function diagrams, I/O list, FMEA and reliability analysis, test specifications and the traceability matrix, all through the change process with approval, re-verification, validation on the test bay and regulatory acceptance.

That is all from our side. Do you have questions for us, and is there anything you would like to add?

Yes, two questions. First, which project would I most likely join and in which phase? Second, what is the most common type of finding in your design reviews, so I know what to be careful about from my first document? To add: I have no nuclear experience yet, but I hope you have seen today that I have the fundamentals, that I structure my thinking, and that I say when I do not know something. I would like to build my career in safety I&C here for the long term. Thank you both for your time; could you tell me what the next step is and when I might hear from you?
